**Summary.** This paper develops a theory for describing open quantum systems when viewed from different Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs). It introduces 'reduced QRF channels' to account for inaccessible degrees of freedom. The key finding is that decoherence rates can be mathematically split into contributions arising separately from the environment and the reference frame itself.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how environmental coupling and measurement context (the reference frame) fundamentally alter the perceived dynamics of quantum systems, which has implications for quantum sensing and gravity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates which dynamical properties of open quantum systems remain invariant when viewed from a different Quantum Reference Frame (QRF). It addresses the fundamental question of subsystem relativity in quantum dynamics.

**Main result.** It derives reduced quantum-reference-frame channels and shows that decoherence rates can split into distinct environmental and reference-induced contributions, which is operationally testable via Ramsey interferometry.

**Method.** The analysis employs a formal framework involving QRF transformations, partial tracing over inaccessible degrees of freedom (environment/reference frame), and characterizing the resulting reduced quantum channels.

**Model / system.** The study models open quantum systems interacting with an environment, extending to include reference frames. Specific applications include pure-dephasing dynamics and a gravity-motivated dephasing model.

**Key observables.** Reduced entropy conservation law, decoherence rates (split into environmental and reference-induced parts), population preservation conditions, and Ramsey visibility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the QRF symmetry representation; the nature of the coupling between system, environment, and reference frame.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the perspectival approach to QRFs and assumes that only operationally accessible degrees of freedom are retained after tracing out inaccessible ones.

**Figures summary.** Figure 5 illustrates a Ramsey interferometer protocol implemented on an effective qubit subspace, showing how outcome probabilities and decoherence rates can be calculated in different reference frames.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework for QRF transformations in open systems. It then characterizes the reduced channels, derives conservation laws, applies these concepts to pure dephasing dynamics (showing rate splitting), and concludes with an illustration using a gravity-motivated model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

When reference frames are treated quantum mechanically, the subsystem structure of quantum systems is no longer absolute, but depends on the choice of the quantum reference frame (QRF). This raises a basic question: which dynamical properties are preserved across QRFs, and which depend on the physical reference used to define the system? We study this question in the general setting of open quantum systems. At the operational level, after a QRF transformation, the old reference frame and environmental degrees of freedom may be inaccessible and must therefore be traced out. This motivates the definition of reduced quantum-reference-frame channels: maps that connect the joint description in one frame to the accessible subsystem in another. We characterize their symmetry-constrained structure and define a regime in which a reduced entropy-coherence conservation law holds. We also identify when the induced reduced action on the open system admits a classical interpretation as random frame misalignment, and when it instead reflects quantum reduced-frame effects. We then apply the framework to pure-dephasing dynamics and derive a necessary and sufficient compatibility condition for population preservation. When the frame symmetry commutes with the open system's free Hamiltonian, coherences acquire a multiplicative frame factor, so that locally inferred decoherence rates split into environmental and reference-induced contributions. Ramsey interferometry gives this split a direct operational meaning. Finally, a gravity-motivated dephasing model illustrates how degradation of a phase reference can mimic signatures usually attributed to intrinsic decoherence mechanisms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to use an in-plane magnetic field to distinguish between Wigner crystallization and Anderson localization as the cause of a 2D metal-insulator transition. By analyzing how the critical density changes with the applied field, the authors show that the dependence reveals whether disorder or electron correlation dominates the insulating state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a clear diagnostic tool using external fields ($B$) to probe the fundamental nature (disorder vs. correlation) of an electronic phase transition, which is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated and disordered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to distinguish between two competing mechanisms—Wigner crystallization (WC) or Anderson localization—responsible for the density-tuned Metal-Insulator Transition (MIT) in 2D electron systems.

**Main result.** Applying a parallel in-plane magnetic field ($B_{||}$) allows differentiation: if the critical density changes with $B$, it suggests disorder-driven localization; if it remains unchanged, it favors WC pinning.

**Method.** The authors develop general theory using Random Phase Approximation (RPA) and Boltzmann transport equations to calculate spin-polarized conductivity ($\sigma$) under an in-plane field.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D doped semiconductor layer with parabolic dispersion, subjected to an in-plane magnetic field that lifts the spin degeneracy via Zeeman coupling. The analysis compares theoretical predictions for WC and Anderson localization criteria.

**Key observables.** Critical density ($n_{MIT}$), resistivity ($ho$) as a function of $B$ and carrier density ($n$), and the effective degeneracy ($g_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** In-plane magnetic field strength ($B_{||}$), disorder type (short-range vs. long-range Coulomb impurities), and dimensionless Wigner-Seitz radius ($r_S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the in-plane field only couples via Zeeman coupling, neglecting orbital effects; approximations like strong screening ($\Pi_0$) are used for calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate resistivity vs. $n$ at various fields and disorder strengths, comparing calculated $ho(B)$ curves against the Ioffe-Regel-Mott (IRM) localization criterion boundary.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the theoretical framework for spin polarization effects on screening to deriving transport equations that yield a field dependence of resistivity ($ho(B)$), which is then used diagnostically to distinguish between WC and Anderson MIT mechanisms based on how $n_{MIT}$ responds to $B$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A parallel in-plane magnetic field could, in principle, distinguish between two competing physical scenarios for the experimentally observed density-tuned 2D metal-insulator transition (where decreasing the carrier density leads to a crossover from an effective metal to an effective insulator): Wigner crystallization or Anderson localization. Since the main scattering mechanism in 2D doped semiconductors arises from screened random charged impurities and screening in turn depends on the electronic density of states, the in-plane magnetic field could distinguish between the two by decreasing screening through spin polarization and this enhances the effective critical density for Anderson localization compared with Wigner crystallization. We give the general theory and provide results for the quantitative magnitudes of the spin polarization effect on the transition density by focusing on two recent experiments [Z. Ge, et al, arXiv:2510.12009, T. Han, et al, arXiv:2604.00113], noting that the critical density may actually decrease if the dominant scattering is by short-ranged defects instead of long-ranged charged impurities. The difference between the two cases arises from whether spin polarization dominates screening (enhanced critical density) or the Fermi surface (suppressed critical density).

</details>

### [Conditional Residence Times and Sequential Transition Dynamics of an Overdamped Dimere](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06110v1)

**Authors:** Dhruv Agrawal, W. L. Reenbohn  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.06110v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work analyzes how two coupled particles (a dimer) transition across a barrier under external periodic driving and thermal noise. By introducing the Conditional Residence Time (CRT), the authors show that the time delay between the two monomers crossing the barrier is highly sensitive to the drive frequency. This establishes CRT as a key measure for understanding sequential, rate-limiting steps in coupled stochastic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical stochastic dynamics, the concept of analyzing sequential transitions (initiation vs. completion) using a specialized time delay metric like CRT is highly relevant to understanding decoherence or multi-step quantum tunneling processes in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the completion dynamics of an overdamped dimer moving in a bistable potential under thermal fluctuations and a weak periodic force.

**Main result.** The mean Conditional Residence Time (CRT) exhibits a non-monotonic dependence on the external drive frequency, demonstrating that transition initiation and completion are distinct sequential processes.

**Method.** The authors use the Conditional Residence Time (CRT) as a diagnostic tool to quantify the delay between successive barrier crossings of two coupled monomers.

**Model / system.** The system is an overdamped dimer modeled by coupled Langevin equations in a one-dimensional double well potential, incorporating harmonic and Lennard-Jones interactions, thermal noise, and a weak sinusoidal external force.

**Key observables.** Conditional Residence Time (CRT), Mean First Passage Times (MFPTs), Escape rates, Probability distributions of transition windows.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($k$), external drive frequency ($\Omega$), Noise strength ($D$), and the competition between escape times and the driving period.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the process can be partitioned into distinct time windows (immediate, first cycle, later cycles) to analyze sequential dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate CRT distributions for varying coupling strengths and drive frequencies, showing how the mean CRT changes non-monotonically with $\Omega$, especially in the weak coupling regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical model and Langevin equations, defines the CRT diagnostic tool, analyzes the dynamics by partitioning the process into three time windows, and concludes by establishing CRT's utility for sequential barrier crossing analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the completion dynamics of an overdamped dimer moving in a bistable potential under thermal fluctuations and a weak periodic force. Both monomers start in one of the two wells separated by a barrier. The transition is initiated when the monomer closer to the barrier makes a jump across it. The completion dynamics refers to the next part of the dynamics where the second monomer has to wait for some time before it can follow up. We use the Conditional Residence Time (CRT) to study the delay between the successive barrier crossing of the two monomers. The CRT distributions highlight qualitatively different regimes formed by the competition between the escape times of the lagging monomer and the time period of the external drive. The effect is strongest in the weak coupling regime where the delayed completion is spread across multiple forcing cycles. By partitioning this process into three windows, i.e. the immediate, first cycle and later cycles, we show that the probability that the lagging monomer will make a transition in the said cycle is redistributed among these pathways as we change the frequency of the drive. This leads to a non-monotonic dependence of the mean CRT on the frequency of the drive. Our results demonstrate that transition initiation and completion in a coupled system are two separate processes and establish CRT as a useful measure to quantify the sequential barrier crossing dynamics in coupled stochastic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces doubly chiral active particles (dcABPs) that exhibit a unique form of topologically protected transport along boundaries. By rigorously contrasting this behavior with simpler models, the authors establish a new physical mechanism for directed motion in active matter. The work bridges theoretical derivation, simulation, and experimental realization using vibrobots.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical active matter, the concept of 'topological protection' in non-equilibrium systems is highly relevant to understanding robust edge states in quantum materials or engineered quantum circuits where dissipation and noise are present.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the conditions under which robust, topologically protected boundary-induced transport can occur in active particles, contrasting this with edge currents observed in simpler chiral models.

**Main result.** Doubly chiral active Brownian Particles (dcABPs) exhibit robustly protected directed motion along boundaries, unlike simple cABPs or chiral rods which either lack protection or backscatter at corners.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical analysis of steady-state probability currents with detailed numerical simulations and the design/testing of a physical vibrobot realization.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the doubly chiral active Brownian particle (dcABP), characterized by two sources of chirality: an intrinsic angular velocity and a translation-rotation coupling arising from asymmetric friction. The analysis extends to simple cABPs and chiral rods in confined geometries (channels, arenas).

**Key observables.** Edge currents ($J_y(x)$ or $J_\phi(r)$), integrated net transport ($\Phi$), and the persistence of directed motion around corners.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strengths of intrinsic rotation versus translation-rotation coupling, and the presence/absence of boundary curvature are critical parameters determining topological protection.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analyses often assume overdamped dynamics for cABPs; later work rigorously derives transport using stochastic averaging techniques applicable to various noise regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics comparing current distributions and cumulative displacement for different particle types (cABP vs. dcABP) in confined geometries, demonstrating the failure of simple models to achieve true boundary-induced transport.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes that simple cABPs do not exhibit true boundary-induced transport ($\Phi$ arises from bulk orbits). It then contrasts this with chiral rods showing backscattering. Finally, it introduces and proves the topological protection mechanism for dcABPs using theory (Appendix A) and simulation/experimentation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using theory, simulation, and experiment, we introduce a new class of active particle which we term doubly chiral active Brownian particles (dcABPs), which show robust topologically protected transport along boundaries without backscattering at corners. Their double chirality stems from the coexistence of an intrinsic angular velocity, which can cause rotation independently of translation, and a translation-rotation coupling inducing cross-alignment to the instantaneous velocity, which causes rotation only concomitantly with translation. A mechanically detailed model shows that the latter effect can arise from an asymmetric friction distribution in the direction perpendicular to the self-propulsion direction. We show that topologically protected modes emerge when the two sources of chirality have opposite sign and the intrinsic rotation is weaker than the translation-rotation coupling. In the deterministic limit, we characterize the emergence of these modes not only along straight boundaries, but also along curved boundaries and during interparticle interactions. We provide a proof-of-principle experimental realization by building a doubly chiral vibrobot. While setting the work into context, we moreover show that the topologically protected boundary-induced transport of dcABPs stands in contrast to the edge currents observed for simple chiral ABPs, which we demonstrate are not associated with boundary-induced transport, as well as to those observed for chiral active rods or self-aligning chiral ABPs, which we show to be associated with boundary-induced transport but to backscatter at corners, implying lack of topological protection.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses free probability theory to analyze the transmission eigenvalues in monitored quantum transport. It proves that under specific limiting conditions (the small-loss limit), the eigenvalue distribution converges to a measure $\mu_	au$ governed by an exponential S-transform. This reveals underlying algebraic structures, such as the semigroup property of the limiting process.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between random matrix theory (Haar unitaries) and free probability tools provides a powerful framework for analyzing transport phenomena in mesoscopic systems, which is relevant to open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the limiting distribution and associated moments for transmission eigenvalues in a monitored quantum transport system undergoing repeated free multiplicative convolutions.

**Main result.** The empirical eigenvalue distribution converges to $\mu_	au$, characterized by the S-transform $S_{\mu_	au}(z) = \exp\left(rac{	au}{1+z}ight)$, which exhibits a semigroup property ($\mu_{	au_1} oxtimes \mu_{	au_2} = \mu_{	au_1+	au_2}$).

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on Free Probability Theory, utilizing S-transforms and applying Lagrange inversion to derive explicit formulas for the moments and cumulants of the limiting measure.

**Model / system.** The model is monitored quantum transport described by a chain of Kraus operators $B_L = (PS_L)\cdots(PS_1)$, where $S_i$ are independent Haar unitaries and $P$ is a projection. The eigenvalues of $B^\dagger_L B$ represent the transmission eigenvalues.

**Key observables.** Transmission eigenvalues, empirical eigenvalue distribution ($	ext{ESD}$), charge-transfer cumulants ($\kappa_r(	au)$), and Fano factor ($F(	au)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of steps $L$, monitoring fraction $c$, and the small-loss limit parameter $	au$. Key regimes include fixed-$L$ and diagonal scaling ($L \sim 	au N, c=1/N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The convergence of empirical spectral distributions to the limiting measure $\mu_	au$ is assumed or proven in specific limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the fixed-$L$ limit using free multiplicative convolution, then analyzes the small-loss limit ($	ext{Theorem 2}$) to find the limiting measure $\mu_	au$. Finally, it investigates the diagonal scaling regime by calculating higher-order cumulants and confirming moment convergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the transmission eigenvalues of monitored Haar products \[   B_L=(PS_L)(PS_{L-1})\cdots(PS_1), \] where the $S_i$ are independent Haar unitaries and $P$ is a deterministic projection. For fixed $L$, we prove that the empirical eigenvalue distribution of $B_L^\dagger B_L$ converges to $ν_c^{\boxtimes L}$, where $ν_c=(1-c)δ_1+cδ_0$. We then take the free small-loss limit and identify the limiting law by \[   S_{μ_τ}(z)=\exp\left(\fracτ{1+z}\right). \] Lagrange inversion gives explicit Erlang-type moments, explaining the polynomials appearing in Beenakker's recursion. We also record spectral consequences, including the atom $μ_τ(\{1\})=(1-τ)_+$ and the real branch point $τ\mathrm{e}^{1-τ}$, and formulate the diagonal scaling $L\simτN$, $c=1/N$, as a quantitative convergence problem supported by low-order moment checks.

</details>

### [Modern view of activated rate processes: unidirectional fluxes at equilibrium, correlation functions, and splitting probabilities](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05672v1)

**Authors:** Alexander M. Berezhkovskii, Kevin Song, Dmitrii E. Makarov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.05672v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This perspective paper rigorously re-derives the theory of activated rate processes, showing how continuous Brownian motion maps onto discrete chemical kinetics. It resolves ambiguities in defining reaction rates by introducing a concept of unidirectional flux based on trajectory history rather than simple spatial partitioning. This provides a unified and robust theoretical tool for studying barrier crossing phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools for calculating rates in complex systems where dynamics are governed by free energy landscapes, which is foundational knowledge for understanding relaxation processes in many quantum or mesoscopic physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a rigorous theoretical framework for mapping continuous, diffusive dynamics (like Brownian motion) onto discrete-state chemical kinetics describing activated barrier crossing.

**Main result.** It establishes that the rate constant can be calculated robustly by considering unidirectional fluxes between sub-ensembles defined by trajectory history, yielding results consistent with established theories like Kramers' theory and correlation function methods.

**Method.** The authors use concepts from non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, analyzing fluxes in phase space and comparing different mathematical formulations (e.g., conditional probabilities vs. relaxation modes) to ensure physical consistency.

**Model / system.** The model is a particle undergoing Brownian motion between two potential wells ('reactant' and 'product') separated by an activation barrier $U(x)$. The dynamics are governed by the Langevin or Smoluchowski equation.

**Key observables.** Reaction rate coefficients ($k_{A	o B}$), equilibrium populations, and the unidirectional reactive flux ($J_{A 	o B}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The barrier height relative to thermal energy ($U(0) - U(a) \gg k_BT$) is a crucial parameter for simplifying assumptions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Naive state separation based only on position fails; the analysis requires considering the full trajectory history and assuming high barriers allow flux independence from boundary placement.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates two methods of mapping continuous dynamics to discrete states (position cut vs. trajectory history). Figure 2 conceptually shows the cycle of ensemble members leading to a net unidirectional flux.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by reviewing Kramers' theory, identifying the mathematical shortcomings of simple state separation, and then rigorously constructing an internally consistent mapping using fluxes derived from both conditional probabilities and time-evolution operator analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

More than 80 years ago Kramers published a paper calculating how fast a Brownian particle escapes from a potential well over an activation barrier. Since then Kramers' model has been widely adopted by nuclear physics, biophysics and chemical physics communities as a description of activated barrier crossing. From a chemical kinetics perspective, Kramers' theory provides a mapping from continuous dynamics to discrete-state chemical kinetics. Motivated by recent developments, this Perspective provides a rigorous way of performing such a mapping, explaining why and how Kramers' theory works from several points of view. Specifically, we consider transitions of a Brownian particle between two potential wells corresponding to the ``reactant'' and the ``product'' of a chemical reaction. A central unifying idea is to divide the equilibrium ensemble of possible states of the system into two sub-ensembles corresponding to the reactant and product states and then to consider fluxes between these sub-ensembles. Importantly, naive separation based on the location measured relative to the barrier top does not result in a mapping that is physically tenable, and instead the past of the trajectory should be considered. Thus constructed reactant and product ensembles provide an internally consistent description of the problem when also viewed from two different perspectives: one based on the definition of the rate as a conditional transition probability per unit time and the other based on the relaxation modes of the time-evolution operator governing the dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reveals an unexpected suppression of quantum chaos ($\langle r angle$) in the interacting SSH model deep within the non-integrable regime. This effect is fundamentally linked to incomplete hybridization between many-body band states rather than simple spectral overlap or localization. The findings challenge conventional assumptions linking non-integrability directly to fully chaotic level statistics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed mechanism for spectral statistics deviations that are purely structural (band geometry) rather than arising from standard sources like disorder or integrability breaking, which is highly relevant for understanding quantum dynamics in engineered lattices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates an anomalous suppression of quantum chaos, evidenced by reduced level repulsion ($\langle r angle$), in non-integrable many-body systems.

**Main result.** This anomaly is attributed not to integrability or disorder, but specifically to incomplete hybridization between distinct many-body band states inherited from the underlying single-particle structure.

**Method.** The authors use exact diagonalization and semi-analytical perturbation theory to calculate level statistics ($\langle r angle$) across varying interaction strengths and hopping parameters.

**Model / system.** The system is the interacting Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain model, described by a Hamiltonian combining non-interacting SSH terms with nearest-neighbor density-density interactions. The analysis probes transitions between integrable limits.

**Key observables.** Mean level-spacing ratio ($\langle r angle$), Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), and the fluctuation measure $\Delta Q$ quantifying hybridization strength.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Alternating hopping parameter ($\delta$), interaction strength ($V_{	ext{int}}$), and symmetry breaking terms like staggered potential ($h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on resolving symmetries to benchmark against GOE statistics, and the mechanism is linked to the geometry of many-body minibands.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $\langle r angle$ dipping anomalously in non-integrable regimes compared to GOE expectations; other figures map out the closing of MB band gaps and the correlation between minimal $\langle r angle$ and maximal hybridization fluctuations ($\Delta Q$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system by first establishing the expected chaotic behavior (GOE), then identifying the anomalous dip in $\langle r angle$, attributing it to incomplete hybridization, and finally using advanced techniques like perturbation theory and symmetry analysis to quantify this geometric origin.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Level statistics in non-integrable quantum many-body systems with time reversal symmetry are expected to follow the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble (GOE), a hallmark of quantum chaos. However, we show that the interacting Su-Schrieffer-Heeger model exhibits a clear suppression of the mean level-spacing ratio $\langle r\rangle$ from the GOE value $\approx 0.535$, persisting deep in the nonintegrable regime. This challenges the conventional association between non-integrability and fully chaotic spectral statistics. Using exact diagonalization supported by semi-analytical arguments, we trace this anomaly to incomplete hybridization of many-body band states inherited from the noninteracting band structure. The resulting restructuring of the spectrum weakens level repulsion without restoring integrability. We show the robustness of this mechanism in extensions of the model which break chiral and inversion symmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models how quarkonium bound states lose their quantum coherence when passing through the hot, expanding medium created in heavy-ion collisions. Using an open quantum system framework and the Lindblad equation, the authors show that while decoherence occurs rapidly at high initial temperatures, the specific hydrodynamic expansion profile is crucial for accurately predicting this loss of quantum information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges fundamental concepts from open quantum systems theory with high-energy nuclear physics phenomenology, providing a quantitative tool to study non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in extreme matter states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates quantum decoherence of quarkonium-like bound states as they propagate through a hot, strongly interacting medium like the Quark-Gluon Plasma (QGP). The goal is to quantify how environmental interactions cause the loss of quantum coherence.

**Main result.** The rate and manner of decoherence are significantly influenced by the temperature evolution of the expanding medium; however, viscous effects were found not to substantially alter the decoherence process compared to ideal hydrodynamics.

**Method.** The analysis employs an open quantum systems approach, modeling the system's time evolution using the Lindblad master equation for the reduced density matrix. This is extended to incorporate time-dependent coupling strengths simulating hydrodynamic expansion.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a 1D damped harmonic oscillator (representing quarkonium) coupled to an environment reservoir representing the QGP degrees of freedom. The evolution is governed by the Lindblad equation derived under Born-Markov approximations.

**Key observables.** Loss of quantum coherence, quantified by analyzing the reduced density matrix elements ($	ext{Im}(ho_{n0})$), and the Wigner function profile $W(x, p, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial temperature ($T_0$), time scale ($	au \sim 1-5$ fm), coupling strength ($\kappa$), and the ratio of shear viscosity to entropy density ($\eta/s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions include using the Born-Markov approximation, assuming weak system-reservoir coupling, and modeling the environment dynamics via ideal or viscous hydrodynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of coherence elements (e.g., $	ext{Im}(ho_{n0})$) comparing static vs. expanding baths, and contour plots show the Wigner function evolving from quantum fringes toward a classical Gaussian profile in an expanding QGP medium.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the open quantum system framework using the Lindblad equation for quarkonium decoherence. It then extends this model to include time-dependent coupling strengths mimicking QGP expansion, comparing results across different hydrodynamic models (ideal vs. viscous) and analyzing the resulting loss of coherence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the quantum decoherence of a bound state interacting with a reservoir of strongly interacting matter within the framework of open quantum systems. The bound state is modeled as a quantum harmonic oscillator whose parameters are tuned to reproduce the root-mean-square radius of $J/Ψ$ particle. The surrounding medium, representing the many degrees of freedom of strongly interacting matter, acts as an environment that induces dissipation and decoherence through system-reservoir coupling. By analyzing the time evolution of the reduced density matrix, we quantify the loss of quantum coherence and its dependence on medium properties. Subsequently, we extend the model by introducing a time dependence in the system-thermal bath coupling, thereby simulating a temperature evolution similar to that occurring during the expansion of a fireball in the central region of heavy-ion collisions. We find that a temperature evolution has a relevant impact on the way the system loses coherence through the coupling with the expanding medium. Finally, we estimate the impact of the time-dependent temperature on the decoherence process, also analyzing a scenario that includes viscous effects without finding a significant change with respect to ideal hydrodynamical evolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes Quantum Probabilistic Local Differential Privacy (QPrLDP) as a relaxed framework for quantifying privacy loss in quantum data analysis. It analyzes the structural properties of this notion and applies it to derive concrete sample complexity bounds for private quantum hypothesis testing. This provides essential theoretical tools for ensuring privacy guarantees when performing statistical inference using quantum resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on privacy theory, the rigorous mathematical tools developed—especially those involving divergences and channel analysis—are highly relevant to characterizing information leakage or distinguishability in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper introduces and rigorously studies Quantum Probabilistic Local Differential Privacy (QPrLDP), a relaxation of quantum local differential privacy.

**Main result.** It derives sample complexity bounds for probabilistically privatized asymmetric and symmetric quantum hypothesis testing, providing a systematic foundation for probabilistic privacy guarantees in quantum inference.

**Method.** The study involves defining QPrLDP based on spectral violation probabilities and connecting it to statistical inference via the hockey-stick divergence. Structural properties are analyzed using tensor products and unitary post-processing.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted within the framework of quantum information processing, involving quantum states ($ho, \sigma$), superoperators (channels), and measurements characterized by POVMs. The context includes depolarizing noise models.

**Key observables.** Trace distance $T(ho||\sigma)$, Quantum hockey-stick divergence $	ext{E}_\gamma(ho||\sigma)$, Sample complexity measures ($	ext{SC}, 	ext{ASC}$), and error probabilities ($\alpha, eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Privacy parameters $(\epsilon, \delta)$ and the noise parameter $p$ (for depolarizing noise).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work builds upon existing concepts of classical and quantum differential privacy, QLDP, and various quantum divergences.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the relationship between $\delta$ and $\epsilon$ for global depolarizing noise, showing how $\delta$ scales with the number of compositions ($n$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically defines QPrLDP, investigates its structural properties (composition, post-processing), characterizes specific noise models (depolarizing noise), and culminates in deriving explicit lower and upper bounds for sample complexity in quantum hypothesis testing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Differential privacy provides a rigorous framework for quantifying privacy leakage in data analysis, while its quantum extensions have become increasingly relevant with the development of quantum computing and quantum machine learning. In this work, we introduce and study quantum probabilistic local differential privacy, a relaxation of quantum local differential privacy in which the privacy constraint is allowed to fail on a spectral violation event with low probability. This quantity can be interpreted as the probability under the quantum superoperation of a quantum privacy-loss violation, and is closely related to the acceptance probability of the quantum Neyman-Pearson test at a small threshold. We investigate the basic structural properties of this privacy notion and clarify its relationship with existing forms of quantum differential privacy. We show the properties of quantum probabilistic local differential privacy under tensor-product composition and unitary post-processing, while it is in general neither convex nor closed under post-processing by arbitrary quantum channels. We further characterize when depolarizing noise satisfies quantum probabilistic local differential privacy under several representative scenarios. Finally, we connect quantum probabilistic privacy constraints with statistical inference by deriving a lower bound on probabilistically privatized contraction coefficients in terms of the hockey-stick divergence. As an application, we obtain sample complexity bounds of probabilistically privated asymmetric and symmetric quantum hypothesis testing. These results provide a systematic foundation for studying probabilistic privacy guarantees in quantum information processing and their operational consequences for private quantum statistical inference.

</details>

**Summary.** The research explores controlling magnetic skyrmions in centrosymmetric lattices by manipulating dipole-dipole interactions and external electric fields. Key findings include the ability of electric fields to continuously tune the skyrmion helicity, providing a mechanism for tuning qubits. This work advances the understanding of topological spin control crucial for next-generation quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental control mechanisms (electric fields) over topological spin textures, which is highly relevant to developing robust quantum memory elements based on magnetic materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing precise control over the helicity and spatial configuration of magnetic skyrmions for use in advanced computational applications.

**Main result.** The study shows that electric fields can continuously tune the skyrmion helicity, offering a novel control handle for tuning two-level systems within skyrmion qubits. Furthermore, dipole-dipole interactions stabilize Bloch helicity and induce new lattice phases even at zero magnetic field.

**Method.** Theoretical modeling using classical spin vector models solved via Monte Carlo simulations (Metropolis-Hastings algorithm) to map out phase diagrams.

**Model / system.** The system is a skyrmion host on a triangular lattice, modeled by an extended Heisenberg Hamiltonian incorporating nearest-neighbor, next-nearest-neighbor, and third-nearest-neighbor exchange interactions. The model explicitly includes magnetic dipole-dipole interactions and external electric fields.

**Key observables.** Topological charge (Q), Root Mean Square of the topological charge ($	ext{QRMS}$), and the balance between skyrmion/antiskyrmion populations ($ar{Q}/	ext{QRMS}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dipole-dipole coupling strength ($C$), external electric field strength, magnetic field ($B_z$), and lattice exchange couplings ($J_2, J_3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is based on a classical spin vector model ($\mathbf{S}$), and phase boundaries are qualitatively defined due to strong thermal fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of magnetic phases (Ferromagnetic, Skyrmion Crystal, Helical) across parameter space ($J_2$ vs $J_3$) and show real-space spin textures corresponding to different helicity types (Bloch vs. Néel).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates the influence of dipole-dipole interactions on skyrmion stability, demonstrating phase transitions at zero field. It then analyzes how applied electric fields mediate a continuous transition between Bloch and Néel skyrmion states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Establishing precise control over the helicity and spatial configuration of magnetic skyrmions will be essential to realize their promise in classical, analog and quantum computation applications. In this work, we explore the role of magnetic dipole-dipole interactions, external electric fields, and magnetic fields in controlling these parameters within a triangular lattice centrosymmetric skyrmion host. We demonstrate that dipole-dipole interactions strongly favor Bloch helicity. Notably, a zero magnetic field skyrmion phase appears upon raising the dipole-dipole coupling strength, with substantial potential for cost-effective quantum device applications. We also report the emergence of a meron/antimeron lattice phase, in the absence of any Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction. In contrast, applied electric fields stabilize high density Néel skyrmion crystals. The interplay between dipole-dipole interactions and external electric fields creates a continuous transition between the two skyrmion types, rather than an abrupt switch. Applied electric fields can therefore be used as a continuous tuning mechanism for skyrmion helicity, and hence a control handle for tuning two-level systems in skyrmion qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the Hilbert-Schmidt speed (HSS) as a diagnostic tool for non-Markovianity in open quantum systems. It proves that HSS decay is guaranteed only when the system's evolution channels are unital. Crucially, it demonstrates via counterexample that if the dynamics are not unital, HSS can increase even when the process remains perfectly Markovian.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a precise mathematical criterion—unitality—for using geometric measures like HSS backflow as reliable witnesses of memory effects in open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the contractivity of the Hilbert-Schmidt speed (HSS) under open quantum system dynamics to establish a rigorous diagnostic tool for non-Markovianity.

**Main result.** HSS is guaranteed to be monotonically non-increasing only if the underlying CPTP maps are unital; otherwise, HSS can increase even in perfectly Markovian dynamics.

**Method.** The authors use geometric analysis on the space of quantum states, deriving differential equations for the time evolution of the squared HSS at both the channel and generator levels.

**Model / system.** The study concerns open quantum systems evolving via CPTP maps or GKSL master equations. The dynamics are analyzed by parameterizing the initial state ($ho_\varphi$) while keeping the map itself independent of $\varphi$.

**Key observables.** Hilbert-Schmidt speed (HSS), $H(ho_\varphi) = rac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \|\partial_\varphi ho_\varphi\|_{HS}$, and its time derivative.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Unitality of the CPTP map ($\Lambda(I)=I$) is the crucial structural parameter determining HSS behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the parameter $\varphi$ enters only through the initial state, and for generator-level proofs, it requires Lindblad operators to be Hermitian.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the structure relies on mathematical derivations (e.g., deriving $rac{d}{dt}H^2(t) \le 0$ or constructing a counterexample).

**Paper structure.** The paper first proves HSS contractivity under unital CPTP maps, establishing monotonicity for divisible evolutions. It then extends this to the generator level for Hermitian Lindblad operators. Finally, it provides a qutrit counterexample demonstrating that non-unitality breaks the monotonic decay property.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the Hilbert--Schmidt speed (HSS), a geometric indicator defined through the Hilbert--Schmidt norm of the tangent vector to a parametrized family of quantum states, under general open-system dynamics. Working in the framework of finite-dimensional, parameter-independent completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) evolution where the parameter is encoded solely in the initial state, we prove that the HSS is contractive under every unital CPTP map. Consequently, for any CP-divisible evolution whose intermediate propagators are unital, the HSS is monotonically non-increasing in time. We then establish the generator-level counterpart for Markovian dynamics governed by a Gorini--Kossakowski--Sudarshan--Lindblad (GKSL) master equation with Hermitian Lindblad operators, deriving an explicit non-positive expression for the time derivative of the squared HSS. These results provide a rigorous foundation for using HSS backflow as a sufficient witness of non-Markovianity in physical settings where the relevant CP-divisible Markovian dynamics is known \emph{a priori} to be unital. Conversely, we show by an explicit qutrit counterexample that HSS can increase even in perfectly Markovian but non-unital dynamics, demonstrating that HSS non-monotonicity is not, in general, a faithful indicator of memory effects unless unitality is guaranteed. Our findings clarify the exact scope of HSS-based diagnostics and identify unitality as the crucial structural ingredient underlying their validity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses the Emery model and CDMFT calculations to investigate what controls the maximum superconducting critical temperature in high-Tc cuprates. The key finding is that Tcmax increases with increasing the copper-oxygen energy distance, suggesting a mechanism for optimizing superconductivity by tuning orbital interactions. This provides new theoretical guidelines for understanding cuprate pairing.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cuprates, the use of advanced many-body techniques like CDMFT to map phase diagrams based on orbital energy differences provides valuable theoretical insight into how local electronic structure dictates macroscopic quantum phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is identifying microscopic parameters within the Emery model that optimize the maximum superconducting critical temperature (Tcmax) in cuprate superconductors.

**Main result.** The study finds that Tcmax unexpectedly increases with increasing the copper-oxygen energy distance, suggesting new pathways for optimizing superconductivity beyond previous models.

**Method.** Calculations utilize Cellular Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (CDMFT) at finite temperature to solve the cluster quantum impurity problem using Continuous-Time Quantum Monte Carlo (CT-HYB).

**Model / system.** The research focuses on the Emery model, which describes electron hopping between Cu $3d_{x^2-y^2}$ and O $2p\alpha$ orbitals, relevant for cuprate superconductors. The analysis is performed within the framework of the Zaanen-Sawatzky-Allen charge-transfer diagram.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature (Tcmax), charge gap size ($\Delta_I$), and doping level ($\delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cu-O energy distance ($	ilde{\epsilon}_p - \epsilon_d$), onsite Coulomb repulsion ($U_d$), and fixed charge gap size ($\Delta_I = 0.6$ eV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes a fixed charge gap size ($\Delta_I$) and that the CDMFT result is representative, while acknowledging it neglects Kosterlitz-Thouless physics.

**Figures summary.** Figures show Tc vs doping (dome shape), Tcmax vs bare charge-transfer energy ($	ilde{\epsilon}_p - \epsilon_d$), and Tcmax vs oxygen hole content ($2pp$), all demonstrating characteristic superconducting dome behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the dependence of Tcmax on various parameters—specifically $	ilde{\epsilon}_p - \epsilon_d$ and oxygen hole content—while keeping the charge gap size constant, mapping out optimal regimes in parameter space.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Identifying microscopic parameters that optimize the maximum superconducting critical temperature $T_c^{\rm max}$ in the canonical model of the copper-oxygen plane of cuprates, the Emery model, remains challenging. Using cellular dynamical mean-field theory at finite temperature, we find that for a fixed charge gap size in the parent charge-transfer insulating state, $T_c^{\rm max}$ unexpectedly increases with increasing the copper-oxygen energy distance, as this favors the transfer of electrons from oxygen to copper orbitals. We show that these findings emerge naturally in the Zaanen-Sawatzky-Allen scheme and capture observed trends in hole-doped cuprates. Overall, our study uncovers that $T_c^{\rm max}$ is optimized in the Emery model under three conditions: upon doping a charge-transfer insulator, close to the charge-transfer insulator to metal boundary, and deep into the charge-transfer regime. This finding indicates new paths for optimizing $T_c^{\rm max}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a theoretical study on enhancing the sensitivity of optical gyroscopes by employing two-mode squeezed beams generated via OPA. The authors show that measuring the intensity difference allows for sub-shot-noise measurements, achieving phase sensitivities approaching the quantum Cramér-Rao bound. This demonstrates a robust quantum metrology technique applicable to high-precision sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it pushes measurement precision beyond classical limits using non-classical light states (squeezed beams) in a practical sensing platform like an optical gyroscope.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to theoretically demonstrate a metrology method for improving the sensitivity of an interferometric optical gyroscope beyond the classical shot-noise limit while maintaining robustness against propagation loss.

**Main result.** The proposed scheme achieves sub-shot-noise sensitivity, with the phase uncertainty approaching the quantum Cramér-Rao bound as the photon number increases. The enhancement factor relative to the shot-noise limit is analyzed in detail.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical derivations based on intensity difference measurements ($\hat{M} = \hat{d}^\dagger \hat{d} - \hat{c}^\dagger \hat{c}$) applied to light sources generated via Optical Parametric Amplification (OPA) seeded by coherent inputs.

**Model / system.** The system is an optical gyroscope implemented using a Sagnac interferometer setup. The quantum resource utilized is the two-mode squeezed beam, which is modeled as the output of an OPA process.

**Key observables.** Phase shift ($\varphi = \mu - 
u$), phase uncertainty ($\Delta\varphi^2$), minimum detectable angular velocity ($\Omega_{\min}$), and the Noise Reduction Factor (NRF).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Parametric gain ($r$), initial coherent field amplitudes ($\alpha, eta$), and propagation loss.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linear error propagation for uncertainty calculation and assumes specific approximations regarding the input coherent light fields in different cases (e.g., $\langle \hat{a} angle^2$, $\langle \hat{b} angle^2 \gg \sinh^2 r$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the optical network diagram for the Sagnac interferometer using coherent-boosted two-mode squeezed light. Other figures analyze the dependence of the enhancement factor ($R$) on parameters like gain ($r$) and phase difference ($\delta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by modeling the OPA output, deriving the measurement observable $\hat{M}$, calculating the expected signal $\langle \hat{M} angle_E$, and finally determining the phase uncertainty $\Delta\varphi^2$ relative to the shot-noise limit across various input field configurations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum techniques, developed in recent decades, provide new approaches to achieving high-precision measurements beyond the classical bounds. In this paper, we theoretically demonstrate a metrology method for improving the sensitivity of the interferometric optical gyroscope, robust against the loss, by using coherent-light stimulated two-mode squeezed beams as the light source. The detection protocol is based on a simple intensity measurement, and the quantum noise is far below the shot-noise limit. The enhancement factors for different coherent light fields are analyzed in detail. Additionally, the influence of loss during the propagation in the optical path is studied, and the conditions for achieving sub-shot-noise measurement sensitivity are obtained. We also find that the phase sensitivity of the proposed gyroscope scheme becomes closer to the quantum Cramér-Rao bound with increasing of the photon number of the coherent beams.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates how conduction electrons tunnel through a material with localized antiferromagnetic moments. It distinguishes between spectral features caused by the static magnetic order and those arising dynamically from magnon excitations. By calculating electron self-energies due to magnon scattering, the authors predict that dynamic effects manifest as distinct 'sidebands' in the tunneling spectrum.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical framework for interpreting spectroscopic measurements (like STM) in magnetically ordered materials by separating static magnetic effects from dynamic quantum fluctuations (magnons), which is crucial for understanding strongly correlated magnetism.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether dynamic effects arising from local moment magnon excitations leave a distinct, measurable fingerprint on the conduction electron tunneling spectrum beyond the spectral changes caused by static magnetic ordering.

**Main result.** Dynamic magnon scattering introduces characteristic 'sidebands' into the tunneling conductance spectrum via self-energy corrections, which must be accounted for when interpreting STM measurements of magnetically ordered materials.

**Method.** The analysis employs Green's function techniques to calculate the conduction electron self-energy ($\Sigma$) due to coupling with magnons (derived from an XXZ model). This self-energy is then used to correct the spectral density of states, $ho(\omega)$.

**Model / system.** The system is a dual model comprising itinerant conduction electrons coupled via an on-site exchange interaction ($H_{ex}$) to localized spins exhibiting Antiferromagnetic (AF) order. The Hamiltonian includes terms for conduction electrons ($H_c$), magnons ($H_m$), and their coupling ($H_{cm}$).

**Key observables.** Tunneling surface conductance ($rac{dI}{dV}$), which maps to the conduction electron Density of States (DOS); specifically, the appearance and magnitude of magnon sidebands relative to static reconstruction features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exchange coupling strength ($I_{ex}$ or $	ilde{\gamma}$), anisotropy parameter ($\Delta$), and the magnon wave vector ($\mathbf{q}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on perturbation theory (second order in $I_0$) for the self-energy, assuming slow variation of the tunneling matrix element with energy, and focusing on a simplified model system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Fermi surface reconstruction due to static coupling ($\pm \mathbf{Q}$ gaps), the magnon dispersion relation ($\omega_{\mathbf{q}}$) dependence on anisotropy, and crucially, the spectral function $ho(\omega)$ showing weight shifts from main peaks into distinct sidebands due to dynamic self-energy corrections.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the static picture (band reconstruction at $\pm \mathbf{Q}$), then introduces the full Hamiltonian including magnon dynamics. The core is the calculation of the electron self-energy ($\Sigma$) via magnon scattering, leading to renormalized quasiparticle bands and the prediction of sidebands in the tunneling spectrum.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The surface tunnelling spectrum of a dual system consisting of localised moments with antiferromagnetic order coupled to conduction electrons by on-site exchange interaction is investigated. In the static approximation of the local moment order it is known that magnetic band reconstruction leads to an anomalous tunnelling spectrum at the magnetic ordering vector of local moments although the latter cannot contribute directly. In this work we consider dynamic effects by including the scattering of conduction electrons from the local moment magnon excitations. They lead to self energies and renormalisation of conduction states which in turn appreciably modify the tunnelling spectrum beyond the influence of static order, interpreted as the appearance of magnon sidebands.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a machine learning approach to solve the practical problem of determining the correct 'crop-length' needed for calculating topological invariants in finite non-Hermitian quantum chains. By training Random Forest models on system parameters, the authors successfully predict this convergence criterion, demonstrating that it is governed by the underlying physical decay lengths associated with the non-Hermitian skin effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges advanced condensed matter topology (non-Hermitian physics) with modern data science techniques. For AMO/open systems, understanding how finite boundaries or measurement limitations affect bulk topological properties is crucial, making the ML prediction of $\ell^\star$ highly relevant for experimental interpretation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a practical, physically interpretable method to determine the necessary 'crop-length parameter' ($\ell$) for a real-space topological invariant in finite non-Hermitian chains.

**Main result.** Machine learning models (Random Forest) can accurately predict this required crop-length ($\ell^\star$), showing that it is fundamentally controlled by physical decay lengths associated with the non-Hermitian skin effect.

**Method.** The authors employ Random Forest regression and classification, training ML predictors on system parameters to automate the selection of $\ell$ needed for convergence.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes finite one-dimensional non-Hermitian chains, including Hatano-Nelson type models (nearest-neighbor) and general $m$-hop hopping systems. The topology is diagnosed using a real-space invariant derived from the polar decomposition of the Hamiltonian matrix.

**Key observables.** Topological Invariant ($w_{PD}(\ell)$), clean momentum-space winding number ($w$), required crop-length ($\ell^\star$), and localization/decay lengths ($\xi, \xi_m$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-reciprocity parameters ($J_R, J_L$), system size ($N$), disorder strength ($r_W$), and the ratio $x = N/\xi$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The convergence of the real-space invariant is assumed to be governed by physical decay lengths ($\xi$ or $\xi_m$) derived from the spectral properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-Hermitian skin effect, the comparison between $w_{PD}$ and $w$, and show how ML predictions generalize across phase diagrams and under disorder.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem of finite-size effects in topological invariants. It then develops an ML framework using decay lengths as key features to predict the convergence parameter $\ell^\star$. Finally, it validates this approach by testing generalization and stability under disorder for various hopping models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A topological invariant based on polar-decomposition of matrices correctly captures the topology of finite non-Hermitian chains exhibiting the non-Hermitian skin effect, provided that an appropriate crop-length parameter is chosen. This parameter, which sets the cutoff used in the calculation of the invariant, is usually chosen empirically and becomes especially important near topological phase transitions, where finite-size effects are strongest. Here we show that the required crop-length is controlled by physical decay (localization) lengths. For nearest-neighbor and pure longer-range hopping Hatano-Nelson-type chains, the crop-length is set mainly by a single localization length and is well approximated by a scalar multiple of that length. For more general longer-range hopping models, it is governed instead by a multichannel root structure of the characteristic polynomial. Random-forest regression captures finite-size and near-boundary corrections while preserving this decay-length interpretation. Trained on one set of Hamiltonians, the predictor accurately generalizes to unseen Hamiltonians and complex base energies, reproducing crop-lengths across full phase diagrams. We further show that the predictions learned from clean nearest-neighbor hopping chains remain stable under moderate hopping disorder. These results provide a practical and physically interpretable way to choose the crop-length, which in turn determines when the real-space invariant can reliably capture the topology of finite non-Hermitian chains.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically explores altermagnetism in a checkerboard lattice using the Hubbard model. By analyzing susceptibility divergences and performing RPA calculations, the authors map out phase transitions to novel magnetic states. The key finding is the observation of chirality-dependent magnon excitations that confirm the unique nature of altermagnetic order.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the study of spin excitations (magnons) derived from Hubbard models provides insights into collective quantum dynamics that can be relevant for understanding strongly correlated quantum materials studied in AMO physics or condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates weak-coupling altermagnetism and the associated chiral magnetic excitations within a checkerboard lattice structure.

**Main result.** The research predicts phase transitions from nonmagnetic to altermagnetic semimetal and insulating phases, showing that magnetic excitations exhibit a characteristic alternating chirality splitting mirroring the electronic band splitting.

**Method.** Theoretical calculations employing mean-field treatments of the Hubbard repulsion combined with Random-Phase Approximation (RPA) for analyzing dynamical spin susceptibilities.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a minimal one-band Hubbard model applied to electrons on a checkerboard lattice. The analysis explores how electron interactions drive the material into an altermagnetic state characterized by spin-split bands.

**Key observables.** Altermagnetic susceptibility ($\chi_{AM}$), spin susceptibility ($\chi_S$), electronic band structure ($E_\pm(\mathbf{k})$), and magnon excitation spectra ($	ext{Im}(\chi)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$) relative to hopping parameters ($t_1, t_2, t_3$); filling level (half-filling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is conducted within a weak-coupling framework and relies on the Random-Phase Approximation (RPA) for excitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice structure, static and RPA susceptibilities ($\chi_{AM}$ vs $\mathbf{q}$), phase diagrams showing transitions between different magnetic/electronic phases, spin-resolved band structures, and magnon spectral functions ($	ext{Im}(\chi)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the model on the checkerboard lattice, using mean-field theory to map out phase transitions based on $U$, and finally applying RPA techniques to calculate the magnetic excitation spectra in the resulting altermagnetic phases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets, characterized by spin-split electronic bands with compensated magnetic moments, have emerged as a new class of magnetic materials garnering attention in recent years. Here, using a minimal one-band Hubbard model, we show that the checkerboard lattice serves as a natural platform for altermagnetism for electrons. The instability towards altermagnetic order is denoted by diverging altermagnetic susceptibility at weak-coupling. Carrying out mean-field treatment of the Hubbard repulsion, we show phase transitions from the nonmagnetic to altermagnetic semimetal and then to altermagnetic insulating phase, allowing clear identification of spin-split states. We then examine magnetic excitations in the altermagnetic phases using a random-phase approximation treatment of the dynamical spin susceptibility. The altermagnetic order is found to be stable against spin-fluctuations with the excitation spectra showing well-defined magnon excitations, which decay into single-particle excitations with decreasing interaction strength. Remarkably, the magnetic excitations exhibit strong dependence on both chirality and direction, showing an alternating chirality splitting, similar to the alternating spin splitting of the electronic bands, which serves as a salient feature of altermagnetism.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Quantum Channel Polynomial Processing (QCPP), an algorithm designed to implement functions of Hermitian operators on quantum states. By replacing complex coherent unitaries with stochastic probabilistic mixtures, QCPP significantly simplifies the circuit requirements compared to existing techniques like QSVT. This advancement makes simulating complex Hamiltonians more feasible for near-term and intermediate-scale quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses algorithmic efficiency in quantum simulation, offering a hardware-friendly alternative to complex unitary encodings required by standard QSVT methods for simulating Hamiltonians relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a quantum algorithmic framework to efficiently apply arbitrary functions of Hermitian operators, $f(H)ho f(H)^\dagger$, on an initial quantum state.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum Channel Polynomial Processing (QCPP) method achieves a favorable tradeoff between sample and query complexity, allowing for polynomial sample scaling with respect to the required precision.

**Method.** QCPP replaces coherent block encoding used in QSVT with stochastic encodings of Hamiltonian commutators/anti-commutators at the channel level, utilizing probabilistic mixtures of unitary channels.

**Model / system.** The framework operates on an n-qubit computational register and one ancillary qubit. The target process approximates applying a function $f$ to a Hermitian operator $H$, which is constructed from Pauli operators.

**Key observables.** Sample Complexity ($\Gamma(p)$), Query Complexity ($d$), and the resulting quantum channel $\mathcal{F}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tradeoff between sample complexity (related to polynomial coefficients) and query complexity (number of basic blocks $d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes the ability to implement the probabilistic building block $E$, and it relies on polynomial interpolation techniques for approximating the target function $f$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the workflow: polynomial interpolation, construction of the basic computational building block via stochastic sampling, concatenation of blocks corresponding to roots, and the final measurement sequence.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the problem (applying $f(H)$) to introducing QCPP as a replacement for QSVT's coherent encoding. It details the mathematical structure using probabilistic mixtures and analyzes the resulting complexity trade-offs by constructing specific polynomials that yield polynomial sample scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a quantum algorithmic framework based on probabilistic mixtures of unitary channels that, similar to the framework of quantum singular value transformations, enables the application of arbitrary polynomials of hermitian operators onto arbitrary initial states. We show that our framework supports a flexible tradeoff between sample- and query complexity ranging from optimal query complexity, meaning logarithmic in the error, and exponentially scaling sample complexity to sub-polynomial query complexity in the error and polynomial sample complexity. Combined with the considerably lower quantum circuit complexity, compared to quantum singular value transformations with a linear combination of unitaries block encoding, we argue that our framework can be seamlessly scaled from NISQ to fault-tolerant quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines the interplay between quantum coherence and the precision limits of estimating temperature and wave vector in a graphene system. Using the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix, the authors find that while coherence peaks near $k_x=0$, the best estimation precision requires considering parameter correlations via a ratio $\Gamma$. The results highlight that optimal metrology depends on more than just maximizing quantum coherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum metrology (a key area in quantum information) with condensed matter physics by applying advanced estimation theory to the electronic properties of graphene, showing that resource quantification (coherence) is not sufficient for optimal measurement design.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum coherence influences the precision achievable when estimating multiple physical parameters, specifically temperature (T) and wave vector ($k_x$), in a graphene system.

**Main result.** Optimal estimation precision does not necessarily coincide with regions of maximal quantum coherence; furthermore, the relationship between independent versus simultaneous estimation strategies is quantified by a ratio $\Gamma$.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) and the quantum Cramér–Rao bound to establish lower limits on parameter uncertainty for both joint and separate estimations.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is graphene, treated using an effective Dirac-like Hamiltonian. The thermal state density matrix $ho$ is derived based on this Hamiltonian, which depends on $k_x$ and $T$.

**Key observables.** Quantum coherence ($C_{\ell_1}$), minimum variances ($	ext{Var}_{\min}(T)$, $	ext{Var}_{\min}(k_x)$), and the comparison ratio $\Gamma$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$) and wave vector component ($k_x$). Key regimes include low temperatures and $k_x \approx 0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses the $\ell_1$-norm for coherence, which is basis-dependent. It also notes that while the bound can be calculated, attainability depends on specific commutation relations between derived operators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of coherence and minimum variance on $k_x$ (for fixed $T$) and the ratio $\Gamma$ as a function of both $k_x$ and $T$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the physical model and quantifies coherence. It then develops the theoretical framework using QFIM for multiparameter estimation, calculating bounds for independent and simultaneous schemes. Finally, it analyzes how coherence relates to these minimum variances across different parameter regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we investigate the relationship between quantum coherence and multiparameter quantum estimation in a graphene-based system. We focus on the estimation of two relevant physical parameters, namely the temperature $T$ and the wave vector $k_x$, and analyze how their variations affect both quantum coherence and the achievable metrological precision. The minimum variances associated with the estimation process are evaluated through the quantum Cramér--Rao bound within both simultaneous and independent estimation schemes. Our results show that quantum coherence is enhanced in the low-temperature regime and around $k_x=0$, while it decreases progressively as either the temperature or the wave vector increases. However, the regions where coherence is maximal do not necessarily coincide with those of optimal estimation precision. In particular, the variance associated with temperature estimation exhibits a divergent behavior near $T=0$, indicating that the system becomes weakly sensitive to small temperature variations in this regime. By contrast, the estimation of the wave vector $k_x$ is more directly related to the coherence properties of the system, with improved precision obtained near $k_x=0$. Furthermore, we introduce the ratio $Γ$ to compare the total variances obtained from the independent and simultaneous estimation schemes. This quantity provides a useful measure of the relative difference between the two strategies when the parameters are estimated separately or jointly.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically calculates the three-point density correlations in a weakly interacting 2D Fermi liquid. Using perturbative methods, it derives interaction corrections for both total and same-spin density correlators. The results are highly relevant for interpreting upcoming experimental measurements using quantum gas microscopy on atomic Fermi gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides highly detailed theoretical predictions for correlation functions in ultracold atomic Fermi gases, directly connecting fundamental many-body theory (Fermi liquid parameters) to measurable quantities accessible via quantum gas microscopy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on calculating the three-point equal-time density correlations ($ho^3$) in a weakly interacting two-dimensional Fermi liquid.

**Main result.** The authors derive explicit, perturbative results for both the total and same-spin density three-point correlators, providing corrections beyond the standard long-wavelength collinear limit.

**Method.** The calculations employ a systematic perturbative expansion in the interaction parameter ($I$) using diagrammatic techniques (Feynman diagrams) within the framework of Fermi liquid theory.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a spin 1/2 Fermi gas confined to two dimensions, interacting via an attractive contact potential. The analysis uses Hamiltonian formulations and correlation functions derived from quantum field theory methods.

**Key observables.** Total density three-point correlator ($s_{ho}^3(q_1, q_2)$) and same-spin three-point correlator ($s_{\uparrow}^3(q_1, q_2)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless interaction parameter $I = -1/\log k_F a$, the Fermi momentum $k_F$, and various wave vectors ($\mathbf{q}_i$). The Long-Wavelength Collinear (LWC) limit is a key regime analyzed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on perturbative expansions in weak interactions ($N_0|u| \ll 1$) and assumes the validity of Fermi liquid theory, although results are extended beyond its strict limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Feynman diagrams for correlation functions at different orders, compare exact calculations against asymptotic approximations and standard Fermi liquid predictions across various momentum configurations (e.g., isosceles triangles).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the observables, calculating the zeroth-order non-interacting case, and then systematically deriving the first-order interaction corrections ($L_1$ or $s^{(1)}$) through complex integral evaluations in momentum space.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the three-point equal-time correlations of the density in a weakly interacting spin 1/2 Fermi gas and present two new results. First, we compute the three-point correlation for the total density $ρ= ρ_\uparrow + ρ_\downarrow$ exactly as a function of momentum to first order in a dimensionless interaction parameter ${\cal I}$. This generalizes a previous result that related the three-point function to the Landau Fermi liquid parameters $F_0^s$ and $F_0^a$ and applied in a certain long-wavelength collinear limit. Second, we compute the leading order ${\cal O}({\cal I}^3)$ interaction correction to the same-spin three-point correlation function in the long-wavelength collinear limit. These results are directly relevant to current experiments on atomic Fermi gases using quantum gas microscopy.

</details>
