**Summary.** The paper introduces a method to measure quantum fluctuations in bright light by adapting attosecond streaking spectroscopy. By analyzing the Quantum Optical Streaking Trace, researchers can detect signatures of field squeezing that are invisible to classical measurement techniques. This opens a new avenue for performing quantum optical metrology with attosecond temporal precision.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges advanced concepts in quantum optics (squeezing, non-Gaussian states) with ultrafast time resolution techniques (attosecond metrology), providing a novel diagnostic tool for fundamental quantum physics research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To extend attosecond metrology from classical field measurements to accurately quantify quantum fluctuations present in bright quantum light.

**Main result.** The developed Quantum Optical Streaking Trace (QOST) serves as a certification method capable of measuring quantum squeezing below the shot noise limit, overcoming limitations of conventional state tomography.

**Method.** The authors utilize an attosecond streaking measurement scheme where photoionization by an XUV pulse followed by interaction with a quantized Infrared (IR) field allows for the calculation of the QOST via trace operations over the joint light-matter quantum state.

**Model / system.** The system involves an electron interacting with an intense XUV burst and an arbitrary IR streaking field, which is modeled as a quantized electromagnetic field capable of being in squeezed vacuum states. The evolution is governed by unitary operators derived from the interaction Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Quantum Optical Streaking Trace (QOST), $	ext{SQ}(p, 	au)$, and its sensitivity to quadrature squeezing properties of the IR field.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Attosecond temporal resolution ($	au$), quantum squeezing parameters, and measurement regimes below the shot noise limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism is derived for an arbitrary light field (classical or quantum); approximations are made when comparing to quasi-classical limits, though the full quantum treatment is emphasized.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QOST for bright squeezed vacuum fields, showing distinct sub-cycle modulations revealing field fluctuations and allowing quadrature squeezing measurement; comparisons are drawn between amplitude vs. phase squeezed states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the gap in current metrology (classical limitation), develops the quantum formalism using the QOST, derives mathematical expressions comparing full quantum traces to semi-classical limits, and finally demonstrates how specific field fluctuations manifest as measurable modulations in the trace.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Attosecond metrology is the ability to measure ultrafast optical light-wave oscillations, yet its approach has been limited to classical fields. Hence, the influence of the fluctuations of a quantum field on attosecond measurements has remained unexplored. Here, we close this gap by showing that the attosecond streaking measurement of bright quantum light is sensitive to quantum fluctuations of the optical field on the attosecond timescale. The distinct sub-cycle modulations allow to extract the properties of the squeezed field quadrature in regimes where conventional state tomography approaches reach their limitation. With the full quantum optical attosecond streaking scheme developed here, we provide a certification method that can measure quantum squeezing below the shot noise limit, thereby overcoming the problem of tomographically measuring bright quantum light. This opens the way towards quantum optical metrology of field fluctuations with attosecond temporal resolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports magnetotransport measurements on decoupled twisted double bilayer graphene to investigate fractional quantum Hall states. The key finding is the observation of a controllable transition at half-filling ($
u_{tot}=-1/2$) from a two-component Abelian state to a one-component non-Abelian state by tuning an external displacement field. This demonstrates that TDBG is a tunable platform for studying fundamental correlated electron physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to tune between different topological ground states (Abelian vs. non-Abelian) using an external electric field ($D$) in a solid-state system like graphene makes this highly relevant for realizing and controlling exotic quantum phases of matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to resolve the long-standing debate regarding the origin of the half-filling fractional quantum Hall state ($
u_{tot} = -1/2$) in double quantum Hall systems, specifically distinguishing between two-component (Halperin) and one-component (Pfaffian) origins.

**Main result.** The authors observed that at zero displacement field ($D=0$), the $
u_{tot}=-1/2$ state is consistent with a two-component Halperin-Laughlin state, which undergoes a transition to a one-component non-Abelian state when a finite displacement field is applied.

**Method.** Magnetotransport measurements (longitudinal resistance) were performed as a function of total carrier density and an out-of-plane magnetic field, supplemented by temperature dependence measurements to extract activation gaps.

**Model / system.** The physical platform utilized is Decoupled Twisted Double Bilayer Graphene (TDBG), which serves as a tunable model for double quantum Hall systems due to its controllable interlayer coupling and orbital degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Resistive minima in $
u_{tot}-D$ maps, thermal activation gaps ($\Delta$), and the transition between 2C and 1C state signatures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Total filling factor ($
u_{tot}$), displacement field ($D$), magnetic field strength ($B \approx 10 	ext{ T}$), and temperature range (350 mK to 1 K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed transitions are governed by polarization induced by the displacement field, which breaks interlayer coherence between different correlated states.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $
u_{tot}-D$ maps revealing integer and fractional Hall states; temperature dependence plots confirm distinct activation gaps for various fillings ($
u=-8/3, -7/3, -5/2$); and specific plots illustrate the transition of the half-filling state with applied $D$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents magnetotransport measurements across varying filling factors and displacement fields. It first establishes the observation of various fractional states (Jain sequence) and then focuses on the $
u_{tot}=-1/2$ state, detailing its transition from a 2C to a 1C non-Abelian state as $D$ is tuned.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The origin of the fractional state at $ν$ = 1/2 observed in double-layer quantum Hall systems has been under debate for decades. Because of the variation of bilayer charge distribution and interlayer tunneling strength, the half-filling state can be attributed to a two-component(2C) or a one-component(1C) origin, which corresponds to Halperin state and Pffafian state, respectively. Here we report the magnetotransport measurement in decoupled twisted double bilayer graphene(TDBG), which has been proved to be a promising platform for double quantum Hall system. Fractional quantum hall states in both odd and even denominator fillings are observed. We also found that the half-filling state occurs at zero displacement field at $ν_{tot}$ = -1/2, which is theoretically consistent with two-component Halperin-Laughlin (Ψ331) state. Moreover, we report the transition from two-component state at zero D field to one-component non-Abelian state by tunning displacement field. Our observation of the half filling state and its transition from 2C to 1C state provides the tunability of decoupled twisted double bilayer graphene and shed light on the understanding of the ground states at half-filling factor in the double quantum Hall system.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explores how weak magnetic anisotropy can induce p-wave superconductivity in a 2D itinerant ferromagnet. The authors show that while perfect symmetry suppresses pairing, the small anisotropy generates an attractive interaction mediated by magnons. This mechanism predicts a high $T_c$ peaked near the onset of ferromagnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism/superconductivity, the detailed diagrammatic approach to pairing interactions in a correlated electron system is highly relevant to understanding unconventional superconductivity mechanisms beyond simple BCS theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the mechanism of superconductivity mediated by magnons in a two-dimensional itinerant ferromagnet, specifically addressing how weak magnetic anisotropy enables pairing.

**Main result.** A small easy-plane magnetic anisotropy breaks SU(2) symmetry, generating an attractive p-wave pairing interaction whose strength is enhanced near the ferromagnetic transition, leading to a sizable superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$).

**Method.** The analysis uses diagrammatic techniques (ladder approximation) applied to electron Green's functions and spin susceptibility to derive the effective electron-magnon coupling constant.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-valley model of itinerant 2D electrons with Hubbard-type interactions, modeled as an isotropic ferromagnet that undergoes a first-order Stoner transition. The key perturbation is a weak easy-plane magnetic anisotropy ($\Omega_0$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), pairing coupling constant ($\lambda_p$), and the spin-flip susceptibility $\Pi^{\uparrow\downarrow}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of anisotropy to Fermi energy ($\Omega_0/E_F$) and the proximity to the ferromagnetic transition ($c-1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as restricting calculations to the ladder approximation, neglecting higher-order vertex corrections in certain limits, and assuming a single-band model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the effective electron-magnon interaction ($\Upsilon\uparrow\downarrow$) derived via ladder diagrams and detail the structure of the vertex correction $A$, showing diagrammatic cancellations due to Pauli exclusion principles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the model in the isotropic limit, calculating the magnon pole from susceptibility divergence, introducing the anisotropy to break symmetry, deriving the effective interaction vertex corrections, and finally solving the linearized gap equation for $T_c$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by recent observations of superconductivity in a quarter-metal state of spin- and valley- polarized graphene multilayers, we investigate pairing within a ferromagnetic phase of a single-valley model of itinerant two-dimensional (2D) electrons with Hubbard-type interaction and no artificial high-energy cutoff. In 2D, the Stoner transition is first-order into a fully-polarized state wherein the only gapless collective excitations are transverse magnons. We find that in a spin-SU(2) symmetric model, this magnon-mediated pairing interaction between equal-spin fermions vanishes at $T=0$. We show that a small easy-plane magnetic anisotropy $Ω_0 \ll E_F$, where $E_F$ is the Fermi energy, breaks the SU(2) symmetry and generates an attractive interaction for equal-spin $p-$wave pairing. We explicitly derive the corresponding coupling constant $λ_p$ as the scaling function of both the relative strength of the easy-plane anisotropy, $Ω_0/E_F$, and the proximity to the ferromagnetic transition. While $λ_p$ is parametrically small in $Ω_0/E_F$ deep inside the ferromagnetic phase, it becomes enhanced near the ferromagnetic transition, reaching order unity regardless of how small $Ω_0/E_F$ is. This mechanism yields a sizable $T_c$, peaked near the onset of ferromagnetism.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends the teleportation-based Quantum State Tomography protocol from qubits to qutrits (spin-1 systems). It demonstrates that by performing multiple quantum teleportation experiments using nine distinct input states, one can fully reconstruct the density matrix of a two-qutrit system. The method is shown to scale systematically to $n$-qutrits, providing a powerful theoretical tool for characterizing high-dimensional quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it develops a concrete, scalable protocol for characterizing multi-partite quantum states in higher-dimensional systems (qutrits), which are crucial testbeds for understanding entanglement beyond simple qubit models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is extending the quantum state tomography protocol, originally designed for qubits (spin-1/2), to reconstruct arbitrary states of qutrits (spin-1 systems). This extends to higher dimensions ($n$-qutrits) as well.

**Main result.** The protocol successfully determines the density matrix $\rho_{12}$ (for two qutrits) by performing multiple quantum teleportation experiments using nine distinct known input states. The method scales to $n$-qutrits, requiring $9^{n-1}$ such experiments.

**Method.** The reconstruction relies on measuring the resulting single-qutrit state ($ho_2$) at Bob's end after Alice performs a Bell measurement on her two qubits (the unknown system and an auxiliary qubit). A consistent set of linear equations is solved using these measurements to determine the density matrix elements.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves qutrits, which are spin-1 systems ($	ext{dim}=3$). The process models quantum state tomography on a two-qutrit system $\rho_{12}$ by leveraging the principles of quantum teleportation.

**Key observables.** The matrix elements ($m_{ij}$) of the unknown density matrix $\rho_{12}$, and the probabilities ($Q_j$) associated with various Bell measurement outcomes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of required input states scales exponentially, $9^{n-1}$, for an $n$-qutrit system. The reconstruction requires at least nine distinct teleportation measurements for two qutrits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that Bob can experimentally reconstruct his single-qutrit density matrix ($ho_2$) using standard QST methods, and the protocol assumes specific measurement outcomes (e.g., measuring $|B_1\rangle$ initially).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the general teleportation-based QST protocol for two qutrits. Figure 3 extends this concept to three qutrits, showing Alice preparing and projecting pairs of states onto Bell bases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the extension from qubits to qutrits, detailing the required measurements (nine input states). It then provides explicit formulas relating the unknown density matrix elements ($\rho_{12}$) to Bob's measured single-qutrit states. Finally, it discusses the general scaling of this method to $n$-qutrits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the teleportation-based quantum state tomography (QST) protocol, originally built to reconstruct qubits (spin-1/2 systems), can be extended to deal with qutrits (spin-1 systems) as well. Similarly to the original proposal, only two resources are needed to implement the spin-1 teleportation-based QST protocol: (1) Alice should be able to implement the analog of Bell measurements for spin-1 systems; and (2) she should be able to prepare a few different single qutrit states that will be teleported to Bob.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the design and rigorous characterization of a high-precision laser beam collimator specifically for use in cold-atom Gravimeter systems. By employing ISO 11146 compliant measurements, the authors demonstrated achieving microradian level collimation accuracy. This robust methodology provides a crucial tool for minimizing systematic errors in long-baseline quantum sensing experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it addresses the critical experimental bottleneck—laser beam stability and characterization—for advanced quantum sensing platforms like atom interferometers, which are central to modern AMO physics research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is maintaining highly collimated laser beams with excellent spatial quality, as residual beam divergence introduces systematic errors that degrade the precision of quantum sensing experiments like cold-atom gravimeters.

**Main result.** The developed high-precision collimator successfully achieved microradian level collimation accuracy, demonstrating stable and well-controlled beam propagation essential for advanced atom interferometry.

**Method.** Characterization involved ISO 11146 compliant beam propagation measurements combined with Gaussian beam analysis to determine key parameters like divergence angle and the beam quality factor ($M^2$).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a custom, high-precision laser beam collimator designed for cold-atom Gravimeter systems. It incorporates a five-degree-of-freedom adjustment mechanism and utilizes optical components to control beam divergence over long distances.

**Key observables.** Measured divergence angles ($0.006^\circ$ x, $0.007^\circ$ y), Beam Waist Asymmetry (BWA = 1.33), and the beam quality factor ($M^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Target output diameter of 16 mm; microradian level collimation accuracy; $\lambda = 780.241 	ext{ nm}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on Gaussian beam theory and the ISO 11146 standard for beam propagation measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the collimator, its mechanical model, intensity profiles (transverse and normalized), and detailed setups for both coarse and fine alignment procedures.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the necessity of high-quality beams, presents the design of the 5-DOF collimator, outlines the ISO 11146 measurement methodology using beam profilers, reports quantitative measurements of divergence and $M^2$, and concludes by validating its use in a cold-atom Gravimeter setup.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Highly collimated laser beam with excellent spatial quality is essential for quantum sensing experiments, where even small residual beam divergence can accumulate over distance and introduce significant systematic errors. In this article, we present the design and detailed characterization of a high precision laser beam collimator developed for a cold-atom Gravimeter system, capable of producing an expanded laser beam with a diameter of 16 mm while achieving microradian level collimation accuracy through a five-degree-of-freedom (5-DOF) adjustment mechanism. The beam quality is evaluated using an ISO11146 compliant beam propagation measurement combined with Gaussian beam analysis to extract key parameters, including the beam waist, divergence angle, Rayleigh length, and beam quality factor $M^{2}$. The measured divergence angles of 0.006° (105 micro-radian) along the $x$ axis and 0.007° (122 micro-radian) along the $y$ axis confirm stable and well controlled collimation over long propagation distances. The demonstrated collimation architecture and characterization methodology provide a robust and scalable solution for cold-atom Gravimetry and other precision optical applications that require stable, high quality laser beams maintained over extended distances.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the electronic structure of LaSr2Mn2O7 in its CE-antiferromagnetic state by combining $\mu$-ARPES experiments with tight-binding theory. The authors successfully demonstrate that the observed band dispersions are best explained by a model incorporating the CE-AFM magnetic ordering, distinguishing it from simpler A-AFM structures. This provides crucial experimental evidence for understanding charge and orbital ordering in these complex manganites.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed comparison between different magnetic ordering states (CE-AFM vs. A-AFM) using spectroscopic techniques provides deep insight into how electronic structure is intrinsically linked to complex magnetic ground states in strongly correlated oxides.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to experimentally and theoretically reveal the electronic structure of LaSr2Mn2O7 when it is in its CE-type antiferromagnetic (CE-AFM) state.

**Main result.** The $\mu$-ARPES measurements revealed a dispersive intensity around the X point, which was successfully explained by the CE-AFM tight-binding model for the first time. This confirms the electronic signature of the CE-AFM phase in this material.

**Method.** The research combines experimental micro-focused angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy ($\mu$-ARPES) with theoretical analysis using detailed tight-binding models derived from LDA+U calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is LaSr2Mn2O7, focusing on the CE-AFM state characterized by charge and orbital ordering. Theoretical modeling involves single-layered (2D) tight-binding Hamiltonians incorporating both FM and AF hopping integrals to capture the complex magnetic structure.

**Key observables.** Electronic band dispersion relations measured via $\mu$-ARPES intensity plots, particularly at high-symmetry points like X and M in the folded Brillouin Zone ($\Gamma_{	ext{CE}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis compares CE-AFM vs. A-AFM states; key energy scales include hopping integrals (e.g., $	ilde{t}_{dd\sigma} = -0.652 	ext{ eV}$) and the magnetic ordering type.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that the observed features are governed by the CE-AFM band structure, although some near-Fermi level intensity mapping might suggest A-AFM contributions; the domain size is estimated to be $\lesssim 20 \mu	ext{m}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare ARPES data (intensity and second derivative) along specific high-symmetry directions ($\Gamma-	ext{V}/	ext{UCE}-\Gamma	ext{CE}$), showing that the CE-AFM model successfully reproduces band features at both $\Gamma_{	ext{CE}}$ points, unlike the A-AFM structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the scientific problem by comparing different magnetic states (CE-AFM vs. A-AFM). It details the theoretical framework using tight-binding models and presents experimental $\mu$-ARPES results that are then used to validate the CE-AFM model over the A-AFM model, concluding with a discussion of domain size.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We have investigated the electronic structure of LaSr$_2$Mn$_2$O$_7$ in the CE-type antiferromagnetic (CE-AFM) state below the Néel temperature using micro-focused angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy ($μ$-ARPES) and tight-binding models. In agreement with the tight-binding calculations, we found a dispersive intensity around the X point in the $μ$-ARPES spectra, where the A-type antiferromagnetic (A-AFM) phase has no bands experimentally and theoretically, demonstrating that we have successfully captured the signatures of the CE-AFM band structure for the first time. Many observed features can be explained by the CE-AFM tight-binding bands, although some of them and the overall near-Fermi level intensity mapping can be explained by the A-AFM band structure. This indicates that the CE-AFM domain size would be no larger than the beam footprint size of a $\sim\!20$~{\textmu}m scale.

</details>

**Authors:** Jian Xu, Delu Zeng, John Paisley, Qibin Zhao  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.06230v1>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Fisher effective dimension as the true measure of complexity governing generalization in quantum circuits used for machine learning tasks. By showing that entanglement inflates this dimension independently of parameter count, it establishes a clear entanglement-generalization trade-off. This reframes quantum policy design away from mere expressivity towards structural complexity control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a theoretical framework connecting geometric properties (Fisher information/entanglement) directly to generalization bounds in quantum machine learning models. This is crucial for designing efficient and provably generalizable quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining a rigorous measure of generalization complexity for quantum policies ($\pi_	heta$) and value functions used in Quantum Reinforcement Learning (QRL). The authors aim to move beyond simple parameter counting to understand the role of entanglement.

**Main result.** Generalization capability is governed by the effective dimension of the Fisher geometry, which quantifies structural complexity. Entanglement acts as an independent axis of complexity, causing circuits with high entanglement but fixed parameters to generalize worse than non-entangled counterparts.

**Method.** The authors derive and apply a PAC-Bayesian generalization bound based on the Fisher Information Matrix ($\hat{F}$). They analyze how varying entanglement connectivity inflates the effective dimension ($	ext{deff} = \log \det(I + \gamma \hat{F})$) while keeping parameter count constant.

**Model / system.** The model utilizes Parameterized Quantum Circuits (PQCs) trained for tasks like supervised classification and value estimation. The circuits are systematically varied by controlling the entangling connectivity pattern (e.g., none, linear chain, full coupling) across a fixed number of qubits and parameters.

**Key observables.** Generalization gap (test vs. train performance), Fisher effective dimension ($	ext{deff}$), entanglement connectivity, and correlation coefficients ($ho$) between $	ext{deff}$ and the gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The generalization constant $\gamma$ used in the bound; fixed number of trainable rotations/parameters ($d$); sample size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is primarily focused on controlled decision models (single-observable readout) to isolate the entanglement effect. The results are claimed to be a 'ranking certificate' rather than a numerically tight bound.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that increasing entangling connectivity monotonically increases the rank of the Fisher matrix and thus $	ext{deff}$, which correlates strongly with an increased generalization gap, even when parameter count is held fixed.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for a better complexity measure than raw parameters. It derives the PAC-Bayesian bound using the Fisher geometry, proves that entanglement inflates this effective dimension by enlarging the light-cone of the readout observable, and validates this mechanism across multiple quantum learning tasks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) are increasingly used as policies and value functions in quantum reinforcement learning, yet it remains unclear when and why quantum policies generalize. We give a PAC-Bayesian account in which generalization is governed not by the raw number of circuit parameters, but by the effective dimension of the Fisher geometry induced by the circuit. This quantity is inflated by entanglement, making entangling connectivity an independent axis of complexity.In controlled experiments that fix the number of trainable rotations and vary only entanglement, we find that circuits with larger Fisher effective dimension exhibit larger train-test gaps, while parameter count is a weak predictor. The resulting bound acts primarily as a ranking certificate: it correctly orders circuits with identical parameter count, which parameter-counting bounds cannot do. We validate this mechanism across supervised classification, quantum contextual bandits, and value-function generalization, where entangled circuits consistently generalize worse than non-entangled circuits of equal parameter count, with gaps shrinking as sample size increases.Our strongest evidence comes from low-variance decision models, including single-observable classifiers, value heads, and one-step policies. In end-to-end multi-step policy learning, entanglement effects remain statistically significant but high return variance leaves the full ordering only partially resolved. Partial-correlation analysis shows that Fisher effective dimension screens off entangling pattern, and controls for training accuracy, readout, and optimizer rule out major optimization confounders. The effect also persists on an IBM Heron quantum processor under real noise. Overall, our results reframe quantum policy design around an entanglement--generalization trade-off rather than expressivity alone.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses DMRG simulations on the critical $J_1$-$J_2$ Ising chain to characterize static vacancies as tunable conformal defects. By analyzing the algebraic decay of vacancy interactions, the transmission ratio, and boundary entropy, the authors demonstrate that these three observables vary smoothly with the coupling ratio $J_2/J_1$. This establishes a quantitative link between microscopic parameters and universal defect properties in quantum critical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work connects concepts from quantum criticality (Ising universality class) to boundary physics via conformal field theory (CFT), providing quantitative evidence for how defect parameters can be continuously tuned by microscopic couplings ($J_2/J_1$).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how static nonmagnetic vacancies act as parametrized conformal defects within the critical $J_1$-$J_2$ transverse-field Ising chain.

**Main result.** The vacancy defect forms a continuous, one-parameter family of partially transmissive conformal defects controlled by the ratio $J_2/J_1$, evidenced by the smooth variation of three key observables ($\alpha_\infty$, $T_\infty$, $\log g_\infty$).

**Method.** Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) calculations were performed on open chains, analyzing power-law decay exponents and boundary entropy.

**Model / system.** The system is the transverse-field Ising chain with first ($J_1$) and second ($J_2$) neighbor couplings, $H = -\sum_{i} J_1 \sigma^x_i \sigma^x_{i+1} - \sum_{i} J_2 \sigma^x_i \sigma^x_{i+2} - \sum_{i} b \sigma^z_i$, with vacancies acting as defects.

**Key observables.** Vacancy-vacancy binding energy decay exponent ($\alpha$), spin correlator transmission ratio ($T_\infty$), and Affleck-Ludwig boundary entropy ($\log g_\infty$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tuning parameter is the ratio $J_2/J_1$, which controls the defect's properties across the quantum critical line.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The vacancy is treated as a bulk defect preserving connectivity, and the critical line location is robustly fixed by matching the bulk spin-correlator exponent $\eta=1/4$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the power-law decay of binding energy $|\Delta b(r)| \sim r^{-\alpha}$ (Fig. 2a), plot the dependence of the fitted exponent $\alpha_\infty$ and transmission $T_\infty$ on $J_2/J_1$, and show the extraction method for boundary entropy ($\log g$) (Fig. 6).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model, applies DMRG to calculate three key observables ($\Delta b(r)$, $T(k)$, $\log g$), analyzes how these observables vary with $J_2/J_1$ along the critical line, and concludes that this variation confirms a continuous family of conformal defects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the problem of two static nonmagnetic vacancies in the transverse-field Ising chain with first- and second-neighbor couplings $J_1$ and $J_2$, now on the critical line, using density-matrix renormalization-group (DMRG) calculations in open chains of up to $N=300$ sites. In contrast to the gapped regime studied previously, where the vacancy-vacancy interaction decays exponentially, along the entire quantum critical line the interaction becomes algebraic, $|Δ_b(r)|\sim r^{-α}$, with $α$ close to the universal Casimir value of unity and a weak but systematic dependence on the second-neighbor coupling, $α_\infty \simeq 1.070 + 0.091\, J_2/J_1)$ across $J_2/J_1\in[0.1,1.0]$. The transmission ratio of the spin correlator across a vacancy approaches a $J_2$-dependent plateau $T_\infty(J_2)$ that grows from $0.11$ to $0.33$ over the same range, and the Affleck-Ludwig boundary entropy is small and approximately constant, $\log g_\infty \approx -0.073$, well above the Ising fixed-BC value $-\ln\sqrt{2}$ and close to the free-boundary value. The three observables vary smoothly and monotonically with $J_2$, consistent with a one-parameter family of partially transmissive conformal defects controlled by $J_2$. Throughout, the critical line is located using the bulk spin-correlator exponent $η=1/4$, the order-parameter exponent of the Ising universality class, which provides a robust criterion in this open geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes that the statistical concept of community detection, quantified by the Ramsey number, has a deep physical underpinning. By mapping network structure onto renormalization group flow and analyzing it with spin Hamiltonians, the authors prove that the detected community pattern is not merely an artifact but represents a genuine, thermodynamically ordered phase in the system.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on network science metrics, the rigorous application of RG flow and phase transition theory (Potts model) to determine intrinsic structural order provides powerful analogies for understanding emergent phases in complex quantum many-body systems or disordered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines when a network's community structure becomes an 'objective fact,' quantified by the Ramsey community number ($r_\kappa$), and establishes its thermodynamic stability.

**Main result.** The Ramsey community number is shown to be an exact renormalization-group crossing, and the optimal partition forms a persistent, thermodynamically ordered hierarchy of communities.

**Method.** The analysis combines Bayesian model comparison (evidence ratio) with Renormalization Group (RG) techniques applied to lattice models and spin Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The study uses the diamond hierarchical lattice structure and analyzes community ordering via a generalized Potts Hamiltonian, which is related to modularity maximization.

**Key observables.** Ramsey community number ($r_\kappa$), block-pair sufficient statistics eigenvalues ($\lambda_{	ext{vol}}, \lambda_{	ext{seam}}$), evidence density flow, and the critical temperatures ($T_c(q)$) for phase transitions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The detection threshold $q$, lattice parameters $(b, s)$, and community depth/number ($q_{	ext{opt}} \sim \sqrt{n}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the exact nature of real-space decimation on the diamond lattice to map RG steps precisely.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of the lattice, the flow of block densities towards a fixed point, and phase diagrams showing community order vs. temperature for different hierarchy depths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining $r_\kappa$ via RG analysis on the diamond lattice to deriving closed-form expressions for $r_\kappa$, culminating in placing the structure into a thermodynamic framework using a Potts Hamiltonian to prove persistent, level-by-level ordering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Ramsey community number $r_k$ is the smallest size at which a network is better described by communities than by none, under a Bayesian detection rule. On the diamond hierarchical lattice we show that $r_k$ is an exact renormalization-group crossing: the block-model sufficient statistics obey a linear map with eigenvalues $\{bs,b\}$, the degree-corrected evidence density flows to $\ln K$ at a community fixed point, and $r_k$ is the generation at which the running evidence clears the detection threshold. Degree correction advances detection by two generations. We derive $r_k(b,s;q)$ in closed form for the whole family. Finally, placing on the lattice the Reichardt--Bornholdt community Hamiltonian -- whose ground state is the partition itself -- we find an exact community-ordered phase: below the ferromagnetic critical temperature the two hubs lock into opposite communities for any resolution $γ>0$, a staggered order that persists as $n\to\infty$. Allowing each nested sub-community its own label, the optimal partition is a hierarchy of $q_{\rm opt}\sim\sqrt{n}$ communities, so the number of Potts states that best describes the network grows with the network. This hierarchy orders thermally level by level, through a cascade of first-order transitions whose temperatures fall as $1/\ln q$, so every stable level persists as $n\to\infty$: the emergent partition is detectable, optimal, and thermodynamically ordered.

</details>

### [Wilczek-Zee Realization of Uhlmann Parallel Transport](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05591v1)

**Authors:** Yu-Huan Huang, Xu-Yang Hou, Jia-Chen Tang, Hao Guo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.05591v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">

The solid black line is the theoretical prediction, while the symbols show numerical results obtained from adiabatic evo- lution under the parent Hamiltonian for ∆/ω = 10 (circles), 100 (squares), 1000 (triangles), and 10000 (stars).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper solves the challenge of simulating mixed-state geometric phases (Uhlmann phase) using only standard, Hermitian Hamiltonian evolution. By embedding the system into an enlarged four-level model, they prove that this complex phase can be exactly reproduced by the Wilczek-Zee connection in 1D parameter spaces. This offers a purely quantum mechanical framework for studying topological invariants of mixed states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool by mapping a complex, non-Hermitian geometric phase problem onto standard, well-understood Hermitian Hamiltonian dynamics, which is crucial for quantum simulation and topological characterization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to find a purely Hermitian, Hamiltonian-based method to simulate the mixed-state geometric phase (Uhlmann phase), which is otherwise complicated by non-Hermitian dynamics.

**Main result.** For one-dimensional parameter loops, they prove that the Uhlmann connection exactly matches the Wilczek-Zee connection derived from an auxiliary four-level Hermitian Hamiltonian. This establishes a purely quantum mechanical route to calculating mixed-state geometric phases.

**Method.** The authors construct an enlarged, Hermitian parent Hamiltonian whose degenerate ground state subspace is used to calculate the WZ connection and compare it directly to the definition of the Uhlmann connection.

**Model / system.** The system involves qubit density matrices undergoing adiabatic cyclic evolution along a 1D loop. This dynamics is embedded into an auxiliary four-level Hermitian Hamiltonian $H(t) = \Delta (I - P(t))$.

**Key observables.** Uhlmann phase ($\Phi_U$), Uhlmann holonomy matrix ($\mathcal{U}_U$), and the comparison between $	ext{AWZ}$ and $	ext{AU}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The purity parameter ($r$) of the density matrix, and the gap ratio ($\Delta/\omega$) used in the adiabatic approximation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction is rigorously proven only for one-dimensional parameter spaces; extension to 2D requires satisfying a Frobenius integrability condition, which is generally violated.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the calculated Uhlmann phase ($\Phi_U$) versus purity ($r$), showing numerical convergence onto theoretical predictions across varying gap ratios ($\Delta/\omega$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of simulating mixed-state phases. It then constructs the auxiliary Hamiltonian and derives the equivalence for 1D loops, confirming adiabatic reproduction. Finally, it analyzes the failure of this construction in higher dimensions due to integrability constraints.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Uhlmann phase extends geometric phases to mixed quantum states via a parallel-transport condition on purification amplitudes, yet its direct implementation under standard Hamiltonian dynamics is obstructed by the non-Hermitian nature of the purification. We establish that for any smooth one-dimensional closed loop of full-rank qubit density matrices, there exists a four-level Hermitian parent Hamiltonian whose doubly degenerate ground-state subspace carries a Wilczek--Zee connection exactly equal to the Uhlmann connection. Consequently, the Uhlmann holonomy is faithfully reproduced by adiabatic evolution in the enlarged system. We further prove that this auxiliary-field construction is obstructed in generic two-dimensional parameter spaces by a Frobenius integrability condition, which we derive explicitly. The one-dimensional Uhlmann phase is thus placed on the same footing as the non-Abelian Berry phase, offering a purely Hermitian, Hamiltonian-based route to simulating mixed-state geometric phases. Numerical integration of the adiabatic dynamics confirms the exact correspondence and validates the convergence to the Uhlmann holonomy in the large-gap limit.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses how environmental noise degrades temporal quantum correlations, measured by the CHSH inequality. It compares two ways to model controlled noise: independent environments versus equivalent Stinespring dilations. The key finding is that coherently controlling the dilation structure allows for a much broader revival of Bell nonclassicality than previously thought possible.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable resource (the choice of dilation) to enhance quantum tests in noisy environments, which is crucial for developing robust protocols in open quantum systems theory relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates how coherent control over noisy quantum evolutions can revive the violation of the temporal Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt (CHSH) inequality, which is otherwise degraded by environmental noise.

**Main result.** Coherent control using equivalent Stinespring dilations significantly extends the range of temporal CHSH violation compared to independent environment models or deterministic scenarios. This highlights that the choice of dilation realization is an operationally relevant resource in testing quantum correlations.

**Method.** The authors compare two physical implementations—independent environmental interactions versus unitarily equivalent Stinespring dilations—by calculating the temporal CHSH parameter ($S_T$) using joint probability distributions derived from these controlled evolutions.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is a single qubit undergoing sequential measurements separated by an intermediate dynamical evolution modeled by a quantum channel, specifically focusing on the amplitude-damping channel (ADC). The dynamics are explored via Stinespring dilations and Choi–Jamiołkowski representations.

**Key observables.** Temporal CHSH parameter ($S_T$), which must exceed 2 to witness nonclassicality; violation of the temporal CHSH inequality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($\gamma$, related to damping), and the specific choice between independent environments versus equivalent Stinespring dilations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on assumptions derived from Macrorealism (MR) regarding physical properties existing independently of observation, and non-invasive measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare temporal CHSH violation vs. damping strength ($\gamma$) for various control methods: Classical Bound, Incoherent Mixture, Environment-Selective Superposition, and Dilation-Selective Superposition, showing the superior activation range of the latter.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem using the temporal CHSH inequality under noise. It then develops mathematical frameworks comparing independent environment models against equivalent Stinespring dilation controls. The core argument is built by calculating $S_T$ for both control types and demonstrating that coherent control at the dilation level yields superior results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The temporal Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt (CHSH) inequality witnesses the nonclassicality of temporal correlations, but its violation is generally degraded by environmental noise. Here, we show that violation of the temporal CHSH inequality can be revived through coherent control of noisy quantum evolutions. We compare two physically distinct implementations: coherent control of noisy evolutions induced by interaction of the system with independent environments, and coherent control of two physically distinct, unitarily equivalent Stinespring dilations of the same noisy channel. Although these constructions generate identical deterministic system dynamics, they induce distinctly different post-selected evolutions. With a focus on the amplitude damping channel (ADC), we show that coherent control of equivalent dilations extend the range of temporal CHSH inequality violation well beyond both the incoherently controlled, or deterministic, scenario and what is achievable with independent environments. Under setting-independent post-selection of the coherent control implementation, the resulting violation further certifies that the channel is not strongly CHSH nonlocality-breaking. Our results identify the choice of Stinespring dilation as an operationally relevant resource in coherently controlled tests of temporal quantum correlations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper argues that electron-phonon dephasing exponents in thin disordered metals are not universal constants but rather 'crossover observables.' By analyzing controlled disorder series, the authors propose a diagnostic map framework that requires considering multiple parameters—like clean-to-dirty ratios and phonon confinement—for accurate physical interpretation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the concept of mapping complex physical behavior onto a diagnostic space based on multiple interacting parameters (like $qT l$ and confinement) is analogous to constructing feature spaces in advanced machine learning models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The electron-phonon dephasing rate in ultrathin disordered metallic films is often modeled by a simple power law, but the exponent varies widely, suggesting it is not a universal constant.

**Main result.** The exponent must be treated as a crossover observable whose interpretation requires considering multiple physical coordinates, such as the clean-to-dirty parameter and phonon confinement effects.

**Method.** The authors analyze data across a controlled-disorder series using $	ext{Ar}^+$-ion irradiation to map out how the dephasing exponent changes relative to other system parameters.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on electron-phonon dephasing ($	au_{e-	ext{ph}}^{-1}$) in ultrathin metallic films, specifically demonstrating results using $	ext{Ar}^+$-ion-irradiated Au films as a controlled-disorder platform.

**Key observables.** Electron-phonon exponent ($p$), prefactor $A(l)$, and the pure-dirty crossover temperature ($T_{	ext{tr}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Clean-to-dirty parameter ($qT l$), phonon confinement, film thickness, and disorder character (dragged vs. static scattering potentials).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The exponent $p$ is not a universal constant but an effective crossover observable dependent on multiple coupled physical parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of the fitted exponent $p$ on the crossover temperature $T_{	ext{tr}}$ for irradiated Au films, and propose a diagnostic map correlating $p$ with other system coordinates.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting the variability of the power law exponent; it then develops a multi-parameter 'diagnostic map' framework using controlled disorder series data to show that simple single-variable fits are insufficient for interpretation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron-phonon dephasing in ultrathin disordered metallic films is often summarized by a power law, $τ_{e-ph}^{-1}=AT^p$. However, thin metals, alloys, and superconducting films exhibit effective exponents close to 2, 3, and 4, as well as intermediate nonuniversal values. We argue that in ultrathin supported films, the exponent should be treated not as a universal material constant, but as a crossover observable. Its interpretation requires several coordinates: the clean-to-dirty parameter $q_T l$, phonon confinement and film-substrate acoustic coupling, and the microscopic character of disorder.   The diagnostic picture is illustrated using Ar-ion-irradiated Au films as a controlled-disorder series. In these films, the fitted electron-phonon exponent remains near $p\simeq 2$ for low values of the pure-dirty crossover temperature $T_{tr}\simeq \hbar s/(k_B l)$ and increases toward $p\simeq 2.8$ as disorder increases. At the same time, the prefactor trend indicates suppression of electron-phonon scattering with decreasing mean free path, consistent with dirty-limit weakening rather than static-disorder enhancement. The persistence of $p<4$ points to the role of thin-film or film-substrate phonon effects. The resulting diagnostic map provides a compact framework for comparing electron-phonon dephasing data in ultrathin disordered films.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work rigorously compares two types of braiding statistics: Bockstein braiding ($W_N$) and conventional three-loop braiding. The authors show that $W_N$ exhausts all loop statistics arising from Abelian fusion rules, but the more complex three-loop braiding must possess non-Abelian features. This advances the understanding of topological order by providing a precise mathematical distinction between different types of quantum entanglement statistics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is abstract topology, the rigorous mathematical machinery developed—relating quantum phases to cohomology groups and local cancellation criteria—is highly relevant for understanding topological order in condensed matter systems like fractional quantum Hall states or superconducting qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares Bockstein braiding statistics with conventional three-loop braiding for topological excitations to determine if they are mathematically equivalent.

**Main result.** Conventional three-loop braiding goes beyond the classification of loop statistics derived from Abelian fusion rules, suggesting it requires non-Abelian fusion rules or exotic particle-loop interactions.

**Method.** The authors use algebraic methods involving local cancellation criteria and cohomology theory to define and classify topological braiding processes ($W_N$).

**Model / system.** The study analyzes topological excitations in $p$- and $q$-dimensional settings, using lattice models based on $\mathbb{Z}_N^2$ toric-code-type ground states.

**Key observables.** Braiding phase (a value in U(1)), the Bockstein response $\tilde{\beta}(B)$, and the statistical process $W_N(X,Y)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The finite order $N$ of the excitations is crucial for the quantization of the statistics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The classification relies on assuming Abelian fusion rules when deriving the cohomology bounds, which is then used to contrast with three-loop braiding.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares ordinary, Bockstein, and three-loop braiding in different dimensions. Figure 2 illustrates the Bockstein braiding process using lattice configurations and spacetime histories.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general framework for classifying topological statistics via an 'Excitation Complex,' applies this to define $W_N$, proves its quantization on a lattice model, and finally contrasts this result with three-loop braiding theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Braiding statistics of $p$- and $q$-dimensional topological excitations is conventionally defined in $p+q+2$ spatial dimensions. We find a novel statistical process $W_N(X,Y)=(Y^{-1}X^{-1})^N(YX)^N$ for two order-$N$ excitations in $p+q+1$ dimensions, detecting the Bockstein response $A\smile β(B)$. This new statistics and fermionic loop statistics exhaust all loop statistics in three dimensions whose fusion rules form an Abelian group $G$, classified by $H^5(B^2G,U(1))$. Surprisingly, conventional three-loop braiding goes beyond this classification, so it must have non-Abelian fusion rules. We suggest viewing three-loop braiding as particle-loop braiding together with exotic fusion rules between loops and point-like defects. We also try to clarify the relationship between statistics and symmetry anomaly.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the fundamental issue of exponential divergence when using Carleman linearization to solve nonlinear differential equations. The authors introduce an advanced regularization method based on analytical continuation and conformal mapping to stabilize the solution over long times. They validate this approach both classically and by detailing a concrete implementation pathway using quantum algorithms like LCU.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational bottleneck of simulating complex nonlinear dynamics by providing a mathematically rigorous technique to extend the time horizon achievable via linearization methods, which is crucial for quantum simulation feasibility.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is overcoming the exponential divergence inherent when using Carleman linearization to solve nonlinear differential equations over long time scales.

**Main result.** The authors propose a regularization technique based on analytical continuation via conformal mapping, successfully correcting the divergence for models like the logistic and KPP-Fisher equations.

**Method.** They employ analytic continuation using Möbius transformations (conformal maps) to map the infinite domain of time evolution into a region where the series converges, followed by regularizing the resulting divergent factor.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on classical nonlinear models such as the logistic equation and KPP-Fisher reaction-diffusion equations. For quantum implementation, it uses the Linear Combination of Unitaries (LCU) algorithm applied to the linearized system matrix.

**Key observables.** Convergence behavior across time ($t$), stability regimes determined by parameters $c$ and initial conditions ($x_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularization parameter $c$, truncation orders $M$ and $K$, and the scale parameter derived from the conformal map.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that setting the regularization order $M$ proportional to the matrix size $K$ ($M \approx K$) is appropriate for convergence. The quantum implementation relies on embedding non-unitary matrices into unitary ones via block encoding.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Carleman linearization against exact solutions, showing divergence over time; they also present phase diagrams classifying convergence regimes based on parameters like $c$ and $x_0$ for various models.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the failure of naive Carleman expansion due to divergence. It then introduces the analytical continuation via conformal mapping (Möbius map) as a fix, regularizing the solution using a specific function. Finally, it validates this corrected method on multiple physical models and details its implementation using quantum algorithms like LCU.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinear differential equations play a crucial role in modeling a wide range of phenomena, yet their solutions remain notoriously difficult to obtain. With the rapid development of quantum computing, quantum algorithms for efficiently solving such equations are actively being explored. One promising approach is based on Carleman linearization, which transforms nonlinear differential equations into linear systems. However, this method suffers from exponential divergence beyond a certain time scale. By reformulating the solutions in terms of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, we identify that this divergence originates from the Laurent expansion outside its neighborhood of convergence. To address this issue, we insert a regularized function to the divergent solution hinted by analytical continuation. We validate this divergence-correction method on both the logistic equation and some other partial differential equations like KPP-Fisher equations and Phase-Field models under periodic conditions. We implement our method for the logistic equation using the Linear Combination of Unitaries (LCU) quantum algorithm, providing a detailed complexity and error analysis.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using an enhanced Quantum Reservoir Network (QRN) architecture to predict the behavior of chaotic PDEs like the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky system. By incorporating metrologically-useful GHZ states into the circuit design, the authors achieve superior prediction accuracy compared to standard QRN implementations or classical methods. This demonstrates a powerful pathway for leveraging quantum entanglement in machine learning for complex physical modeling.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses applying quantum resources to model complex, non-equilibrium dynamics found in fluid physics and chaos theory, which is a major frontier for quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is predicting chaotic partial differential equations (PDEs) using quantum computation.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum Reservoir Network (QRN) utilizing metrologically-useful GHZ state preparation significantly outperforms alternative QRN implementations and classical benchmarks in predicting the latent space of the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky system.

**Method.** A hybrid approach combining a quantum reservoir network with a classical autoencoder is used to map high-dimensional time-series data into a low-dimensional, predictable latent space representation.

**Model / system.** The model targets the 1-D Kuramoto-Sivashinsky (KS) system, a high-dimensional chaotic PDE. The quantum circuit uses recurrent blocks and incorporates GHZ state preparation to enhance entanglement and measurement sensitivity.

**Key observables.** Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) on the test set latent space KS system; Quantum Fisher Information (QFI); expectation values of readout qubits over time steps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n_q$), the depth of the circuit, and the number of measurement shots ($	ext{nshots}$) are critical parameters analyzed for scaling behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that embedding the data into a quantum state space using specific unitary transformations (like those generating GHZ states) can capture the necessary dynamics for accurate prediction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the full GHZ QRN Circuit structure, compare latent space test performance across different circuit types (GHZ vs. random/none), and show reconstructed PDE output compared to ground truth data.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of chaotic PDE prediction, details the hybrid QRN methodology incorporating metrologically-useful states (GHZ unitary), presents rigorous simulations comparing performance against baselines, and concludes with analysis of scaling laws and potential limitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interest in using quantum computers for the purpose of predicting chaotic partial differential equations (PDEs) has been growing with the advent of newer low-error quantum computers and robust simulation tools. In this paper, we present a method that utilizes a quantum reservoir network (QRN) to predict latent space representations of the high-dimensional chaotic 1-D Kuramoto-Sivashinksy (KS) system. This hybrid approach takes advantage of advancements in classical machine learning (ML) through the use of a classical autoencoder as well as techniques from quantum metrology through the use of a unitary that creates metrologically-useful states. Through rigorous simulation and analysis, we show that the proposed method outperforms alternative QRN implementations without this metrologically-useful state preparation, and also show better performance than classical echo-state networks when weight regularization is not used. Finally, we bring to light potential issues that can arise when using autoencoders within QRC pipelines.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper establishes a provable learning separation for predicting the time evolution of quantum many-body systems. By formulating this as a PAC-learning problem, they show that while quantum computers can efficiently learn the underlying Hamiltonian from short-time data, classical algorithms face an exponential computational barrier unless BQP is classically easy. This rigorously connects quantum simulation difficulty to fundamental complexity theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous complexity-theoretic boundary for quantum simulation tasks within QML, directly connecting fundamental questions about computational complexity (BQP vs P/poly) to practical challenges in simulating complex physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish provable learning separations for physically motivated Quantum Machine Learning (QML) tasks involving predicting the time-evolution of quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate an exponential learning separation, showing that while a quantum procedure can efficiently learn the underlying Hamiltonian from short-time samples and perform inference, no classical polynomial-time algorithm can solve this task unless BQP is contained in P/poly.

**Method.** The work uses PAC-learning theory applied to a supervised learning problem where training data consists of expectation values of observables on time-evolved states. Classical hardness is proven by embedding a BQP-complete computation into the dynamics using specific Hamiltonian constructions.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum many-body system whose dynamics are governed by an unknown Hamiltonian H. The learning task involves inferring H from measurements of expectation values $\langle \hat{O}(t) angle$ at various time points $t$.

**Key observables.** Expectation values of observables ($\langle \hat{O} angle$), the underlying Hamiltonian coefficients ($\lambda_a$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The evolution time interval $[0, T]$ (polynomially large), the desired precision $\epsilon$, and the confidence parameter $\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The separation relies on the complexity-theoretic assumption BQP $
ot\subseteq 	ext{P/poly}$. The analysis also assumes access to 'sufficiently rich' training data enabling Hamiltonian learning.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the process: (a) Training samples are generated by time-evolution of H over random times t. (b) Labels are a vector of expectation values on pre-determined observables. (c) Short-time samples are used classically to identify H. (d) A quantum computer performs inference, potentially predicting a BQP-complete computation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the learning task by formulating it in PAC terms, deriving error bounds for Hamiltonian estimation using short-time data, and then constructing a classically hard instance based on embedding BQP-complete computations into the time dynamics to prove the separation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Given that quantum computers are naturally suited to simulate the behavior of quantum many-body systems, an immediate question arises: can one formulate physically motivated quantum machine learning (QML) tasks that exhibit learning separations? We address this problem by studying the learnability of quantum many-body dynamics from the perspective of probably approximately correct (PAC)-learning. Concretely, we devise a supervised learning problem where the training set consists of specifications of randomized stabilizer probe states, evolution times sampled uniformly from a polynomially large time interval $[0,T]$, coupled with expectation values of certain observables evaluated on the resulting time-evolved state under an unknown Hamiltonian. For this learning task, we provide an efficient quantum procedure whose training phase learns the underlying Hamiltonian from short-time training samples, and whose deployment phase combines Hamiltonian simulation with the classical shadows protocol to perform inference on a newly given data point. By contrast, the existence of $O(\mathsf{poly}(n))$-time instances ensures classical hardness: by embedding a $\mathsf{BQP}$-complete computation into the polynomially long time-dynamics of a low-intersection variant of the Feynman-Kitaev clock Hamiltonian construction, we show that, for a certain family of input distributions, no randomized classical polynomial-time algorithm can fulfill our learning condition, unless $\mathsf{BQP}\subseteq\mathsf{P/poly}$. Furthermore, we show that the classically hard instance maintains quantum learnability. We also give an interpretation of our results in learning-assisted certified quantum simulation. Taken together, our results demonstrate a rigorous learning separation for a natural ML task based on Hamiltonian evolution, while building connections between quantum learning theory, quantum simulation, and QML.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel Signed Multifractal Detrended Cross-Correlation Coefficient ($ho_{SMFDCCA}$) designed to overcome numerical instabilities associated with analyzing negative fluctuation orders in multifractal analysis. By conditioning the correlation on local fluctuation amplitudes, the observable remains bounded within [-1, 1] for all q. It successfully characterizes how cross-correlations change based on both temporal scale and the magnitude of fluctuations across diverse real-world datasets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum systems, the rigorous statistical framework developed for analyzing complex, non-stationary time series (like those found in quantum measurements or open system dynamics) provides advanced tools for characterizing multi-scale correlations that could be adapted to analyze noise spectra from quantum experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard Multifractal Detrended Cross-Correlation Coefficient fails for negative fluctuation orders due to numerical instabilities and unbounded values.

**Main result.** The proposed Signed Multifractal Detrended Cross-Correlation Coefficient remains strictly bounded within [-1, 1] and successfully characterizes scale- and amplitude-dependent cross-correlations in diverse datasets.

**Method.** It introduces an amplitude-conditioned observable by using locally normalized detrended correlations weighted by a fluctuation amplitude term to ensure mathematical stability for all orders q.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to analyzing time series data from various physical and financial systems, including stock market indices (DJIA/NASDAQ) and meteorological records (temperature/humidity).

**Key observables.** Signed Multifractal Detrended Cross-Correlation Coefficient ($ho_{SMFDCCA}(n, q)$), local detrended correlation coefficient ($r_v(n)$), and local fluctuation amplitude ($A_v(n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fluctuation order $q$ (ranging from negative to positive values); temporal scale $n$; regularization constant $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the cross-correlation structure can be robustly captured by an amplitude-conditioned weighted average, and a small regularization parameter ($\epsilon$) is necessary for numerical stability when $q < 0$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $ho_{SMFDCCA}(n, q)$ dependence on $q$ for meteorological variables (e.g., max vs min temperature), showing complex evolution across scales.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of existing correlation coefficients for negative orders; it then rigorously derives and proposes the $ho_{SMFDCCA}$; validation is performed using fractional Gaussian noise, followed by empirical applications to financial and climate data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The multifractal detrended cross-correlation coefficient $ρ_q(n)$ is widely used to investigate scale-dependent interactions, but its application to negative fluctuation orders is affected by numerical instabilities, unbounded values, and interpretational difficulties. We propose a Signed Multifractal Detrended Cross-Correlation Coefficient, $ρ_{\mathrm{SMFDCCA}}(n,q)$, an amplitude-conditioned correlation observable for multifractal detrended analysis, based on locally normalized detrended correlations and regularized fluctuation amplitudes. The proposed coefficient preserves the sign of local interactions, remains strictly bounded within $[-1,1]$ for both positive and negative values of $q$, and eliminates the corrective procedures required by previous approaches. Validation using independent fractional Gaussian noise confirms the absence of spurious cross-correlations and the numerical stability of the method. Applications demonstrate that the proposed observable resolves how cross-correlations evolve jointly with temporal scale and fluctuation amplitude, revealing scale- and amplitude-dependent correlation structures, including stronger synchronization during large fluctuations in stock-market indices and heterogeneous coupling patterns in temperature records.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Path–Endpoint Separation Pulse (PESP) technique to improve fast single-qubit gates on transmon qubits by treating transient leakage exposure and final state leakage as separate control problems. By applying a path-shaping pulse and an endpoint-cancellation pulse sequentially, the method achieves superior suppression of both dephasing-induced errors and coherent residual errors compared to standard techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly modular and interpretable framework for quantum control in superconducting qubits, moving beyond monolithic optimization to address distinct physical error channels (coherent vs. incoherent).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Fast single-qubit gates on weakly anharmonic transmons are limited by leakage to noncomputational states, and standard mitigation techniques fail to address both transient exposure and endpoint errors independently.

**Main result.** The proposed Path–Endpoint Separation Pulse (PESP) successfully decouples these two control objectives, significantly reducing dephasing-induced leakage ($\sim 20\%$) and the residual endpoint floor ($7	imes10^{-7}$ to $3	imes10^{-8}$) independently.

**Method.** The authors developed a staged pulse sequence combining a Path-Shaping Pulse (PSP) to suppress time-integrated exposure and an Endpoint-Cancellation Pulse (ECP) to null the final drive spectrum amplitude.

**Model / system.** The system is based on transmon qubits modeled as weakly anharmonic oscillators, analyzed using a rotating-frame Kerr-oscillator truncation Hamiltonian. Leakage analysis involves tracking populations in higher energy levels ($|2angle, |3angle$).

**Key observables.** Endpoint Leakage ($|	ilde{\Lambda}(\eta)|^2$), Transient Leakage Exposure ($ar{P}_A$), Dephasing-induced Excess Leakage ($P_{\phi}^{	ext{excess}}$), and Gate Fidelity ($F_{	ext{comp}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate time (e.g., $10 	ext{ ns}$ for $R_X(\pi/2)$), anharmonicity ($\eta$), dephasing rate ($\gamma_\phi$), and the relative weights assigned to path vs. endpoint control.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that endpoint leakage and transient exposure are mathematically distinct control objectives, allowing for modular optimization; hardware limitations like ZOH sampling and LP filtering are also analyzed as constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate pulse waveforms, spectral components, and leakage trajectories comparing PESP to standard DRAG. Tables quantify path reduction across parameter grids and detail the sensitivity of the final error floor to various hardware imperfections (amplitude mismatch, sampling rate).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the limitations of existing methods by distinguishing between endpoint and transient leakage sources. It then introduces the PESP protocol, detailing the PSP for path shaping and the ECP for endpoint cancellation. Finally, it validates the method through extensive numerical simulations testing robustness against noise, sampling artifacts, and parameter variations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast single-qubit gates on weakly anharmonic transmons are limited by leakage to noncomputational states, and standard mitigations such as DRAG (derivative removal by adiabatic gate) act on the leakage amplitude at the end of the gate. We show that this endpoint amplitude and the transient leakage exposure accumulated during the gate are two distinct control objectives that can be assigned to separate modules. The endpoint is a single sample of the drive spectrum, $|\tildeΛ(η)|^2$; the exposure is a band integral about $η$ and governs leakage under dephasing, and the spectral-null condition $\tildeΛ(η)=0$ constrains only the former. We realize this split in a path--endpoint separation pulse (PESP): a path-shaping pulse suppresses the exposure, and a two-tone endpoint-cancellation pulse cancels the residual amplitude. For a $10$ ns $R_{X}(π/2)$ gate at $η/2π=0.2$ GHz, in numerical simulations the path-shaping pulse reduces the dephasing exposure by ${\sim}21\%$ relative to cosine DRAG and the independently simulated Lindblad excess leakage by ${\sim}20\%$, consistent with $P_{\mathrm{excess}}^φ\simeqγ_φT\bar{P}_{A}^{\mathrm{deph}}$, whereas matched-budget endpoint-only and spectral-null controls leave it essentially unchanged. The residual endpoint floor splits exactly into a $|2\rangle$ back-action and a $|3\rangle$ cascade, which the two tones cancel one-to-one, driving the floor at the path-exposure knee from ${\sim}7\times10^{-7}$ to ${\sim}3\times10^{-8}$ without perturbing the path. By separating transient exposure from endpoint leakage, PESP turns leakage suppression in fast weakly anharmonic gates into a modular, interpretable control problem: dephasing-induced leakage and the coherent residual error are reduced by separate, individually verifiable modules.

</details>

### [Geometric obstructions to quadratic time scaling in multiparameter quantum estimation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06410v1)

**Authors:** Eoin O'Connor, Jiayu He, Matteo G. A. Paris, Marco G. Genoni  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.06410v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>
