**Summary.** This paper establishes a universal geometric obstruction proving that simultaneous quadratic scaling in multiparameter quantum estimation fails if the commuting components of the Hamiltonian derivatives are linearly dependent. The failure manifests as a bottleneck limiting precision to $O(t^0)$ rather than the desired $O(t^{-2})$. This diagnostic tool is crucial for designing high-precision quantum sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, computable diagnostic tool (the Gram matrix) to predict fundamental limitations in quantum sensing protocols before extensive numerical simulation is required.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental geometric conditions under which multiparameter quantum estimation can achieve the optimal quadratic scaling ($t^{-2}$) in precision.

**Main result.** A universal obstruction exists: linear dependence among the commuting components of the Hamiltonian derivatives limits the achievable precision to $O(t^0)$ scaling, regardless of ancilla assistance.

**Method.** The analysis uses geometric tools like the Gram matrix and asymptotic analysis of the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) derived from unitary evolution.

**Model / system.** The framework is general for time-independent Hamiltonians $\hat{H}(\mathbf{g})$, applicable to discrete systems (e.g., spin magnets) and continuous variable setups (e.g., quantum harmonic oscillator).

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM), SLD bound, Gram matrix of diagonal generators ($G_{\mu
u}$), and the minimum eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\min}$) of the QFIM.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time $t$, number of parameters $\mu$, and the structure of the Hamiltonian derivatives decomposed into commuting ($\hat{D}_\mu$) and non-commuting ($\hat{O}_\mu$) parts.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on decomposing parameter dependence into commuting/non-commuting parts, and in some examples (like the QHO), assumes Gaussian states for covariance matrix calculations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the structure revolves around deriving diagnostic criteria based on linear algebra applied to generator components.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general framework using Hamiltonian decomposition, derives the geometric obstruction condition via the Gram matrix, analyzes specific physical examples (spin systems, QHO), and discusses potential workarounds like nuisance parameter treatment or adaptive control.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unitary encoding of a single parameter provides quadratic enhancement in precision, with the quantum Fisher information scaling quadratically with the encoding time. However, when estimating multiple parameters simultaneously, this fundamental scaling is not guaranteed. Here, we establish a universal geometric obstruction that dictates when multiparameter quantum metrology fails to achieve simultaneous $t^{-2}$ scaling. By decomposing the Hamiltonian derivatives into components that commute and do not commute with the system Hamiltonian, we prove that linear dependence among the commuting components inevitably generates a slow parameter direction whose Fisher information remains bounded as O$(t^0)$, limiting the overall estimation precision. We demonstrate this mechanism in both discrete- and continuous-variable setups, including collective spin magnetometry and a generalized quantum harmonic oscillator, and contrast it with the Lipkin--Meshkov--Glick model where $t^{-2}$ decay is preserved. Remarkably, while the slow direction fundamentally limits the achievable precision, the measurement incompatibility between fast and slow directions decays as $1/t$, rendering the symmetric logarithmic derivative bound asymptotically saturable. Our framework provides a readily computable diagnostic, given by the Gram matrix of the diagonal generators, for identifying such obstructions in arbitrary multiparameter estimation problems. We further show that the bottleneck can be circumvented by relegating slow directions to nuisance parameters or by employing adaptive quantum control.

</details>

**Authors:** Praneeth Narisetty, Uday Kumar Reddy Kattamanchi, Shiva Nagendra Babu Kore

The twin (a) renders a telemetry window from one seed; a single summarize_window feeds the identical window to both the 5-role agent panel (b) and the supervised ML path (c). Because both methods see the same seed-addressed scenarios, the head-to-head uses the exact paired McNemar test (d). **Summary.** This paper introduces 'Onnes,' a digital-twin simulator of dilution refrigerators used in quantum computing. It compares a multi-agent LLM diagnostic system against supervised ML classifiers for identifying complex cryogenic faults. The key finding is that by incorporating physics knowledge and few-shot examples, the LLM agent can achieve near state-of-the-art classification accuracy without explicit retraining.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on AI/ML for infrastructure, the reliance on a detailed physics model (digital twin) to constrain and validate an advanced AI agent is highly relevant for building trustworthy quantum hardware control systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is improving cryogenic fault diagnosis for superconducting quantum computers, moving beyond simple threshold alarms to specific diagnoses.

**Main result.** A physics-grounded LLM agent, enhanced with few-shot demonstrations, achieved classification accuracy matching supervised ML classifiers (0.990 vs 0.985) on challenging, confusable faults.

**Method.** The study uses a head-to-head comparison between a multi-agent LLM simulator and a supervised ML classifier operating on simulated cryogenic telemetry data.

**Model / system.** The system is 'Onnes,' a physics-grounded digital twin of a dilution refrigerator, simulating temperature, flow, and pressure across multiple stages using real noise fingerprints from BlueFors logs.

**Key observables.** Fault classification accuracy, false-alarm rate (FAR), detection capability, and structured diagnostic output (e.g., class / severity / action).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperatures down to mK; 1000-turn evaluation length; six distinct physics-grounded fault classes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The LLM agent's performance gain is attributed almost entirely to the curated contrastive few-shot demonstrations, not inherent multi-agent debate mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the end-to-end system architecture comparing the LLM panel vs. ML path, and show how different faults overlap on temperature but separate on flow/pressure.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem (cryo fault diagnosis), details the Onnes digital twin simulator, compares zero-shot LLM performance against supervised ML benchmarks, and concludes with sim-to-real validation results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dilution refrigerators are the enabling infrastructure of superconducting quantum computers, yet their fault diagnosis is still dominated by threshold alarms that report that something is wrong, not what. We present Onnes, a physics-grounded digital-twin simulator of a dilution refrigerator (a forward physics model with a learned real-fridge noise fingerprint) that drives a live multi-agent LLM operations layer, and use it for a controlled head-to-head between a zero-shot LLM agent panel and a supervised ML classifier on cryogenic fault diagnosis. The twin couples a real dilution-cooling floor, a noise-and-correlation fingerprint learned from real BlueFors logs, and six physics-grounded fault classes, three engineered to overlap on temperature but separate on flow and pressure. Across a 1000-turn evaluation the zero-shot panel shows no significant difference from the classifier on detection but trails on classification, its errors concentrating on the confusable faults. Curated contrastive few-shot demonstrations and self-consistency voting then raise classification accuracy from 0.685 to 0.990, matching the supervised classifier (0.985) with no parameter updates and six labeled demonstrations; an ablation attributes the gain almost entirely to the demonstrations. Run as a continuous monitor across a nine-run fault-by-seed sweep, the agent catches every developing fault within one poll interval, and a confidence gate suppresses pre-onset false alarms whose rate is backend-dependent. As a first sim-to-real check, a detector trained purely on real BlueFors telemetry posts a real-hardware false-alarm rate of 6.4% and 100% recall on physics faults injected onto real held-out windows. All numbers are drawn verbatim from released run logs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work conducts a comprehensive comparison of client device architectures designed for Verifiable Blind Quantum Computing (VBQC). By analyzing emission-based, measurement-based, and rotation-based approaches using photonic qubits against a matter-qubit server, it establishes a framework to guide the selection of the most suitable architecture based on specific security or performance requirements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly compares hardware implementations for quantum computation protocols, offering guidance on selecting optimal architectures based on practical constraints like error rates and required fidelity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses Verifiable Blind Quantum Computing (VBQC), which allows a client to delegate quantum computations while ensuring privacy and verifying the server's correct execution.

**Main result.** It provides a comparative analysis of three main client architectures—emission-based, measurement-based, and rotation-based—for VBQC with a matter-qubit server.

**Method.** The authors compare these architectures across security guarantees, derived rate equations, error behavior, and hardware cost using information-theoretic security models.

**Model / system.** The analysis is restricted to single-server, single-client protocols based on measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC), utilizing photonic qubits for communication with a matter-qubit server.

**Key observables.** Security guarantees, protocol execution rates, error behavior, and hardware cost/design considerations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Single-server/single-client protocols; information-theoretic security requirements.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of matter qubits for the server and photonic qubits for communication, focusing on single-user scenarios.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 depicts a taxonomy classifying client architectures into emission-based, measurement-based, and rotation-based categories.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically classifies client capabilities (Emission, Measurement, Rotation) by detailing the physical mechanisms for state transfer within each category, followed by a comparative evaluation across multiple performance dimensions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Blind quantum computing (BQC) allows a client to delegate quantum computations to a remote server without revealing the input, computation, or output. In addition to being blind, the client can sometimes also verify that the server has performed their instructions correctly, a property known as verifiability. A key part of realizing such verifiable BQC (VBQC) is choosing the design of the client device: many architectures have been proposed, each with different hardware requirements, security properties, and performance characteristics, making it difficult to identify which is most suitable for a given implementation. In this work, we present a comparative analysis of client architectures for VBQC with a matter-qubit server. We restrict our analysis to single-server, single-client protocols with information-theoretic security based on measurement-based quantum computation. We identify three main categories of client: emission-based, measurement-based, and rotation-based, each with multiple variants depending on how the client interacts with the server. We evaluate each across different dimensions: we compare guarantees of existing corresponding security proofs, we derive equations for the rate at which each client can execute a protocol, we provide an overview of each architecture's error behaviour, and discuss hardware cost and design considerations. Client architectures implementing measurement-based remote state preparation and reflection-based teleportation emerge as strong default candidates, but as the right choice remains context-dependent, we provide a framework for navigating considerations to guide the selection of the most suitable architecture for a given setting.

</details>

### [Enumeration of Laplacian integral and {-1,0,1}-diagonalizable graphs](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06336v1)

**Authors:** Nathaniel Johnston, Sarah Plosker, Luis M. B. Varona  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.06336v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<img src="2607.06336_figures/2607.06336_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: A graph with spectrum St8,9u6 9, thus providing a counterexample to [HT23, Conjecture 5.4].</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep structural characterization and extensive enumeration of graphs whose Laplacian matrices have integer eigenvalues or possess specific $\{-1, 0, 1\}$-valued eigenvector bases. By linking these graph properties to underlying algebraic structures, the authors advance both pure graph theory and provide tools relevant for analyzing quantum walks on networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core subject is graph theory, the explicit mention of Laplacian matrices and their connection to Continuous-Time Quantum Walks (CTQWs) makes it highly relevant for theoretical physicists studying open quantum systems dynamics on structured lattices or graphs.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper focuses on characterizing and enumerating two specific families of graphs: Laplacian integral graphs (where Laplacian eigenvalues are integers) and $\{-1, 0, 1\}$-diagonalizable graphs.

**Main result.** They developed structure theorems for these graphs of prime order and provided extensive computational enumeration results up to moderate orders. They also established an exponential lower bound on the number of connected $\{-1, 0, 1\}$-diagonalizable graphs.

**Method.** The methods combine algebraic graph theory with advanced linear algebra techniques, including developing structure theorems based on properties like 'balanced vectors' and using computational tools (like QR factorization) to verify eigenvector bases.

**Model / system.** The mathematical model is the graph $G$, analyzed via its Laplacian matrix $L$. The concepts are related to Continuous-Time Quantum Walks (CTQWs), suggesting a connection to quantum dynamics on graphs.

**Key observables.** Integer eigenvalues of the Laplacian matrix, and the existence of an eigenvector basis whose entries belong only to $\{-1, 0, 1\}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order $n$ of the graph (especially prime orders), and the structure of the associated eigenspaces.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For some results, the analysis assumes considering sorted, non-increasing entries for balanced vectors. Computationally, the methods become infeasible for graphs exceeding $n \approx 19$.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention Figure 1 showing a graph realizing a specific spectrum that serves as a counterexample to an earlier conjecture.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops structure theorems (especially for prime order) and then applies novel computational techniques, including DFS searches over balanced vectors and rank checks on eigenvector basis matrices, to enumerate graphs in the two specified classes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A graph with Laplacian matrix $L$ is called Laplacian integral if the eigenvalues of $L$ are all integers, and it is called $\{-1,0,1\}$-diagonalizable if $L$ has a full set of eigenvectors with entries from $\{-1,0,1\}$. We herein develop a structure theorem for both Laplacian integral graphs and $\{-1,0,1\}$-diagonalizable graphs of prime order, and combine it with some novel computational techniques to characterize all such graphs for orders larger than was previously possible. For example, we enumerate all Laplacian integral and $\{-1,0,1\}$-diagonalizable graphs of order $13$ or less, all $\{-1,0,1\}$-diagonalizable graphs of prime order $23$ or less, all regular integral graphs of order $15$ or less, and all regular $\{-1,0,1\}$-diagonalizable graphs of prime order $53$ or less. As an immediate byproduct of our work, we show that the $S_{n,n}$ conjecture for Laplacian integral graphs is true when $n = 12$, thus making $n = 16$ the smallest open case; additionally, we disprove two related conjectures regarding Laplacian spectra. We also establish an exponential lower bound on the number of connected $\{-1,0,1\}$-diagonalizable graphs of order $n$, thus beating the previously best-known (subexponential) lower bound. Finally, we show that every bipartite $\{-1,0,1\}$-diagonalizable graph is regular (a fact that fails to generalize to Laplacian integral graphs).

</details>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the challenge of making Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks practical by integrating them with Bivariate Bicycle (BB) quantum error correction. By implementing a transversal encoding scheme, the authors demonstrate that their low-overhead QEC technique significantly enhances the network's ability to train and maintain performance under realistic hardware noise compared to unprotected methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the critical gap between theoretical quantum computation models (like QCNNs) and near-term hardware limitations (NISQ noise). The focus on low-overhead, structured codes like BB codes is highly relevant for experimentalists building fault-tolerant quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is enabling practical Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks (QCNNs) by mitigating high noise levels inherent in current quantum hardware while avoiding the prohibitive qubit overhead of existing error correction codes.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrate that integrating a low-overhead, distance-4 Bivariate Bicycle (BB) code via a transversal architecture significantly improves QCNN trainability and convergence compared to unprotected or standard implementations under realistic noise conditions.

**Method.** They propose embedding the QCNN operations within the protective subspace defined by the BB quantum error-correcting code. The training process involves simulating noisy evolution using transversal gates that map logical operations onto physical qubits, allowing for direct comparison of performance metrics like loss convergence.

**Model / system.** The system models a QCNN applied to tasks like Quantum Phase Recognition (QPR) in 1D spin systems. Error correction is achieved using the Bivariate Bicycle (BB) code, which offers a favorable balance between high error threshold and low qubit overhead compared to surface codes.

**Key observables.** Training loss convergence, learning rate, error rates ($p$), and classification accuracy for quantum phases (e.g., SPT phase).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance $d=4$, noise probability $p \in \{0.0001, 0.001\}$, and the structure of the BB code defined over $\mathbb{F}_2[x, y]/(x^\ell-1, y^m-1)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that transversal operations can successfully encode QCNN logic while respecting the locality constraints of the underlying quantum error-correcting code structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare prediction values (violin plots) between standard and transversal QCNNs across varying Pauli noise levels, showing superior resilience for the transversal approach. Other figures illustrate phase boundaries or circuit expectation values.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need to improve QCNNs against hardware noise. It then introduces the BB code as a solution, detailing the integration via transversal operations. The core of the work involves simulating and comparing standard vs. transversal QCNN performance under increasing levels of Pauli noise ($p$), culminating in validation of the low-overhead error correction scheme.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum convolutional neural networks (QCNNs) combine the power of quantum computing and classical CNN for computational speedup in classification tasks. However, noise levels on state-of-the-art quantum devices remain too high for practical QCNN execution. In addition, despite the reliable surface code providing a method for error rates below a threshold value, they have a prohibitively large qubit cost. Recently introduced bivariate bicycle (BB) codes are of particular interest for their high error threshold, constant encoding rate, and linear code distance. Through simulation with realistic hardware noise sources, we demonstrate that a 4-qubit unprotected QCNN fails to converge and exhibits a worse learning rate compared to numerical simulations. Addressing both limitations, we propose a distance-4 BB quantum error-correction (QEC) technique for QCNNs. In doing so, we validate that our low-overhead QEC technique for QCNNS represents a step toward practical QCNNs.

</details>

**Summary.** The research introduces a novel, scalable method for creating high-performance plasmonic nanostructures by forming many-body clusters through controlled dewetting. By leveraging statistical averaging over particle disorder, the system achieves superior electromagnetic field enhancement and unprecedented uniformity compared to traditional few-particle designs. This establishes a practical platform for next-generation sensing and quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how collective behavior in disordered media (plasmonics) can be modeled using statistical averaging principles to achieve robust performance, which has parallels in understanding complex condensed matter systems like spin glasses or amorphous materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving wafer-scale, uniform, and giant electromagnetic field enhancement in plasmonic nanostructures while overcoming the inherent trade-off between high-enhancement few-body designs (sensitive to flaws) and low-enhancement scalable methods.

**Main result.** By engineering a many-body plasmonic architecture using controlled dewetting, the system achieves high collective enhancement that surpasses optimized few-body systems while maintaining exceptional uniformity across large areas.

**Method.** The authors combine lithography-free, multi-step dewetting fabrication with advanced electromagnetic simulations (FDTD) and characterization techniques like SERS spectroscopy to validate the statistical averaging principle.

**Model / system.** The system is a plasmonic nanocluster formed by gold nanoparticles on a substrate. The model leverages statistical mechanics principles, treating the structure as approaching a continuum limit ($N \gg 1$) where local disorder averages out, enabling high performance despite geometric imperfections.

**Key observables.** Near-field enhancement ($	ext{NFE} |E/E_0|$), Surface-Enhanced Raman Scattering (SERS) enhancement factor, and the Relative Standard Deviation ($	ext{RSD}$) quantifying uniformity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of satellite nanoparticles ($N$), gap size, and the specific dewetting sequence used to control particle density are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that optical performance can be decoupled from precise nanoscale positioning by relying instead on the statistical consistency of nanoparticle distribution across the wafer.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of nanocluster formation via dewetting, compare SERS enhancement factors and RSD for different cluster architectures (e.g., 10-14-6 vs 10-8-8), and show simulated near-field profiles confirming high, uniform field localization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the scalability/enhancement trade-off to proposing a statistical mechanics-inspired many-body solution. It details the dewetting fabrication process, presents simulation results showing enhancement scaling with particle count, and validates uniformity using SERS measurements across wafer areas.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable plasmonic technologies face a critical trade-off: few-body architectures offer high enhancement but are sensitive to fabrication flaws, while scalable methods like solid-state dewetting yield large, low-enhancement gaps. We introduce a paradigm shift using a many-body plasmonic architecture inspired by statistical mechanics. By moving toward the continuum limit (N>>1), local geometric variations are statistically averaged out, effectively decoupling optical performance from microscopic disorder. We implement this concept via a lithography- and etching-free, multi-step dewetting strategy, creating wafer-scale nanoclusters. This process strategically forms a robust many-body system by introducing numerous small satellite nanoparticles between larger particles. Crucially, this design achieves a high collective enhancement that surpasses even optimized few-body systems, despite having larger individual gaps. Under optimized conditions, these substrates exhibit a surface-enhanced Raman scattering enhancement factor approaching 4 x 108 with unprecedented reproducibility (RSD of ~10%). This scalable, low-cost concept establishes a practical route toward reproducible wafer-scale nanophotonic platforms for sensing, spectroscopy, and quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a resource measure called stellar rank to analyze bosonic quantum error-correcting codes under finite preparation constraints. It demonstrates that these physical limitations force a trade-off between ideal code properties and achievable performance against noise like photon loss or dephasing. The findings establish stellar rank as a crucial, operationally relevant metric for designing robust continuous-variable quantum information protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous resource theory framework (stellar rank) for analyzing bosonic QEC performance in realistic settings where perfect state preparation is impossible, bridging abstract theory with practical quantum optics limitations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work addresses the practical challenge of designing bosonic quantum error-correcting (QEC) codes when limited by finite resources for preparing non-Gaussian states.

**Main result.** Finite stellar rank imposes a trade-off among state approximability, energy, and logical protection; optimized encodings reveal noise-adapted structures, establishing stellar rank as an operationally meaningful resource measure.

**Method.** The authors use stellar rank as a resource measure to benchmark bosonic codes (like Cat/GKP) under finite preparation constraints, optimizing directly within these constrained variational classes.

**Model / system.** The system involves continuous-variable bosonic modes used for QEC. The analysis considers noise channels such as photon loss and photon-number dephasing acting on encoded states.

**Key observables.** Stellar fidelity ($f^\star_k$), channel fidelity, Wigner functions of optimized codewords.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Stellar Rank ($k$), Photon Loss rate ($\kappa$), Dephasing strength ($\kappa_\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the optimal recovery map can be found, and the analysis relies on approximating ideal states using finite stellar rank resources.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how stellar fidelity increases with increasing stellar rank for Cat codes. Figure 9 shows Wigner functions of optimized states revealing grid-like or rotationally symmetric structures depending on the noise type and stellar rank.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem by noting that finite resources constrain ideal QEC codewords, then develops methods using stellar fidelity to quantify approximation quality, and finally optimizes encodings directly under these constraints to reveal noise-adapted code geometries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bosonic quantum error correction (QEC) relies on non-Gaussian bosonic encodings whose preparation cost is a central practical constraint. In this work, we use stellar rank as a resource measure to design and benchmark bosonic codes under finite non-Gaussian resources. For fixed cat and Gottesman--Kitaev--Preskill (GKP) code families, we show that finite stellar rank creates a trade-off among state approximability, energy, and logical protection under photon loss and photon-number dephasing, evaluated with optimal recovery. This trade-off implies that codewords with better ideal error-correction properties need not be optimal once finite-rank preparation constraints are imposed. Going beyond fixed-target codewords, we directly optimize bosonic encodings at fixed stellar rank, revealing noise-adapted code structures and concrete resource thresholds. Grid-like encodings emerge under photon loss, whereas approximately rotation-symmetric encodings arise under dephasing. In the optimized search, stellar rank k=2 suffices to surpass break-even for all dephasing strengths considered, while under photon loss the required rank increases with the loss rate. These results establish stellar rank as an operationally meaningful resource measure for bosonic QEC under practical state-preparation constraints.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel hybrid quantum-classical method, the CEFV, to represent floating-point numbers in quantum states while preserving high arithmetic precision. By classically tracking global information like range and tolerance, it significantly reduces error accumulation during repeated operations compared to purely quantum approaches. This advancement is crucial for implementing complex numerical algorithms on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on arithmetic circuits, the rigorous management of state representation fidelity and resource optimization (depth vs. accuracy) is highly relevant to designing complex quantum simulations for condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is mitigating precision degradation when performing repeated arithmetic operations on quantum floating-point variables while maintaining low circuit depth.

**Main result.** A hybrid quantum-classical representation, the Classically-Enriched Floating Point Variable (CEFV), was introduced, which significantly reduces approximation error compared to previous methods.

**Method.** The method combines a quantum register for values with a classical register storing global parameters like range and tolerance, allowing arithmetic operations (sum/product) to be defined while managing overflow and tracking precision loss.

**Model / system.** The model uses quantum registers to encode random variables, extending the concept of basis encoding to handle fractional numbers via the CEFV structure. It explicitly accounts for compatibility with potentially continuous random variables using tolerance parameters ($\epsilon_\pm$).

**Key observables.** Approximation error ($\epsilon_{	ext{approx}}^\pm$), circuit depth (measured in $[	ext{u}3, 	ext{cx}]$ basis), and the required number of output qubits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling factor ($b$) and offset are critical parameters that determine the achievable precision and the complexity of the arithmetic circuits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that global properties (like range or exponent) can be efficiently stored classically to guide quantum operations, and it relies on defining compatible error bounds ($\epsilon_\pm$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the CEFV structure mapping basis states to values; heat maps compare relative errors for addition based on scaling factor choices; CDFs show the benefit of using offsets during repeated summation; and other figures compare circuit depth and error reduction across different algorithmic implementations.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by first introducing the hybrid CEFV representation, defining compatible sum/product operations that manage overflow and tolerance propagation. It then rigorously analyzes the impact of scaling factors ($	ext{blead}$) and offsets on error accumulation, concluding with comparisons of circuit depth improvements from algorithmic refinements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

There are several possible ways to encode random variables in a quantum state. The basis encoding of bit strings has paramount importance because it allows to load the values of a random variable through the superposition of corresponding basis states, and to then exploit quantum parallelism in processing algorithms. The basis encoding offers a natural way to represent an unsigned integer random variable, and extends to signed integers, as well as to fixed-point and floating-point variables. Each quantum representation of fractional numbers, however, involves a trade-off between accuracy and depth of manipulation circuits. Here, an efficient hybrid quantum-classical representation of quantum floating points is introduced. It combines a quantum register containing the values, with a classical register storing global information about the variable, namely the range and approximation tolerances. The sum and product operations are defined, in such a way as to ensure they are performed without overflow. By taking advantage of the stored classical information, the precision degradation that occurs due to rounding after repeated data manipulations, can be significantly reduced compared to known strategies. Ad hoc examples show up to around $90\%$ reduction in approximation, compared to previous techniques, after repeated additions. The method finds application in many algorithms of practical relevance and constitutes a significant advance in the design of arithmetic circuits with low depth and high accuracy.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the Emery model for cuprates to understand what drives high-temperature superconductivity. Using advanced quantum many-body theory (CDMFT), the authors find that $T_c$ is primarily controlled by how much charge resides on the oxygen sites (oxygen hole content) and the size of the charge-transfer gap. This provides a crucial theoretical map for understanding cuprate physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection drawn between model parameters (like charge gap) and macroscopic properties ($T_c$) provides a concrete theoretical framework that can be tested against experimental measurements, including those from ultracold atoms simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the fundamental mechanisms controlling the superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$) trends observed in high-temperature cuprate superconductors.

**Main result.** The maximum $T_c$ is controlled by two key electronic structure parameters: the charge-transfer gap size ($\Delta_I$) and the oxygen hole content, with the latter being identified as the dominant variable.

**Method.** The research employs cellular dynamical mean-field theory (CDMFT) to solve the model Hamiltonian at finite temperature, guided by the Zaanen-Sawatzky-Allen diagram.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the Emery model, which describes the copper-oxygen plane in cuprates. This model incorporates onsite repulsion ($U_d$) on Cu and hopping terms between Cu and O orbitals.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_{max}^c$), charge-transfer gap size ($\Delta_I$), and oxygen hole content.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Copper-oxygen energy distance ($\Delta$), onsite repulsion on Cu orbital ($U_d$), and the doping level (related to hole content).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the charge-transfer insulating regime ($U_d > \Delta$) and utilizes CDMFT approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Zaanen-Sawatzky-Allen diagram mapping out constant lines for $\Delta_I$ and oxygen hole content, alongside isotherms used to determine the gap size.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using the Emery model and CDMFT. It then systematically maps the parameter space using the Zaanen-Sawatzky-Allen diagram to quantify how $T_c^{m max}$ depends on $\Delta$ and $U_d$, culminating in identifying the roles of gap size and hole content.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Investigating the drivers of superconducting critical temperature trends in cuprates is crucial for uncovering the mechanism of high-temperature superconductivity. Here we study this problem in the canonical model of the copper-oxygen plane, the Emery model, with cellular dynamical mean-field theory. Using the Zaanen-Sawatzky-Allen diagram as a guiding framework, we systematically quantify how the maximum superconducting critical temperature $T_c^{\rm max}$ depends on the copper-oxygen energy distance and on the local repulsion on the copper orbital. Unexpectedly, $T_c^{\rm max}$ is optimized not only near the charge-transfer insulator to metal boundary, consistent with previous findings, but also deep in the charge-transfer regime, revealing an unexplored mechanism. Then we link model parameters to physical observables, identifying the charge-transfer gap size and the oxygen hole content as two mechanisms controlling $T_c^{\rm max}$. $T_c^{\rm max}$ increases monotonically as the oxygen hole content increases and the charge gap size decreases. The oxygen hole content is the dominant variable in varying $T_c^{\rm max}$. Our work provides predictions for proposed realizations of the Emery model with ultracold atoms and a theoretical framework for understanding key experimental trends in hole-doped cuprates.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an adaptive, hardware-aware decoding framework for the Rotated Surface Code designed to overcome the bottleneck of real-time QEC. It uses a fast neural network path supplemented by a computationally intensive MWPM refinement stage only when necessary. This approach significantly boosts logical accuracy while maintaining high throughput suitable for scaling quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the operational feasibility of fault-tolerant quantum computing by optimizing the classical post-processing step (decoding) to meet stringent real-time latency requirements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Real-time decoding is a major bottleneck preventing the scaling of Quantum Error Correction (QEC) from NISQ devices to fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** The adaptive framework significantly improves logical accuracy (e.g., 99.21% to 99.81%) by selectively routing only low-confidence syndromes to a computationally expensive refinement stage, with minimal overhead.

**Method.** It proposes an adaptive, two-stage decoding inference: a fast path using a lightweight neural network for most measurements, and a refinement stage using Minimum-Weight Perfect Matching (MWPM) for uncertain cases.

**Model / system.** The work is implemented on the Rotated Surface Code, simulating performance under circuit-level depolarizing noise across various code distances ($d=3$ to $11$).

**Key observables.** Logical accuracy, confidence-controlled accuracy–latency trade-offs, decoding throughput (up to $4.6 	imes 10^5$ samples s$^{-1}$), and per-shot latency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distances ($d$), physical error rates ($p$), and the adaptive confidence threshold ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantitative results are limited to the rotated surface code family and independent circuit-level depolarizing noise, with no generalization claimed for correlated or coherent noise.

**Figures summary.** Tables summarize joint performance metrics (accuracy, error rate, runtime, throughput) across a grid of code distances and noise probabilities; Figure 1 outlines the overall hardware-aware co-design framework.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the bottleneck problem, details the adaptive neural/MWPM decoding methodology, presents comprehensive benchmarking results across multiple parameters (Table 10), and concludes by outlining future architectural directions beyond the current scope.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Real-time decoding is a major bottleneck in scaling quantum error correction (QEC) from noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices to fault-tolerant quantum computing. We present an adaptive confidence-gated decoding framework for the rotated surface code that treats decoding as a two-stage inference problem. A lightweight feed-forward neural network performs fast-path decoding for the majority of syndrome measurements, while only low-confidence predictions are escalated to a minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM) refinement stage. We benchmark the framework on rotated surface codes with distances $d \in \{3,5,7,9,11\}$ under circuit-level depolarising noise using the Stim stabiliser simulator. The evaluation characterises logical accuracy, confidence-controlled accuracy-latency trade-offs, decoding throughput, per-shot latency, and decoding-graph resource scaling. Routing only 3.3%-6.2% of syndromes to the refinement stage improves logical accuracy from 99.21% for the neural-only baseline to 99.81% at a confidence threshold of 0.95 while incurring only a bounded increase in average decoding cost. Neural-decoder throughput saturates near $4.6 \times 10^{5}$ samples s$^{-1}$ at batch size 512 on commodity CPU hardware, indicating that the neural fast path is not the dominant throughput bottleneck beyond code distance $d=7$. We release the complete benchmarking pipeline, trained models, raw benchmark data, and source code, and explicitly distinguish the experimentally validated contributions from the broader hardware-aware QEC co-design roadmap, including hardware-constrained code discovery, GPU-accelerated inference, and multi-noise optimisation, which remain directions for future work.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously analyzes the performance limits of an initialization-free variant of the Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm. By deriving explicit formulas based on initial state coherence, the authors prove that this IF-BV method generally surpasses the standard BV algorithm. The findings provide deep insights into which quantum resources are essential for achieving optimal computational success probabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical characterization of quantum advantage in specific quantum algorithms, particularly how modifying initial state preparation can boost computational power, is highly relevant to understanding resource theories in quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the performance of an initialization-free variant of the Bernstein-Vazirani (BV) quantum algorithm compared to its standard probabilistic counterpart.

**Main result.** It derives explicit formulas showing that the IF-BV algorithm generally outperforms the standard BV algorithm, and it establishes necessary and sufficient conditions for achieving maximal success probability.

**Method.** The analysis employs techniques from quantum channel discrimination theory, utilizing Semidefinite Programming (SDP) characterization to derive performance bounds based on initial state coherence properties.

**Model / system.** The model is the probabilistic IF-BV algorithm applied to an oracle problem, considering pure initial states $|\muangle = \alpha |+angle|\phi_0angle + eta |-angle|\phi_1angle$ for the ancilla register.

**Key observables.** Performance ($P_W$) of the IF-BV algorithm and its comparison to the standard BV performance ($P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coefficients $\alpha, eta$, and the coherence properties (Robustness of Coherence $R$) of the initial states $|\phi_0angle$ and $|\phi_1angle$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on pure initial states for deriving closed-form expressions; comparisons often rely on a specific relabeling convention for the standard BV algorithm characterization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the IF-BV performance, deriving explicit formulas relating it to coherence (Chunk 2), then establishing necessary and sufficient conditions for maximal performance (Theorem 6), and finally proving that IF-BV generally outperforms standard BV under certain assumptions (Theorem 8).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Naseri et al. [Phys. Rev. A 106, 062429 (2022); arXiv:2205.13610] studied which quantum resources in initial states are essential for the probabilistic Bernstein-Vazirani (BV) algorithm, defining its performance as the optimal average success probability over all measurements. In this work, we consider a variant of BV algorithm, which is called the initialization-free (IF) BV algorithm, in which an arbitrary ancilla state as the oracle register is allowed, to improve the performance. We derive an explicit formula for the performance of the probabilistic IF-BV algorithm and obtain a necessary and sufficient condition for an initial state to achieve maximal performance. We further prove that, under a suitable ordering assumption on the coefficients of the initial state, the probabilistic IF-BV algorithm outperforms the standard probabilistic BV algorithm.

</details>

**Authors:** Guang Lin, Qibin Zhao  
**Type:** both · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.06058v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces SQGen, a novel quantum generator that uses a Quantized Tensor Train structure enhanced by latent modulation to generate images directly on quantum hardware. By training classically and ensuring the inference path is purely quantum, it overcomes major hurdles like decoder dependency and circuit depth limitations for end-to-end quantum machine learning.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on image generation, the rigorous use of tensor network structures (QTT) combined with advanced conditioning techniques (latent modulation) provides a powerful framework for modeling complex quantum correlations in structured physical systems beyond simple state preparation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is generating high-fidelity, image-scale data directly from quantum circuits on NISQ hardware without relying on a classical decoder to maintain end-to-end quantum advantage.

**Main result.** SQGen successfully trains stably and generates images end-to-end using a shallow circuit structure that incorporates latent modulation, achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction quality metrics.

**Method.** The authors propose a full quantum generator built on a Quantized Tensor Train (QTT) skeleton, enhanced with latent modulation to condition the generation process. Training is performed classically by optimizing a differentiable model compatible with native quantum gates.

**Model / system.** The model uses a QTT structure where bond indices are mapped onto ancilla qubits. Latent variables are injected into rotation angles via an exact additive factorization, allowing for structured conditioning while keeping the inference path purely quantum.

**Key observables.** Per-sample reconstruction quality (KL divergence and L1 distance), sample diversity, and circuit complexity metrics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond rank ($\chi$), latent dimension ($d$), noise strength ($p$), and the torus prior $	ext{T}^d$ for the latent variable $z$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes that the quantum process can be accurately represented by a QTT structure, and it relies on classical training to establish parameters that map one-to-one onto native quantum gates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SQGen architecture using latent modulation within the QTT skeleton; comparison tables (e.g., Table 3) benchmark performance against various quantum image generation families.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the novel QTT structure and latent modulation mechanism, describes the classical training pipeline with a torus prior, and validates the end-to-end process through extensive numerical experiments on MNIST and synthetic data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Generating images directly from quantum systems is an attractive but unresolved goal on NISQ hardware. Existing quantum generators face several coupled obstacles: barren plateaus that block trainability, expensive quantum circuit preparation, and hardware noise that erodes quantum information with depth. A further difficulty is producing image-scale output without a classical decoder, whose use would otherwise break the end-to-end quantum advantage. We propose SQGen, a full quantum generator built on a quantized tensor train (QTT) with a latent modulation architecture. Specifically, SQGen promotes the QTT bond index of the target pixel distribution to ancilla bond qubits, so that each circuit site operates locally on a bond register plus the two physical qubits that carry the row- and column-bit of one image scale. We further introduce latent modulation: each re-uploading rotation is factorized at the angle level into a trainable main path plus an additive latent term, reducing to the trainable main path when the latent term is disabled. During training, we create a differentiable model in the classical system under gate-compatibility constraints, with a torus prior as the latent distribution. After training, every operator maps one-to-one to a native quantum gate, yielding a compact, deployable quantum circuit with no classical decoder in the inference path. Together, these design choices address the obstacles raised above. Extensive experiments on image datasets and synthetic data demonstrate that SQGen trains stably, generates images end-to-end from a shallow circuit with no classical decoder, and shows promising feasibility on real quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes Tile Codes for fault-tolerant quantum computation on 2D lattices, focusing on implementing syndrome extraction using only local interactions. By developing optimized routing schemes, they show that while locality imposes a threshold penalty, the resulting codes offer superior qubit efficiency compared to surface codes at low physical error rates. The work validates planar qLDPC codes as a viable alternative for near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, hardware-aware comparisons of quantum error correcting codes, directly addressing the practical constraints (like nearest-neighbor connectivity) that limit theoretical proposals in superconducting or solid-state quantum computing platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop and analyze strictly local Tile-Code Architectures for fault-tolerant quantum computation on 2D planar lattices, seeking to improve encoding efficiency over the surface code.

**Main result.** Routing syndrome extraction circuits significantly reduces the error threshold of tile codes when nearest-neighbor connectivity is enforced. Furthermore, routed tile codes become more qubit-efficient than the surface code below a critical physical error rate ($p^* \approx 0.08\%$).

**Method.** The authors developed an exhaustive search algorithm for SWAP-based routing schemes to implement syndrome extraction circuits and compared performance using various decoding algorithms like BP+OSD.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Tile Codes, a family of planar quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes, implemented on a two-dimensional square lattice. The analysis involves simulating error correction under the SI1000 noise model and standard depolarizing noise.

**Key observables.** Circuit-level thresholds ($p_{th}$), resource footprint (physical qubits per logical qubit), and logical error rate ($p_L$) vs. physical error rate ($p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The encoding efficiency $kd^2/n$, the crossover physical error rate $p^* \approx 0.08\%$, and the noise model parameters (SI1000).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that SWAP gates are native operations, and optimal routing schedules are found within a specific class of uniform SWAP direction assignments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate code constructions on lattices, compare $p_L$ vs. $p$ for different decoders (e.g., BP+OSD vs. others), and show the resource efficiency comparison between routed tile codes and surface codes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the local architecture and developing routing schemes; it then analyzes the resulting circuit-level thresholds under noise models; finally, it compares decoding performance across multiple algorithms and assesses qubit resource efficiency against established codes like the surface code.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tile codes are a family of planar quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes with weight-6 stabilizers and open boundary conditions, offering an encoding efficiency $kd^2/n$ of up to four times that of the surface code. In this work, we develop an exhaustive search algorithm for finding SWAP-based routing schemes that implement syndrome extraction for four tile-code families using only nearest-neighbor interactions on a two-dimensional square lattice, matching the connectivity of the surface code. Using explicitly constructed routed syndrome-extraction circuits decoded with BP+OSD, we estimate the circuit-level thresholds of these code families. For the SI1000 noise model, the threshold without such a connectivity constraint is obtained in a range 0.23%-0.31%, while it decreases to 0.11%-0.13% with routing, representing a reduction factor of around two to three. Despite this threshold penalty, our resource-footprint analysis shows that routed tile codes require fewer physical qubits per logical qubit than the surface code at sufficiently low physical error rates: Under the SI1000 noise model, we find a crossover near $p^*\approx 0.08\%$, below which routed tile codes become more qubit-efficient, with an advantage that grows monotonically as the physical error rate decreases.

</details>

### [Universal quantum cloning beyond noncontextual theory](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05959v1)

**Authors:** Min Namkung, Hyang-Tag Lim  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.05959v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that universal quantum cloning is fundamentally contextual. By contrasting the process with an idealized 'noncontextual theory,' the authors show that any attempt to model cloning using non-contextual probabilities leads to mathematical contradictions or trivial, meaningless results. This highlights a deep structural difference between quantum mechanics and classical probability theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a foundational test case for quantum theory's non-classical features, showing how fundamental processes like state copying necessitate contextuality when viewed through the lens of classical probability models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to theoretically prove that universal quantum cloning, which is possible in standard quantum mechanics, cannot be replicated within the framework of a noncontextual theory.

**Main result.** Universal quantum cloning exhibits an intrinsically contextual nature, as demonstrated by mathematical contradictions arising when assuming its existence in a noncontextual model. Specifically, attempts to model it yield only maximally mixed partial states or violate necessary conditions for preserving distinguishability (confusability).

**Method.** The analysis involves comparing the unitary operations of quantum mechanics with analogous transformations derived from an operational statistical model called 'noncontextual theory.' Key mathematical tools include analyzing 'confusability' and deriving constraints on these noncontextual transformations.

**Model / system.** The system under study is the process of cloning unknown quantum states (qubits), specifically examining $N 	o M$ scenarios. The theoretical comparison is between standard quantum mechanics and an operational statistical model that assumes context-independent probabilities.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($f$), Confusability ($c_{\psi, e\psi}$), and the resulting partial states after cloning attempts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of input to output copies ($N 	o M$) and the fidelity parameter $f$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that if a process cannot be described by preserving 'confusability' within the noncontextual framework, it must be fundamentally contextual.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually compares deterministic universal cloning in quantum theory (unitary $\hat{U}$) versus noncontextual theory ($L$). Figure 2 illustrates postselection-based universal cloning in the noncontextual model.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing the theoretical framework of noncontextuality, then applying this framework to the $1 	o 2$ and general $N 	o M$ cloning scenarios. It uses specific mathematical calculations involving confusability preservation conditions to derive contradictions that prove contextuality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum theory fundamentally forbids the perfect copying of an arbitrary unknown quantum state, according to a principle known as the no-cloning theorem. Nevertheless, it is possible to construct a deterministic quantum map that produces multiple approximate copies of an unknown quantum state. This task is referred to as universal quantum cloning, further facilitating numerous quantum technologies such as quantum cryptography and quantum communication. In this work, we theoretically verify that the universal quantum cloning cannot be realized within a noncontextual theory, highlighting its intrinsically nonclassical nature. Our verification first {focuses on revealing that} $1\rightarrow2$ cloning scenario {is fully contextual}, and {further covers general examples to observe the contextual behavior of} $N\rightarrow M$ scenario. We believe that our results regarding quantum cloning serve a key role for understanding both quantum foundation and application.

</details>

**Authors:** Jordan Baglo, Quentin Barthélemy, Étienne Lefrançois, Anne Forget, Dorothée Colson, Cyril Proust, Louis Taillefer  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.06332v1>

**Summary.** This work measures the low-temperature thermal conductivity in the underdoped cuprate $	ext{HgBa}_2	ext{CuO}_{4+\delta}$ to probe its nodal quasiparticle structure. By analyzing the field dependence of the residual thermal conductivity, the authors extract a large velocity ratio ($v_F/v_\Delta$). This result strongly suggests that the Fermi surface is more complex than previously assumed, likely involving multiple electronic pockets contributing to the heat transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation of thermal transport provides a highly sensitive probe into the gap structure and electronic dimensionality of strongly correlated materials like cuprates, offering insights complementary to ARPES or quantum oscillation measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate anomalous properties of high-Tc cuprate superconductors, specifically determining the nodal quasiparticle velocity ratio ($v_F / v_\Delta$) using thermal conductivity measurements.

**Main result.** The measured residual linear thermal conductivity ($\kappa_0/T$) yields an anomalously large quasiparticle velocity ratio ($v_F / v_\Delta = 23 \pm 3$), suggesting the presence of more than one Fermi surface sheet crossing the nodal line.

**Method.** Measuring in-plane longitudinal thermal conductivity ($\kappa$) at very low temperatures and extrapolating the field dependence using the K¨ubert-Hirschfeld model to extract the zero-field residual term $\kappa_0/T$.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the single-layer cuprate superconductor $	ext{HgBa}_2	ext{CuO}_{4+\delta}$ ($	ext{Hg1201}$). The analysis uses theoretical models for nodal d-wave superconductors relating $\kappa_0/T$ to the ratio of Fermi and gap velocities.

**Key observables.** In-plane longitudinal thermal conductivity ($\kappa$), the residual linear term $\kappa_0(B)/T$, and the velocity ratio $v_F / v_\Delta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T_c = 76 	ext{ K}$, doping $p = 0.11$, low temperatures (down to $0.1 	ext{ K}$), magnetic field dependence ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes contributions from only a single line node per quadrant when calculating the velocity ratio, though this assumption is challenged by other experimental results.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $\kappa/T$ vs. $T$ at various magnetic fields, and the extrapolation of $\kappa_0(B)/T$ vs. $B$, which are fitted to theoretical models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system and measurement technique (thermal conductivity). It applies a theoretical model relating $\kappa_0/T$ to nodal properties, derives an anomalously large velocity ratio from the data, and concludes by suggesting that this implies multiple Fermi surface sheets are present in the material.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The single-layer cuprate superconductor HgBa$_{2}$CuO$_{4+δ}$ (Hg1201) is an ideal candidate for investigating many properties of cuprates with minimal disorder and without the complication of multiple CuO$_2$ layers. Here we measure the in-plane longitudinal thermal conductivity $κ$ of underdoped Hg1201 ($T_c$ = 76 K, $p$ = 0.11) at dilution refrigerator temperatures to extract the nodal quasiparticle velocity ratio $v_F/v_Δ$. Assuming contributions from only a single line node per quadrant on the Fermi surface leads to a value of $v_F/v_Δ$ = $23 \pm 3$, anomalously large compared to other cuprates at similar dopings. In conjunction with the anomalously high quasiparticle specific heat of Hg1201 in the normal state reported previously at a similar doping, this points to more than one Fermi surface sheet crossing the nodal line, suggesting the presence of more than the single small electron pocket detected by quantum oscillations.

</details>

### [Complete local expansion of the availability function in random sequential adsorption of aligned squares at low density: Termination at fourth order](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05634v1)

**Authors:** F. Tolea, M. Tolea  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.05634v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

**Summary.** This paper provides a detailed theoretical derivation for the low-density behavior of the availability function during Random Sequential Adsorption (RSA) of squares. By employing an inclusion-exclusion expansion based on geometric overlaps, the authors derive the polynomial coefficients up to fourth order. The work is significant because it establishes the exact local overlap hierarchy for this physical process and validates its results against numerical simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical packing problems, the rigorous application of inclusion-exclusion principles to calculate system properties based on local geometric constraints is a powerful technique applicable to understanding correlation functions in many-body quantum systems or open quantum dynamics where local approximations are used.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors derive the low-coverage expansion of the availability function, $\alpha(q)$, for Random Sequential Adsorption (RSA) of aligned squares.

**Main result.** They establish that this local overlap hierarchy terminates exactly at fourth order due to geometric constraints, yielding a complete polynomial approximation for low densities.

**Method.** The derivation systematically uses the inclusion-exclusion principle applied to the overlapping exclusion regions created by deposited squares.

**Model / system.** The model is Random Sequential Adsorption (RSA) of aligned squares on a surface or lattice. The process is irreversible, leading to a jammed state characterized by coverage $q$.

**Key observables.** The availability function $\alpha(q)$, which measures the fraction of positions available for an additional square insertion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-density regime (low coverage $q$), and the geometric overlap structure involving up to four squares.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the low-density limit, assuming that only local overlaps (up to four bodies) contribute significantly to the expansion coefficients.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the exclusion zone of a single square and show how the overlap regions for two and three squares are geometrically accounted for in the inclusion-exclusion corrections.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the expansion $\alpha(q)$ term by term, starting with the first order (single block), then systematically correcting for double overlaps ($C_2$), triple overlaps ($C_3$), and finally confirming the termination at fourth order ($C_4$) using both analytical geometry and numerical simulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider random sequential adsorption (RSA) of aligned squares and derive the low-coverage expansion of the availability function alpha(q), the fraction of positions accessible to an additional square, up to fourth order in the coverage q. At low coverage, the reduction of available space can be understood in terms of geometric overlap between exclusion regions created by previously deposited squares. A single square blocks a finite area; pairs of squares may have overlapping exclusion zones, reducing the total blocked area; similarly, three and four squares can share a common overlap region, leading to higher-order corrections. These contributions can be systematically accounted for through an inclusion-exclusion expansion based on the geometry of overlapping exclusion regions, with alternating signs dictated by inclusion-exclusion. The expansion terminates exactly at fourth order, since no more than four deposited squares can simultaneously overlap the exclusion region of a trial insertion. The coefficients are obtained by explicit enumeration of all such geometrically admissible configurations and are further confirmed by numerical simulations on a discrete lattice, showing agreement within statistical uncertainty.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an exact theoretical framework using dimensional reduction to analyze interacting Fermi liquids with forward scattering. By mapping the problem onto solvable 1D bosonic theories, the authors show that while single-particle physics remains stable, many-body instabilities are enhanced by interactions, predicting specific superconducting or density wave instabilities in certain regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of dimensional reduction and bosonization techniques to study correlation functions in higher dimensions provides powerful analytical tools applicable to strongly correlated electron models where mean-field or simple RPA approaches fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates the instabilities of Fermi liquids when subjected to arbitrary forward-scattering density-density interactions in D dimensions.

**Main result.** While single-particle properties remain Fermi-liquid-like, many-body channels are significantly dressed by finite-range interactions, leading to singular power-law scaling for relevant susceptibilities at low temperatures. Stable long-range order is generally not possible from forward scattering alone.

**Method.** The authors employ a dimensional reduction technique, mapping D-dimensional correlation functions onto effective 1D correlators which are then evaluated exactly using a 1D bosonizable (Gaussian) theory.

**Model / system.** The system is an N-fold degenerate D-dimensional electron gas with a spherical Fermi surface, interacting via small momentum transfer forward-scattering interactions. The analysis extends to finite-range interactions ($R_{\mathrm{s}} \gg 1/k_{\mathrm{F}}$).

**Key observables.** Fermion Green function, pair susceptibility ($\chi_{2e}$), charge/flavor susceptibilities ($\chi_{2k_F}$, $\chi_{2N k_F}$), and the single-particle spectral function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T 	o 0$), interaction range ($R_{\mathrm{s}}$), dimensionality ($D$), and degeneracy ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on dimensional reduction techniques equivalent to Haldane patch construction, assuming a spherical Fermi surface, and performing calculations in the leading order of $(kR_s)^{-1}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically applies dimensional reduction to map D-dimensional correlation functions onto 1D bosonizable theories. It first confirms single-particle Fermi liquid behavior, then analyzes how many-body susceptibilities are dressed by interactions, concluding with specific predictions for composite instabilities like $4e$ superconductivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we consider $N$-fold degenerate $D$-dimensional electron gas with spherical Fermi surface and arbitrary forward-scattering density-density interaction transferring small momentum compared to the Fermi momentum $k_{\mathrm{F}}$. The dimensional reduction that is mathematically equivalent to the Haldane patch construction and similar multidimensional bosonization techniques, provides a natural map of two-point $D$-dimensional correlation functions (fermion Green function, susceptibilities etc.) onto effective one-dimensional (1D) correlators with the same diagrammatic structure, which can be evaluated exactly within a 1D bosonizable (Gaussian) theory. We then apply this formalism to evaluate the fermion Green function, pair and charge/flavor susceptibilities, as well as the composite correlation functions for the case of a finite-range interaction, where the interaction range $R_{\mathrm{s}} \gg 1/k_{\mathrm{F}}$ is large compared to the Fermi wavelength. First, we find that the single-particle spectral function remains Fermi-liquid-like which is fully consistent with the previous research. In contrast to the single-particle sector, the many-body channels are efficiently dressed by finite-range interactions, and this dressing is fully equivalent to the one-loop renormalization group (RG), which is also in line with previous multidimensional bosonization results. Within the forward-scattering model, stable long-range order is not possible, and relevant susceptibilities demonstrate singular power-law scaling with temperature $T$ at $T \to 0$. The rest of the abstract is in the PDF.

</details>

**Summary.** This review bridges the gap between quantum statistical mechanics and analytic number theory by showing that the density of states for many-body quantum systems mathematically mirrors the counting function for integer partitions. Using semiclassical methods like trace formulas and saddle-point approximations, the authors derive asymptotic formulas connecting these two seemingly disparate fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful mathematical framework showing how fundamental concepts in quantum statistical mechanics (like energy distribution) can yield deep insights into pure number theory problems, suggesting underlying physical principles govern arithmetic structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper explores a deep mathematical analogy between calculating the quantum density of states for many-body systems and determining the number of ways an integer can be partitioned into smaller integers.

**Main result.** It establishes that the asymptotic, smooth part of the quantum level density reproduces the known formulas for integer partitions (like the Hardy-Ramanujan formula), while specific oscillations relate to classical periodic orbits.

**Method.** The primary method involves using semiclassical trace formulas and saddle-point approximations on partition functions derived from statistical mechanics principles.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers non-interacting quantum systems whose energy levels dictate the density of states. The mathematical model is generalized to include various statistics (Bose, Fermi) and specific spectral forms, such as those leading to prime partitions.

**Key observables.** Quantum Density of States ($ho(E)$), Number of Partitions ($P(n)$), Entropy ($S$), and the smooth/oscillating parts of $ho(E)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (or inverse temperature $eta$), Energy $E=n$, Spectral exponent $s$, and occupancy parameter $k$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies heavily on semiclassical approximations, such as the saddle-point method, to extract asymptotic behavior from complex sums.

**Figures summary.** Figure 22 compares the exact partition function count ($P(1)_k(n)$) with its derived asymptotic density of states ($ho(1)_k(E)$) for a specific spectral case.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the connection via single-particle spectra and trace formulas, then extending this to many-body systems using partition functions, and finally applying these techniques to derive advanced asymptotic results for prime partitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this review we discuss semi-classical methods that are traditionally used to describe many-body systems in physics, but may also be used to describe partitions of integers in analytic number theory. Specifically, we explore the connection between the methods of statistical mechanics and number partitions. Though the two fields appear very different, their fundamental issues bear a close resemblance. In the former case it is the distribution of a given amount of energy among the particles in an ensemble at a given temperature with well defined properties, while in the latter case it is the way an integer is partitioned into other integers, with or without restrictions. We begin with a discussion of the single-particle quantum density of states, also called the level density, in which we illustrate the connection between the density of states and the classical periodic orbits through the semiclassical trace formula. This is then extended to many particle systems. We show that the asymptotic number partition is reproduced by the average (smooth) part of the level density at discrete integer values of the argument. In the especially interesting case of distinct square partitions, pronounced oscillations are well reproduced by the periodic orbit theory in terms of a few orbits characterised by Pythagorean number triples. We speculate on the connection to Fermat's theorem as to why such regular oscillations (though vanishing asymptotically) exist only in this special case. Finally, we discuss some new results for integer partitions of primes, both unrestricted and distinct.

</details>
