**Summary.** The paper investigates if quantum mechanics can be formulated purely over real numbers while remaining empirically equivalent to standard complex QM. It demonstrates that achieving this equivalence necessitates imposing strong constraints, such as gauge invariance and a specialized 'balanced' tensor product rule. Ultimately, the authors argue that the theory is mathematically equivalent to complex QM written in a redundant real notation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses foundational questions about the mathematical structure of quantum theory, which underpins all applications in AMO physics and quantum information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is whether complex numbers are fundamentally necessary for quantum mechanics, or if a formulation over real numbers ($\mathbb{R}$) can be empirically equivalent.

**Main result.** The authors conclude that any operational equivalence to standard complex QM requires imposing constraints (like gauge invariance and specific composition rules) such that the theory is best understood as complex QM written in a redundant real notation, not an independent real-amplitude theory.

**Method.** The analysis involves comparing predictions from quotient-space constructions over $\mathbb{R}$ against established results from standard complex quantum mechanics ($\mathbb{C}$) using algebraic diagnostics like tensor product structures and gauge invariance requirements.

**Model / system.** The model is the mathematical structure of quantum theory itself, specifically analyzing state spaces formulated over real Hilbert spaces versus those naturally defined by complex amplitudes. The focus is on single-system states and composite systems (e.g., in network scenarios).

**Key observables.** Physical effects ($\hat{E}$) must preserve an $SO(2)$ gauge generated by the operator $\hat{J}$; violation of source independence under ordinary tensor products.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure is governed by the distinguished real linear operator $\hat{J}$ such that $\hat{J}^2 = -\mathbb{1}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Equivalence requires assuming that all physical dynamics and measurements preserve the $SO(2)$ gauge generated by $\hat{J}$, which restricts the observable algebra.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference comparisons of dimensions ($	ext{dim}_{\mathbb{R}}$) and numerical violations in Bell-network tests comparing standard vs. quotient factorization.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares various real formulations (including those based on quotients) against complex QM predictions, establishing necessary constraints—such as superselection rules and the balanced tensor product ($\otimes_B$)—to achieve empirical equivalence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Barrios Hita et al. [Phys. Rev. Lett. $\bf{136}$, 240202 (2026)] argued that quantum mechanics can be formulated over the real numbers by replacing the tensor-product postulate with a quotient-space construction, and concluded that complex numbers are therefore a matter of convenience. We show that the operational content of this construction is not that of a generic real Hilbert-space theory. Empirical equivalence requires a distinguished real linear operator $J$ with $J^2 = -\mathbb{1}$, and all physical effects, instruments, and dynamics must preserve the corresponding $SO(2)$ gauge. Moreover, the composite-system rule is a balanced tensor product over this hidden complex structure, not the ordinary tensor product over $\mathbb{R}$. In multipartite network scenarios, this changes the meaning of source independence: canonical real representatives are not source-factorizable in the usual tensor-product sense. Thus, the construction is best understood as standard complex quantum mechanics written in real notation, not as an independent real-amplitude theory. This clarifies what is, and is not, excluded by experiments testing the necessity of complex numbers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel architectural modification to the IPv6 protocol stack to facilitate quantum networking functions, such as teleportation and path superposition. By utilizing unused features like Extension Headers, it outlines a pathway for integrating quantum information transfer into existing classical internet infrastructure. The work provides a theoretical blueprint for building the Quantum Internet at the network layer.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on network protocols, the theoretical framework of encoding complex quantum operations (like superposition) into structured data packets is highly relevant for understanding the physical implementation challenges of distributed quantum computation and communication architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is designing a modified Internet Protocol (IP) packet format to enable quantum teleportation, quantum routing (superposition of routes), and superpositions of these processes within the emerging Quantum Internet.

**Main result.** The authors propose an architecture utilizing IPv6 Extension Headers to incorporate quantum functionality at the network layer, enabling path superposition while maintaining a minimalist approach to standard alterations.

**Method.** The methodology involves adapting the layered structure of the OSI model by proposing new 'Quantum Extension Headers' for IPv6 that carry instructions necessary for quantum operations like teleportation and routing superposition.

**Model / system.** The system model is based on modifying the IPv6 packet format (Layer 3) to create a hybrid classical-quantum data stream. It assumes the availability of quantum memory at routers and models information arrival sequentially: classical header first, followed by quantum payload.

**Key observables.** Quantum teleportation success, path superposition outcomes, and the successful transmission of quantum states via modified IP headers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The use of IPv6 Extension Headers; the requirement that no router action destroys a requested path superposition (avoiding 'which-way' information leakage).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes quantum memory is available on all routers, and that information arrives sequentially in pulses (classical header followed by quantum data). The design is explicitly stated as non-optimal.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a system model demonstrating path superposition using nodes A, B, C, and D. Figure 2 shows the Quantum IP Stack for direct transmission synchronization. Figure 3 proposes the structure of a new IPv6 Extension Header for 'Quantum Routing' to support path superposition.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon existing networking standards by proposing modifications at the IP layer (IPv6). It details the necessary header additions ('Quantum Routing' Header) and outlines how quantum processes like teleportation are scheduled relative to classical control data within this extended stack.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a new design for quantum packet routing within the emerging Quantum Internet, highlighting how a little-used feature of Internet Protocol Version 6 (IPv6), namely Extension Headers, can lead to a significant amount of quantumness within the IP layer. Taking a minimalist approach to alterations of established standards, we outline the changes required in order for quantum teleportation, quantum routing, and superpositions of these processes to be enabled. Relative to other proposals for routing within the Quantum Internet, the architecture we propose enables a wider range of outcomes allowed by quantum mechanics. We do not claim any optimally in our design, but rather a pathway to invoke new quantum routing outcomes via small additions to the current IPv6.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a systematic framework to encode the Module Learning With Errors (MLWE) problem into a QUBO model solvable by quantum annealers. It analyzes the robustness of this encoding by linking geometric stability regions in the solution space to the energy gap of the resulting optimization landscape. The work establishes a rigorous theoretical basis for studying lattice problems on quantum hardware, while cautioning about current scaling limitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO physics, this work is highly relevant to quantum computation theory as it provides a structured method for translating hard mathematical problems into physical optimization models suitable for near-term quantum hardware benchmarking.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work develops a framework to encode Module Learning With Errors (MLWE) problems, central to post-quantum cryptography, into Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) models suitable for quantum annealing.

**Main result.** The methodology successfully maps MLWE instances to QUBOs, allowing the joint recovery of secret coefficients and error vectors. It establishes a formal link between cryptographic hardness, geometric stability regions, and the energy gap in the optimization landscape.

**Method.** The core method involves formulating the minimization problem as minimizing a quadratic objective function, which is then mapped onto the Hamiltonian structure of a quantum annealer (QUBO). Stability is analyzed using both geometric polytope definitions and the energy gap between the ground state and first excited state.

**Model / system.** The model is based on lattice-based cryptography problems (MLWE) solved via optimization. The physical system utilized for analysis is Quantum Annealing, which seeks the minimum energy configuration of a QUBO Hamiltonian ($\min \mathbf{x}^	op Q\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{c}^	op \mathbf{x}$).

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the QUBO energy gap ($\Delta E$), the geometric stability region (a convex polytope), and the scaling of logical variables ($N_{	ext{logical}}$) versus physical hardware connectivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the modulus $q$, the noise regularization parameter $\lambda$, and the dimensionality of the MLWE instance. Scaling is limited by computational complexity ($\approx N=30$) and quantum annealer connectivity overhead.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations are that current quantum annealing hardware lacks sufficient connectivity for cryptographically relevant parameters, and exact simulation/brute-force search quickly becomes infeasible due to exponential scaling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the block structure of the QUBO matrix ($Q$), the geometric stability region as a convex polytope defined by half-space constraints, and comparisons showing how the energy gap closes or the brute-force runtime explodes as noise increases or dimensions grow.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the MLWE to QUBO encoding, details the joint representation of secrets and errors into a block matrix $Q$, develops stability analysis using both geometric (polytope) and energetic (energy gap) viewpoints, validates these concepts with low-dimensional numerical experiments, and concludes by analyzing scaling limitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lattice-based post-quantum cryptography relies on the hardness of the Learning With Errors (LWE) and Module Learning With Errors (MLWE) problems. This work introduces a constructive framework for encoding small MLWE instances as Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) models suitable for quantum annealing. The formulation jointly represents secret coefficients and explicit error variables within a unified binary optimization structure, enabling their simultaneous recovery from the ground-state solution. Beyond the encoding, we develop a stability analysis of the resulting optimization landscape under additive perturbations. We show that the admissible noise region forms a convex polytope defined by competing candidate secrets, and establish an equivalent characterization in terms of the QUBO energy gap between the optimal and second-best solutions. Numerical experiments on low-dimensional benchmark instances using exact simulation demonstrate correct recovery of both secret and discretized error vectors, and confirm consistency between geometric stability regions and energy-gap behavior. We further quantify the scaling of logical variables and embedding overhead with increasing MLWE dimensions to assess feasibility on quantum annealing architectures. The results establish a systematic connection between MLWE problems and quantum optimization while providing a framework for analyzing robustness properties of QUBO formulations. Although current quantum annealing hardware remains insufficient for cryptographically relevant parameters, the proposed methodology offers a structured basis for studying lattice-based problems in quantum optimization settings without implying a practical threat to standardized post-quantum schemes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive framework using pipe diagrams to compile logical computations onto the triangular color code. It establishes this representation by linking it to ZX calculus and demonstrates that the color code offers geometric advantages over the surface code for fault-tolerant operations. The results provide a foundation for automated, efficient quantum circuit compilation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical blueprint for implementing fault-tolerant quantum computation using advanced topological codes like the Color Code, which is highly relevant to understanding physical implementations of quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to develop automated lattice surgery compilation techniques for the Color Code, extending successful methods previously established for the Surface Code.

**Main result.** They successfully developed a distance-independent pipe diagram representation for the triangular color code on the 6.6.6 lattice, enabling both macroscopic spacetime optimization and microscopic circuit construction.

**Method.** The authors utilize Pipe Diagrams as an intermediate representation, establishing its correspondence with ZX-diagrammatic descriptions to map logical computations onto physical syndrome extraction circuits.

**Model / system.** The study compares two topological quantum error-correcting codes: the Surface Code and the Color Code. The color code is implemented on a triangular lattice (6.6.6), offering advantages like reduced qubit overhead and transversal single-qubit Clifford gates compared to the surface code.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates, spacetime embedding efficiency, correlation surfaces ($\lambda_X, \lambda_Z$), and syndrome extraction circuit structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), lattice type (triangular), and gate set (restricted to Clifford operations).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The current framework is restricted to Clifford gates; incorporating T gates is identified as a necessary next step for universal computation. Decoder performance also plays a significant role in error rate comparisons.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the compilation pipeline from logical circuits through ZX diagrams to color code pipe diagrams, comparing stabilizer placements and showing syndrome extraction mechanisms (folding/superdense) for both codes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first introducing the general framework of lattice surgery using pipe diagrams. It then details the specific adaptation of this framework to the triangular color code, contrasting its geometric advantages over the surface code in terms of logical connectivity and gate implementation. Finally, it presents simulations comparing error rates for various operations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pipe diagrams have emerged as a powerful framework for flexible lattice surgery compilation and spacetime optimization for the surface code. In contrast, analogous compilation techniques for color code architectures remain largely unexplored, despite the color code's favorable properties, including reduced qubit overhead and transversal single-qubit Clifford gates. In this work, we develop a pipe diagram representation for the triangular color code on the 6.6.6 lattice and establish its correspondence to ZX-diagrammatic descriptions of computation. We present distance-independent constructions of color code pipe diagrams together with explicit realizations of correlation surfaces, stabilizers, and syndrome extraction circuits. This framework enables both macroscopic optimization of logical computations in spacetime and microscopic compilation to executable syndrome extraction circuits. We demonstrate the potential for compact spacetime embeddings with the color code's geometry. These results provide a foundation for automated lattice surgery compilation and diagrammatic optimization in color code architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel multi-layer routing protocol for qLDPC codes designed for future optical quantum hardware. It uses advanced spectral graph theory to simplify the analysis of complex Tanner graphs associated with HGP and BB codes. The resulting architecture promises substantial improvements in per-cycle operation speed compared to existing single-layer methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the physical implementation challenges of large-scale quantum computation by providing theoretical speedups and architectural blueprints for qubit connectivity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve qubit routing efficiency for quantum low-density parity check (qLDPC) codes by analyzing their underlying Tanner graph structure.

**Main result.** The authors propose a multi-layer optical lattice routing protocol that achieves significant per-cycle wall-clock speedups ($\sim 50-300	imes$) compared to single-layer methods, contingent on advanced hardware.

**Method.** The work employs spectral analysis (SVD and Fourier transforms) to characterize the Tanner graph spectrum of HGP and BB codes, leading to simplified routing depth calculations for a multi-layer architecture.

**Model / system.** The model involves three-dimensional reconfigurable qubit architectures utilizing optical lattices (AOL/AOD) to implement qLDPC codes like Hypergraph-Product (HGP) and Bivariate Bicycle (BB).

**Key observables.** Spectral ratio ($eta$), Tanner graph diameter ($D_T$), chromatic index ($\chi'$), and per-cycle syndrome extraction depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of layers ($L_{	ext{layers}}$), base matrix singular values ($\sigma_k$), and the setup cost $T_{Valiant} = O(\log N)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are contingent on the availability of multi-layer AOL hardware, and some theoretical bounds rely on unverified hypotheses (H1–H3).

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail; notes reference spectral visualizations for HGP codes showing eigenvalue matching.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first characterizing the spectra of HGP/LP and BB codes using advanced spectral techniques, deriving simplified formulas for routing depth bounds. It then constructs a multi-layer 3D AOL routing protocol based on these findings, culminating in performance estimates compared to single-layer baselines.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterize the Tanner graph spectrum of hypergraph-product (HGP) / lifted-product (LP) codes and bivariate-bicycle (BB) codes, informing qubit routing for three-dimensional reconfigurable qubit architectures. Syndrome-extraction routing depth on HGP/LP Tanner graphs reduces to a single SVD on the base parity-check matrix, using a spectral ratio $β_\text{HGP} = (1 + β_\text{base})/2$ where $β_\text{base} = σ_2(H)/σ_1(H)$ for the base parity-check matrix, and a diameter identity $D_T = 2 D_\text{base}$ where $D_\text{base}$ is the base Tanner graph diameter. Fourier spectral reduction reveals that the BB Tanner graph spectrum equals the union, over the $l \times m$ grid of characters of $\mathbb{Z}_l \times \mathbb{Z}_m$, of the singular values of a single $2 \times 2$ symbol matrix built from the two defining polynomials. This reduces spectral analysis from an $O((lm)^3)$ diagonalization of the $4lm$-node Tanner graph to $lm$ independent $2 \times 2$ SVDs. These results compose into a multi-layer three-dimensional AOL routing protocol with one-time setup cost $T_\text{Valiant} = O(\log N)$ atom rearrangements amortizable over a memory experiment of $R$ rounds. For a Tanner graph chromatic index $χ'$ and $L_\text{layers}$ stacked AOL planes, the per-syndrome-cycle depth is $\lceil χ'/L_\text{layers} \rceil$ AOL pattern activations with no atom motion, an $8\times$ step-count reduction at $L_\text{layers} \geq χ' = 8$. Contingent on multi-layer AOL hardware, this yields an estimated $\sim50-300\times$ per-cycle wall-clock advantage over a single-layer AOD baseline (degrading to $\sim5-100\times$ under AOD-crosstalk overhead), reducing to equality in the single-layer limit. This paper therefore presents a route toward practical routing improvement for future quantum hardware incorporating multi-layer reconfigurable qubit architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that spontaneous light emission from non-equilibrium phonons can be a major energy dissipation channel in materials like boron nitride. By using ultrafast spectroscopy and theory, the authors distinguish this radiative process from purely anharmonic decay, opening new avenues for understanding out-of-equilibrium physics and infrared optoelectronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics and energy relaxation mechanisms beyond simple thermal models, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems where coupling to a bath (phonons) dictates the system's evolution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spontaneous light emission from non-equilibrium phonons, proposing this radiative decay as a significant relaxation pathway competing with intrinsic anharmonic phonon decay processes.

**Main result.** The authors report the first detection of mid-infrared photoluminescence originating from these non-equilibrium phonons in boron nitride, demonstrating that radiation can be an efficient out-of-equilibrium energy dissipation mechanism.

**Method.** The work combines ultrafast optical spectroscopy (femtosecond two-photon excitation) with theoretical calculations (DFT) to model phonon decay pathways and measure spectral signatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is boron nitride (BN), particularly rhombohedral BN microcrystallites, which are used as a platform for observing light-matter interactions under non-equilibrium conditions. The physics involves the relaxation of excited states generating non-thermal phonon populations.

**Key observables.** Photoluminescence (PL) signal in the mid-infrared spectral range ($\lambda \sim 7 \mu	ext{m}$), excitonic PL ($\lambda \sim 215 	ext{ nm}$), and the partial temperature ($eT$) derived from power dependence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Femtosecond excitation timescales, cryogenic temperatures ($T \sim 25$ K), and spectral regimes distinguishing one-phonon (strong coupling) vs. two-phonon (weak coupling) decay.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between intrinsic (two-phonon) and extrinsic (one-phonon) radiative decay based on established coupling regimes, and the $eT$ estimate assumes negligible exciton collisional broadening.

**Figures summary.** Figures show IR-UV dual-band imaging contrasting phononic PL ($\lambda \sim 7 \mu	ext{m}$) with excitonic PL ($\lambda \sim 215 	ext{ nm}$), and power dependence measurements confirming Auger processes and the transition between one-phonon and two-phonon decay regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from initial experimental observation of MIR photoluminescence in BN, to detailed analysis distinguishing radiative pathways (one- vs. two-phonon), culminating in theoretical calculations matching these intrinsic two-phonon decay signatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

When a system is brought out of equilibrium by an external excitation, its relaxation to thermodynamic equilibrium generates phonons. These non-equilibrium phonons degrade via cascaded anharmonic decay processes, progressively leading to a thermal population of phonons following a Bose-Einstein distribution at the system temperature. Preceding heat dissipation by convection, conduction and incandescence, this early phase of the relaxation dynamics is commonly assumed to be exclusively non-radiative. Here, we demonstrate that the radiative emission by phonons can be an efficient relaxation pathway competing with the intrinsic anharmonic decay. Optical spectroscopy under femtosecond two-photon excitation in boron nitride unveils a photoluminescence signal in the mid-infrared spectral range, stemming from the spontaneous emission of light by non-equilibrium phonons. This observation of non-thermal radiation from phonons introduces a new paradigm for out-of-equilibrium physics, mid-infrared optics, and thermal management.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a general and practical theoretical framework to model Floquet polaritons—light-matter excitations driven by strong external fields. By calculating an effective susceptibility incorporating material nonlinearities, the authors predict novel plasmonic and phonon modes in materials like graphene and hBN. This work is significant for understanding how time-periodic driving can engineer exotic quantum states in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum optics (polaritons) with non-equilibrium physics (Floquet engineering) in solid-state materials, providing concrete, experimentally measurable predictions based on standard nonlinear optical coefficients rather than requiring complex Hamiltonian derivations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to theoretically describe Floquet polaritons, which are coupled light-matter excitations whose properties are dramatically altered by strong, time-periodic optical driving fields (pump pulses).

**Main result.** They successfully predict the existence and characteristics of novel Floquet plasmon bands in graphene, phonon polariton bands in hBN, and Josephson plasmons in superconductors using a generalized susceptibility approach.

**Method.** A practical theoretical framework is developed by calculating an effective linear optical susceptibility ($\chi_{	ext{eff}}$) that incorporates nonlinear material susceptibilities ($\chi^{(2)}, \chi^{(3)}$) induced by the pump field. This involves solving modified Maxwell's equations via Dyson-like self-energy corrections.

**Model / system.** The study applies this method to diverse quantum materials, including graphene (using $\chi^{(3)}$), hexagonal boron nitride ($	ext{hBN}$) (via phononic nonlinearity), and layered superconductors (exhibiting Josephson-type nonlinearity).

**Key observables.** Floquet polariton spectra (dispersion relations), THz reflectivity measurements, and the observation of exceptional points near plasmonic band crossings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The strength and frequency of the external pump field ($\omega_p$), and the material's nonlinear optical susceptibilities ($\chi^{(2)}, \chi^{(3)}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is using a classical treatment for Green functions, although the full Keldysh formalism exists. The method assumes that the relevant physics can be captured by calculating the self-energy $\Sigma$ from nonlinear susceptibilities.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually illustrates the transition from equilibrium polaritons ($\chi$) to Floquet polaritons ($\chi_{	ext{eff}}$) via Dyson expansion, showing how self-energies are incorporated using nonlinear susceptibilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general formalism by deriving $\chi_{	ext{eff}}$ from Maxwell's equations and incorporating pump effects via self-energy diagrams. It then applies this framework sequentially to three distinct physical systems: graphene, hBN, and layered superconductors, detailing the specific nonlinear mechanism for each.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Polaritons are coupled collective modes of light and matter in quantum materials. In modern pump-probe experiments, a pump light pulse may dramatically alter the properties of the polaritons, rendering them Floquet polaritons that can be detected by a probe pulse. We present a practical framework to describe Floquet polaritons in terms of the linear and nonlinear optical properties of the material. The central quantity that yields the spectra of Floquet polaritons is an effective linear optical susceptibility contributed by the pump through nonlinear optical susceptibilities. We apply this method to graphene and show that via its third-order optical nonlinearity, infrared pump leads to Floquet plasmon bands. Notably, near plasmonic band crossings, parametric instability leads to flat bands with unstable modes and exceptional points that closely resemble those of non-Hermitian systems. As a second example, we show that in hexagonal boron nitride pumped by mid-infrared laser, the pump induces Floquet phonon polariton bands via phononic nonlinearity, which can be detected with either far-field or near-field optical technique. Finally, in layered superconductors pumped by THz light polarized along the out-of-plane direction, the Josephson-type optical nonlinearity leads to Floquet Josephson plasmons, which manifest as new peaks in the THz reflectivity of a probe pulse.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates that by manipulating the edge curvature of nanoislands in Artificial Spin Ice, one can precisely engineer nonlinear magnon scattering. This finding establishes tip curvature as an independent control knob, allowing researchers to tune complex magnetic responses like subharmonic generation without compromising the fundamental lattice structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of controlling complex many-body dynamics (magnon interactions) in a solid-state system using precise geometric engineering, which has parallels to designing quantum metamaterials or artificial lattices for wave manipulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is engineering nonlinear magnon scattering in Artificial Spin Ice (ASI) by identifying a geometric control parameter that allows tuning of nonlinearity independently from the linear spectrum and lattice density.

**Main result.** Edge curvature has been established as a key, footprint-preserving design parameter; dumbbell shapes promote subharmonic spectra, while field orientation controls the second harmonic amplitude in an experimentally accessible way.

**Method.** The study relies on micromagnetic simulations solving the Landau–Lifshitz-Gilbert equation to model spin dynamics under microwave excitation.

**Model / system.** The system is Artificial Spin Ice (ASI), a geometrically frustrated array of interacting nanoislands, modeled using micromagnetics. The energy includes exchange and demagnetization contributions.

**Key observables.** Spin wave resonance spectra, harmonic/subharmonic generation amplitudes, and the angular dependence of the second harmonic amplitude.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Edge curvature (e.g., sharp vs. dumbbell tips), nanoisland width ($w$), applied microwave field angle ($	heta$), and lattice spacing.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous geometric controls suffer from an intrinsic trade-off between modifying nonlinearity and altering the overall island footprint or dipolar coupling.

**Figures summary.** Figures show resonance spectra for varying widths, hysteresis loops demonstrating switching behavior, and harmonic spectra confirming subharmonic generation promoted by specific tip curvatures (dumbbell vs. sharp).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates how changing geometric parameters like width and curvature affects the magnon spectrum. It contrasts previous methods with the new finding that curvature decouples nonlinearity from footprint changes, detailing angular dependence measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial spin ice (ASI), composed of geometrically frustrated arrays of interacting nanoislands, provides a versatile platform for reprogrammable magnonic functionality. However, the commonly used geometric control parameters such as island length, width, and aspect ratio simultaneously modify the island footprint, inter island dipolar spacing, and shape anisotropy, making it difficult to tune the nonlinear response independently of the linear spectrum and lattice density. Using micromagnetic simulations of kagome ASI under strong microwave drive, we identify edge curvature as a geometric degree of freedom that separates nonlinear magnon scattering from the island footprint. Sharp tipped islands predominantly generate integer harmonics, whereas dumbbell shaped tips produce a transition toward subharmonic rich spectra by concentrating demagnetizing and exchange fields near the island ends without changing the overall island volume or lattice spacing. By mapping the curvature and drive parameter space, we identify a continuous threshold for subharmonic onset controlled by tip curvature. We further show that the angular dependence of the second harmonic amplitude reverses between sharp and dumbbell geometries, providing an experimentally accessible signature of curvature localized nonlinearity. Width and leg length asymmetry can also modify harmonic amplitudes, but they do not remove the intrinsic trade off between footprint and coupling. These results establish tip curvature as a footprint preserving design parameter for engineering nonlinear magnon scattering in ASI, with implications for reconfigurable magnonic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This experiment explores correlated insulating states in TDBG interfaced with $	ext{CrOCl}$. By measuring transport properties under high magnetic fields, the authors conclude that charge-transfer effects at the interface are responsible for enhancing these correlated states. This demonstrates a powerful method of interfacial engineering to control exotic electronic phases in moiré graphene systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides direct experimental evidence of how interfacial chemistry (charge transfer) can be used as a tuning knob to manipulate strongly correlated electronic phases in 2D materials, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence and nature of correlated insulating states in twisted double bilayer graphene (TDBG) when interfaced with an antiferromagnetic substrate ($	ext{CrOCl}$). The core question is whether the observed effects are due to magnetic proximity or charge-transfer interactions.

**Main result.** The enhanced correlated insulating state at half-filling is primarily attributed to a charge-transfer effect between TDBG and $	ext{CrOCl}$, rather than direct magnetic exchange. This interfacial engineering opens a new route for controlling correlated states in moiré systems.

**Method.** Transport measurements were performed using ultrahigh magnetic fields and low temperatures, analyzing longitudinal/Hall resistance, Landau fan diagrams, and temperature dependence of the insulating state.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Twisted Double Bilayer Graphene (TDBG) placed on a $	ext{CrOCl}$ substrate. The analysis focuses on correlated electron behavior in this moiré heterostructure under external magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Enhanced resistance at half-filling, thermal activation gap ($\Delta$), and the effective g-factor derived from temperature dependence measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultrahigh magnetic field (up to 20 T), low temperatures ($10 	ext{ mK}$), and the charge-transfer doping regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the observed enhancement stems from a charge-related reconstruction of the band structure due to interfacial coupling, distinguishing it from purely magnetic effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show gate maps indicating the charge-transfer boundary, Landau fan diagrams comparing states with and without CT effect, and plots demonstrating how the insulating gap ($\Delta$) and g-factor change with temperature and applied magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental platform and observing the correlated state. It then systematically analyzes the dependence of this state on external fields (B) and temperature (T), using quantitative diagnostics like $\Delta$ and $g$-factors to confirm the role of charge transfer over magnetism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interaction between different two dimensional materials can give rise to many exotic physical phenomena which are rarely observed in intrinsic materials. Recently, several theoretical and experimental works have revealed that magnetic proximity effect between pristine graphene and magnetic substrates can lead to the emergence of quantum anomalous Hall states and quantum spin Hall states. However, interplay between correlated states in graphene-based systems and magnetic materials has seldom been studied. Here we perform the transport measurement at ultrahigh magnetic field of twisted double bilayer graphene (TDBG) on CrOCl (COC) substrate, which is an antiferromagnetic material. Instead of a magnetic-exchange effect on graphene, we observe an enhanced correlated insulating state at half-filling factor of TDBG as a result of the charge-transfer process between TDBG and COC. The temperature and magnetic field dependence of this enhanced state are further studied. Our results demonstrate the influence of charge-related effect at the interface, and shed a light on a new route for manipulating the correlated states in graphene-based moiré systems using interfacial engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper shows how to engineer exact flat energy bands in a 3D photonic crystal by exploiting its underlying symmetry. By analyzing the vector nature of Maxwell's equations and projecting the problem onto a radial subspace, the authors derive a necessary coupling balance condition ($J_L = -J_T$). Satisfying this condition allows the system to host an exactly solvable scalar sector with flat bands.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, symmetry-based design rule for realizing robust flat bands in complex electromagnetic structures, which is highly relevant to quantum simulation platforms using photonic lattices or metamaterials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is engineering exact flat bands in a fully vectorial photonic crystal, overcoming the obstruction posed by Maxwell's equations which typically prevents simple scalar-based band engineering.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that an exact scalar sector carrying flat bands can be realized in a 3D metallic network if the coupling constants satisfy a specific symmetry condition: $J_L = -J_T$.

**Method.** The analysis involves projecting the full twelve-band vector Hamiltonian onto a radial subspace using analytical identities, leading to an effective scalar problem whose solvability dictates the flat band existence.

**Model / system.** The system is a 3D metallic network structured as a space-group-224 lattice, modeled by a coupled-dipole Hamiltonian. The physics is analyzed via projecting the vector dynamics onto a radial subspace to find an effective scalar Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Flat band frequency ($\lambda$), energy gap opening, and the ratio of longitudinal ($J_L$) to transverse ($J_T$) coupling constants.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anisotropy parameter ($\Delta\chi$), which controls spectral separation; the critical balance condition $J_L = -J_T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving an effective Hamiltonian by assuming that the radial subspace forms an invariant subspace of the full vector Hamiltonian, requiring the specific coupling balance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry (space-group-224), compare band structures for scalar vs. vector systems, and show how the exact flat bands appear only when the coupling achieves a Householder reflection symmetry ($J_L = -J_T$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the difficulty of vectorial flatness, derives an effective Hamiltonian by projection onto the radial subspace, identifies the necessary symmetry condition ($J_L = -J_T$) for exact decoupling, and confirms this analytically and numerically.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic flat bands are hard to engineer because Maxwell's equations are vectorial: transversality obstructs the localized scalar-like bases that generate destructive-interference flat bands in tight-binding models. We show that a three-dimensional metallic network of dipolar cavities joined by waveguide channels--a fully vectorial photonic crystal belonging to space group No. 224--hosts an exact scalar sector, carrying exact flat bands. The twelve-band vector problem contains one self-adaptive radial dipole axis per site whose projection is exactly the scalar four-band Hamiltonian of the same network. A microwave-scale coupled-dipole calculation confirms this scalar-vectorial duality. The result is a symmetry-based design rule for scalar-like flat bands in reciprocal vector media.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes vortex motion in superconductors by incorporating the crucial role of charge-imbalance potential relaxation into the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau framework. It derives an advanced equation of motion that captures retardation effects, showing how these mechanisms fundamentally alter the kinetic inductance observed in superconducting circuits at high frequencies.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the inclusion of dynamic potential relaxation and resulting frequency-dependent inertia/damping mechanisms provides a rich model for understanding dissipative dynamics in complex quantum systems near phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the relaxation of the charge-imbalance potential influences the high-frequency dynamics of Abrikosov vortices in superconducting circuits.

**Main result.** Retardation effects, governed by this potential relaxation, significantly alter the vortex equation of motion and consequently modify the kinetic inductance of superconducting devices at high frequencies.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau (TDGL) equations, employing perturbation theory and Fourier transforms to derive frequency-domain equations for vortex velocity and supercurrent density.

**Model / system.** The system is a Type-II superconducting sample carrying a microwave current. The model is based on extending the TDGL framework to account for charge-imbalance potential dynamics, which governs the retarded response of vortices.

**Key observables.** Kinetic inductance ($L_k$), vortex velocity ($	ilde{v}(\omega)$), and the total dynamic impedance contribution from vortices ($z_v$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Characteristic timescales like the Ginzburg-Landau relaxation time ($	au_E$) and frequency regimes relative to $\omega	au_E$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is primarily restricted to gapless superconductors in the dirty limit, and approximations are made regarding the averaging over the vortex lattice.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates a moving vortex, detailing related quantities like core radius, induced charge-imbalance potential, and various supercurrent components.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the fundamental TDGL equations to derive an effective equation of motion for vortex dynamics that incorporates both Ohmic dissipation and retardation effects arising from the charge-imbalance relaxation. This leads to a frequency-dependent impedance calculation affecting $L_k$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that relaxation of the charge-imbalance potential plays a key role in the retarded dynamics of Abrikosov vortices in a type-II superconducting sample carrying a microwave current. Starting from the time-dependent Ginzburg--Landau equations we derive the vortex equation of motion accounting both the dissipation and retardation effects. The retardation is governed by the dynamics of the charge-imbalance potential and reveals itself at characteristic timescales diverging near the superconducting critical temperature $T_{c}$. These retardation effects in vortex dynamics strongly affect the kinetic inductance of superconducting circuits being, thus, responsible for the magnetic field dependence of characteristics of different superconducting devices in the high frequency range.

</details>

</div>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the physics of photonic time crystals where energy gaps (k-gaps) are opened by temporal modulation. It demonstrates that perturbing the resulting nonlinear soliton background triggers a unique 'breathing k-gap event.' This mechanism, termed instability on instability, allows for deterministic control over extreme spatiotemporal wave generation using engineered seeding techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear optics with condensed matter concepts of bandgaps and instability, providing a clear mechanism (instability on instability) for generating complex wave phenomena in periodically structured media, which is highly relevant to advanced photonics and metamaterials research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the generation and control of transient spatiotemporal wave excitations, termed 'breathing k-gap events,' within nonlinear photonic time crystals (PTCs). It specifically addresses the underlying mechanism identified as an 'instability on instability.'

**Main result.** These breathing events are shown to be robust against noise and disorder and can be deterministically controlled by engineering the initial seeding phase or amplitude. This establishes k-gap engineering as a powerful method for controlling extreme spatiotemporal waves in time-varying media.

**Method.** The analysis uses coupled-mode equations derived from Maxwell's equations, modeling the nonlinear wave propagation using a slowly-varying envelope approximation and Floquet-Bloch theory.

**Model / system.** The system is Nonlinear Photonic Time Crystals (PTCs) characterized by periodically modulated permittivity ($\epsilon_1(t)$). The dynamics are governed by coupled nonlinear evolution equations for forward ($A_f$) and backward ($A_b$) propagating envelopes, incorporating Kerr nonlinearity.

**Key observables.** Breathing k-gap event peak intensity, temporal shift of the excitation, spatially integrated intensity $P(t)$, and collective breathing patterns.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kerr coefficient ($\gamma$), modulation depth ($\delta$), initial seed amplitude ($A_0$), and phase engineering parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the assumption of small modulation depth ($\delta \ll 1$) and the use of the slowly-varying envelope approximation for deriving the coupled nonlinear equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PTC setup, the formation of k-gap soliton trains, and the resulting breathing event. They also show parameter dependence (e.g., intensity vs $\gamma$ or time delay vs $A_0$) and examples of phase-engineered collective patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper first derives the governing coupled nonlinear equations for the forward and backward envelopes using a coupled-mode framework based on Floquet theory. It then analyzes the dynamics, showing k-gap soliton formation followed by triggering the breathing event via localized perturbation. Finally, it demonstrates control over this event using varying nonlinearity or engineered seeding phases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic time crystals (PTCs) host momentum bandgaps, or k gaps, that enable parametric amplification and lasing of seeded fields. In nonlinear PTCs, Kerr saturation dynamically suppresses the exponential growth, reshaping k-gap amplification into an active, spatially homogeneous k gap soliton train. Here, we show that a localized perturbation on this unstable background then nucleates a transient spatiotemporal excitation: the breathing k gap event. Unlike Peregrine breathers emerging from modulational instability on a planewave background, this event extracts energy from competing host k gap solitons and remains sustained by their interaction. We identify this process as an instability on instability mechanism intrinsic to nonlinear k gap dynamics. The event is robust against noise and disorder, and can be deterministically reshaped into collective breathing patterns by periodic and phase engineered seeding. These results establish k gap engineering as a route to generating and controlling extreme spatiotemporal waves in photonic time varying media.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that rhombohedral graphene acts as a highly tunable platform capable of hosting a switchable quartet of chiral superconductors. By controlling the carrier density, gate voltage, and magnetic field, researchers can programmatically access different spin-valley polarized superconducting states. This establishes $	ext{R}6	ext{G}$ for potential applications in topological quantum electronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer multiple, distinct topological superconducting phases using external electric and magnetic fields in a single 2D material platform is highly relevant for building quantum circuits or studying exotic pairing mechanisms beyond simple BCS theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to explore and control all spin-valley flavors of superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene ($	ext{R}6	ext{G}$) to realize programmable superconducting networks.

**Main result.** The authors report the realization of a switchable chiral-superconductor quartet in $	ext{R}6	ext{G}$ by tuning external fields, establishing the material as a multi-knob platform for novel topological devices.

**Method.** Transport measurements, including resistance mapping ($R_{xx}$, $R_H$), anomalous Hall measurements, and quantum oscillation analysis, were performed across varying magnetic fields and gate voltages.

**Model / system.** The physical system is rhombohedral hexalayer graphene ($	ext{R}6	ext{G}$), which possesses multiple degrees of freedom (spin, valley, layer) allowing for the study of spin-valley-polarized superconductivity. The physics involves competition between Kane-Mele-like spin-orbit coupling and magnetic field coupling.

**Key observables.** Zero-resistance states ($	ext{SC}1$, $	ext{SCH}$), anomalous Hall resistance sign reversal, quantum oscillation frequencies, and the ability to switch superconducting phases using external knobs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Carrier density ($n$), gate displacement field ($D$), and out-of-plane magnetic field ($H_{\perp}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The parent state switching ($	ext{QM} 	o 	ext{QM}'$) is interpreted as arising from the competition between SOC and magnetic coupling; the existence of Majorana modes at domain walls is a potential consequence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate superconducting phase diagrams in $(n, D)$ space showing $	ext{SC}1$ and $	ext{SCH}$ regions. They also map out the emergence of $	ext{SCH}$ above $0.8 	ext{ T}$ and demonstrate programmable switching between states using gate voltage or magnetic field tuning.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps superconducting phases in the $(n, D, H_{\perp})$ parameter space. It first identifies low-field chiral superconductivity ($	ext{SC}1$, $	ext{SC}2$), then reports the emergence of a high-field state ($	ext{SCH}$), and finally argues that tuning all three knobs allows access to a full quartet of spin-valley polarized superconducting states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chiral superconductors break orbital time-reversal symmetry and may host topological quasiparticles with non-Abelian statistics. In rhombohedral graphene, superconductivity develops from a spin-valley-polarized quarter-metal (QM) parent state and features unique magnetic hysteresis of resistance that indicates orbital time-reversal-symmetry-breaking. Exploring and controlling the full spin-valley flavors of such superconductivity could enable novel superconducting and topological devices, but have remained unexplored. Here we report transport measurements on rhombohedral hexalayer graphene (R6G), which reveal a new superconducting state (SCH) that is induced by an out-of-plane magnetic field, in addition to chiral superconductivity (CSC) similar to those observed in thinner layers. This SCH state emerges above 0.8 T, persists up to 1.6 T and can be switched on/off by magnetic field $H_\perp$, carrier density $n$, and gate displacement field $D$. Quantum oscillations and anomalous Hall measurements show that SCH stems from a field-induced quarter-metal (QM$'$) parent phase, which carries orbital magnetization opposite to that of the zero-field QM. Across the full $(n, D, H_\perp)$ parameter space, superconductivity can be realized from all four spin-valley isospin flavors, establishing a switchable chiral-superconductor quartet in R6G. We interpret the parent-state switching as arising from competition between a Kane-Mele-like spin-valley splitting and magnetic-field coupling to spin-valley-dependent magnetic moments. Our work establishes rhombohedral graphene as a multi-knob platform for different isospin-polarized superconductivities, which enables programmable superconducting networks with possible Majorana modes along domain walls.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally and theoretically investigates how hydrostatic pressure modifies the complex magnetic order of (Co$_{0.5}$Fe$_{0.5}$)$_5$GeTe$_2$. By analyzing magnetoresistance, the researchers quantified that pressure strongly enhances interlayer coupling while successfully modeling subtle AFM hysteresis using a linear chain approach, suggesting surface effects dominate over bulk FM coexistence.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to tune magnetic interactions ($J$) by external pressure, combined with modeling layer-by-layer magnetism using concepts from quantum many-body physics (like chain models), provides insights into emergent quantum phenomena in 2D materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how external pressure tunes the magnetic properties of (Co$_{0.5}$Fe$_{0.5}$)$_5$GeTe$_2$, specifically focusing on understanding the nature of its magnetic order within the Antiferromagnetic (AFM) state.

**Main result.** Applying pressure significantly enhances the interlayer exchange coupling ($J$) by about 50% at 2 GPa, and the small hysteresis loops observed in the AFM phase are explained by surface layer behavior modeled via a linear chain approach rather than coexisting ferromagnetic order.

**Method.** The research combines magnetoresistance measurements (Hall effect) under varying pressures with theoretical modeling using both two-sublattice and linear chain models to extract magnetic parameters.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the 2D magnet (Co$_{0.5}$Fe$_{0.5}$)$_5$GeTe$_2$. The analysis employs the two-sublattice model for bulk behavior and a linear chain model to describe layer-dependent phenomena, such as surface layer switching.

**Key observables.** Magnetoresistance ($R_{xy}$) vs. magnetic field ($H$), Spin-flop transition field ($	ext{H}_{	ext{SF}}$), small hysteresis loops within the AFM state, and interlayer exchange coupling ($J$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied pressure ($p$) up to 2 GPa; anisotropy constant ($K$); interlayer exchange coupling ($J$); layer number ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The small hysteresis loops are attributed solely to surface layer switching described by the linear chain model, avoiding the need for a coexisting FM phase. The analysis often involves fitting data using simplified two-sublattice approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show SEM/optical images and $R_{xy}$ vs. $H$ curves at various pressures and temperatures; subsequent figures plot derived fields ($	ext{H}_{	ext{SF}}$) versus pressure or temperature, and analyze the ratio of hysteresis features ($\Delta R / \Delta r / N$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from presenting experimental measurements of magnetoresistance under pressure to applying theoretical models (two-sublattice and linear chain) to fit the data. It systematically analyzes phase transitions ($	ext{SF}$ vs $	ext{FM}$) and finally uses the chain model to explain fine details like small hysteresis loops.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cobalt-doped Fe$_5$GeTe$_2$ possesses a rich magnetic phase diagram as a function of Co concentration. The nature of magnetic order in (Co$_{0.5}$Fe$_{0.5}$)$_5$GeTe$_2$ is especially interesting, as it has been shown to exhibit ferromagnetic order, A-type antiferromagnetic (AFM) order, or potentially both at the same time. Here we present magnetoresistance measurements on antiferromagnetic (Co$_{0.5}$Fe$_{0.5}$)$_5$GeTe$_2$ at a series of pressures and extract the anisotropy and interlayer exchange fields using the two-sublattice model. We show a 50 % increase of the interlayer exchange at 2 GPa, highlighting the sensitivity of magnetic properties to interlayer distance. In addition, we find that the sharp hysteretic transitions observed within the AFM state can be qualitatively described by a linear chain model, which suggests an even-odd effect as a function of layer number instead of a coexisting ferromagnetic phase.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically examines spin-orbit coupled graphene junctions, demonstrating that the junction's edge structure dictates its transport properties. Key findings include robust valley filtering in the normal state and a significant Josephson Diode Effect (JDE) in the superconducting state due to broken mirror symmetry at zigzag edges. This highlights how engineered symmetries can control quantum electronic functionalities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong dependence of macroscopic quantum effects (like SDE) on microscopic symmetries (edge termination leading to broken mirror symmetry) is highly relevant for designing novel quantum electronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates transport properties, including valley filtering and superconducting diode effects, in graphene junctions that acquire spin-orbit coupling (SOC) via proximity to a substrate.

**Main result.** The system exhibits strong non-reciprocal supercurrents leading to a significant Josephson Diode Effect (JDE), which is strongly dependent on the edge termination symmetry of the junction.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using tight-binding models solved in the ballistic and zero temperature limits, employing scattering formalism and analyzing Andreev Bound States (ABS).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a graphene Josephson Junction (GJJ) where SOC is induced by proximity. The model uses a tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating Rashba and intrinsic SOC terms.

**Key observables.** Valley polarization ($\kappa$), supercurrent ($I(\phi)$), non-reciprocal supercurrent ($\Delta I_c$), and magnetic interference patterns.

**Important parameters / regimes.** SOC strengths ($\lambda_R, \lambda_{VZ}$), junction width/length ratios, perpendicular magnetic field strength ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations are performed in the ballistic and short junction approximations at zero temperature.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures showing valley polarization gaps (QSH/QVSH phases) and show the dependence of valley polarization ($\kappa$) on junction length, alongside superconducting current analysis vs. magnetic flux.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing normal state transport to demonstrate valley filtering in QSH/QVSH phases, and then extends this analysis to superconducting junctions to reveal non-reciprocal supercurrents causing the JDE.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the transport properties of proximitized graphene, which can acquire a spin-orbit coupling by the proximity effect with a substrate. We focus on the ballistic and zero temperature limits, making use of a tight-binding procedure based on the KWANT Python package. We first find key results on valley-filtering properties and asymmetric edge transport in spin-orbit coupled graphene single junctions, and then move to the analysis of the superconducting transport in a graphene Josephson junction, in the short junction limit. We study the relative contribution of edge modes for different edge terminations and some degree of edge disorder, and also analyze the magnetic interference pattern that arises when threading the junction with a perpendicular magnetic field. We find residual supercurrent at high magnetic fluxes, due to the localized nature of transport in the junction, and a strong non-reciprocal transport that leads to a significant Josephson diode effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces interfacial noncollinear filtering as a mechanism to reprogram spin-current polarization in spintronic materials. By treating the emitted current as a hybrid bulk-interface response, the authors show that even high-symmetry sources can generate unconventional out-of-plane spin currents if the interface possesses sufficient low symmetry. This shifts control from intrinsic material symmetry to engineered interfacial quantum selection.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the concept of engineered boundary conditions controlling quantum response echoes principles used in open quantum systems where environment coupling dictates observable dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The conventional view treats Spin Hall currents as bulk responses constrained by crystal symmetry, limiting the achievable spin polarization direction.

**Main result.** Interfacial noncollinear filtering allows for generating unconventional emitted spin currents from high-symmetry sources by selectively transmitting hidden momentum-resolved spin components at the interface.

**Method.** The authors establish the emitted spin current as a bulk–interface hybrid response, modeling the effect using interfacial spin-orbit fields that impose spin-dependent transmission probabilities.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes heterostructures involving high-symmetry spin sources (like Dirac semimetals) coupled to low-symmetry interfaces, modeled using effective Hamiltonians incorporating Rashba-type coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Emitted out-of-plane spin current ($J_{zzz}$), the ratio $J_z/J_y$, and $\mathbf{k}_{\|}$-resolved hidden spin Berry-curvature components ($\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The strength of the interfacial low-symmetry coupling ($\lambda_z$) is crucial, as it breaks symmetry and enables the unconventional current.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The emitted current is treated as a hybrid response combining bulk texture and interfacial transmission; analysis often focuses on states with positive group velocity normal to the interface.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that low-symmetry interfaces lead to noncollinear filtering, resulting in finite out-of-plane spin currents ($J_{zzz} 
eq 0$), whereas high symmetry leads to cancellation ($J_{zzz}=0$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the limitation of bulk symmetry constraints to proposing the hybrid bulk-interface mechanism. It develops models using minimal and realistic Dirac semimetal systems to calculate how interfacial coupling selectively transmits hidden spin components, demonstrating a quantitative increase in unconventional current with interface asymmetry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin Hall currents generated in nonmagnetic materials are conventionally regarded as bulk responses whose polarization is fixed by crystal symmetry. This view has motivated the search for intrinsically low-symmetry spin sources when unconventional spin polarizations are required. Here we point out that, in realistic heterostructures, the device-relevant quantity is not the fully symmetry-averaged bulk spin Hall current, but the emitted spin current transmitted across the interface. We therefore establish emitted spin currents as bulk-interface hybrid responses and propose interfacial noncollinear filtering as a mechanism to bypass the bulk-symmetry constraint. A low-symmetry interfacial spin-orbit field, generally noncollinear with the momentum-resolved spin polarization of the incident spin Hall current, imposes spin-dependent transmission and converts hidden momentum-resolved spin-polarization components into an observable unconventional emitted spin current. Using both a rotationally symmetric minimal model and a realistic high-symmetry Dirac-semimetal model, we show that conventional spin Hall sources can emit sizable out-of-plane spin currents when their hidden bulk spin Hall textures are selectively transmitted by the interfacial spin-orbit field. Our results reveal that spin-current polarization emerges from the cooperative action of bulk and interfacial responses, providing a strategy for reprogramming spin-current polarization in high-efficiency, CMOS-compatible spin Hall materials without relying on intrinsically low-symmetry bulk crystals or external symmetry-breaking schemes.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study predicts that sandwiching bilayer graphene between Janus monolayers like WSSe or MnSSe creates a platform for advanced spintronics. The key mechanism involves the monolayer inducing opposite signs of Rashba SOC in the top and bottom layers, allowing researchers to independently switch spin current directions by applying gate voltages.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter spintronics, the concept of using engineered interfaces (Janus materials) to induce controllable, layer-specific spin-orbit coupling is relevant to understanding topological phenomena in low-dimensional quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to design gate-tunable, layer-selective spintronic devices by independently controlling the spin current direction in the top and bottom layers of bilayer graphene.

**Main result.** Intercalating bilayer graphene with Janus monolayers (WSSe or MnSSe) induces opposite signs of proximity-induced Rashba SOC in each layer, enabling independent control over spin currents via electrostatic gating.

**Method.** The study uses first-principles DFT calculations to derive parameters for an effective tight-binding model, followed by linear response theory and Rashba-Edelstein conversion calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is bilayer graphene ($	ext{Gr}$) sandwiched between a Janus monolayer ($	ext{WSSe}$ or $	ext{MnSSe}$). The low-energy physics is modeled using two independent, proximitized graphene layers described by effective Hamiltonians incorporating orbital, intrinsic SOC, and Rashba SOC terms.

**Key observables.** Rashba-Edelstein coefficient ($\alpha_{	ext{REE}}$), spin current direction, layer-resolved band structure, and chemical potential shifts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The proximity-induced Rashba SOC signs, the exchange coupling parameters ($\Delta_A, \Delta_B$) for magnetic cases, and gate voltages applied to individual layers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The low-energy physics is assumed to be described by two independent graphene layers due to the decoupling effect; the $	ext{MnSSe}$ magnetic configuration uses an out-of-plane antiferromagnetic model case.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows how a charge current generates spin accumulations in opposite directions. Figure 2 confirms decoupled Dirac cones and layer-resolved band structures for both heterostructures.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the physical system (Janus intercalation), deriving effective Hamiltonians incorporating proximity effects, calculating key transport coefficients like $\alpha_{	ext{REE}}$, and demonstrating that opposite SOC signs lead to independent spin current control via gating.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We predict that intercalating bilayer graphene with nonmagnetic WSSe or magnetic MnSSe Janus monolayers induces a layer-selective switch of the in-plane Rashba spin texture, resulting in opposite spin current directions in the top and bottom graphene layers. First-principles calculations reveal that both Janus monolayers decouple the two graphene layers while simultaneously inducing opposite signs of the proximity-induced Rashba spin-orbit coupling in each. Tight-binding modeling of the proximitized layers, combined with Rashba-Edelstein charge-to-spin conversion calculations, confirms that the spin current direction can be independently controlled by gating the top or bottom graphene layer. Bilayer graphene intercalated by Janus monolayers thus represents a promising platform for gate-tunable, layer-selective spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The study uses advanced Raman spectroscopy and DFT calculations to characterize the vibrational properties of the altermagnetic material $	ext{Co}_{1/4}	ext{NbSe}_2$. It reveals that Co intercalation reconstructs the phonon spectrum while confirming the existence of spin-phonon coupling in specific modes. This work advances understanding of how magnetic ordering influences lattice dynamics in transition metal dichalcogenides.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed spectroscopic probe into emergent magnetic phenomena (altermagnetism) in 2D materials, linking electronic order directly to lattice vibrations via measurable Raman shifts. This type of magneto-vibrational coupling is highly relevant for designing novel quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how Co intercalation and altermagnetic ordering affect the lattice dynamics of the layered van der Waals material $	ext{Co}_{1/4}	ext{NbSe}_2$. The goal is to identify vibrational signatures and evidence for spin-phonon coupling.

**Main result.** Intercalation causes significant reconstruction of the phonon spectrum via zone folding, while temperature measurements reveal indirect spin-phonon coupling in specific $	ext{A}_{1g}$ symmetry modes. Furthermore, Co atoms do not contribute to any Raman-active modes by symmetry.

**Method.** The research combines polarization-resolved Raman spectroscopy with Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to model phonon dispersion and analyze temperature dependence across the altermagnetic transition.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Co}_{1/4}	ext{NbSe}_2$, an altermagnet, which is a layered van der Waals material. The analysis models lattice vibrations using DFT for various magnetic states (NM, FM, AFM) and examines temperature evolution via the Balkanski anharmonic model.

**Key observables.** Raman-active phonon frequencies ($	ext{cm}^{-1}$), mode symmetry ($	ext{A}_{1g}, 	ext{E}_{2g}$, etc.), spectral weight changes due to zone folding, and spin-phonon coupling constants ($\lambda$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Altermagnetic transition temperature ($T_N \approx 168 	ext{ K}$), magnetic moment ($\mu_B$), and the coupling constant $\lambda$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that spin-phonon coupling can be quantified by analyzing frequency shifts ($\Delta\omega$) relative to zero-temperature fits, despite the itinerant nature of Co electrons.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate lattice structures, polarization-resolved Raman spectra showing selection rules, and temperature evolution plots demonstrating phonon frequency shifts across multiple samples.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents structural details, analyzes the spectral reconstruction due to intercalation using DFT calculations for various symmetry modes, examines temperature dependence relative to $T_N$, and finally quantifies spin-phonon coupling constants ($\lambda$) based on mode eigenvectors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the influence of Co intercalation and altermagnetic order on the lattice dynamics of the layered compound Co$_{1/4}$NbSe$_2$. Polarization-resolved Raman spectroscopy, supported by density-functional theory, enables identification of six Raman-active phonons. Co intercalation drives a substantial reconstruction of the vibrational spectrum through zone folding of NbSe$_2$ phonons, producing hybridized modes with mixed zone-center and zone-boundary character. Despite this, Co atoms do not participate in any Raman-active modes by symmetry, which is in marked contrast to related 1/3 compounds where intercalant modes do contribute to the Raman spectrum. Temperature-dependent Raman measurements across the altermagnetic transition show no discontinuities, which is consistent with short-range spin correlations in the quasi-one-dimensional Co chains. However, we find evidence for spin-phonon coupling in A$_{1g}$ symmetry modes owing to their out-of-plane Se displacements. Our work demonstrates the substantial impact of intercalation on the vibrational properties of transition metal dichalcogenides and the presence of spin-phonon interactions in a newly discovered altermagnetic material.

</details>
