**Summary.** This paper provides a microscopic theory for calculating the lower critical magnetic field (B_c1) in superconducting thin films by solving the 2D Usadel equations. It overcomes limitations of older models by self-consistently resolving the vortex core structure, demonstrating that the resulting energy scale differs significantly from simple London predictions. This work establishes a robust theoretical basis for predicting superconducting behavior in nanoscale devices across various temperatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the rigorous application of advanced continuum field theories (Usadel equations) to resolve fundamental physical quantities like core structure without ad-hoc cutoffs is a valuable methodological contribution applicable to other strongly coupled quantum systems where approximations are common.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a microscopic theory for determining the lower critical field (B_c1) in superconducting thin-film strips, addressing shortcomings in standard phenomenological models.

**Main result.** The microscopic calculation resolves the vortex core structure without needing an arbitrary cutoff, showing that the resulting vortex self-energy significantly deviates from naive Pearl-London estimates.

**Method.** The authors solve the two-dimensional Usadel equations self-consistently for both vortex and Meissner states at fixed magnetic fields to calculate B_c1 via Gibbs energy differences.

**Model / system.** The system is a narrow superconducting thin-film strip. The theory uses the dirty limit approximation solved via 2D Usadel equations, incorporating the applied magnetic field directly into the momentum terms.

**Key observables.** Lower critical field (B_c1), dimensionless vortex self-energy factor (E_v), and edge barrier field (B_s).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dirty-limit coherence length (xi), Pearl length (Lambda), normalized strip width (w = W/xi), and temperature (T < Tc).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The film is assumed thin enough that superconducting parameters are uniform across the thickness dimension; magnetic screening by the strip itself is neglected.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares self-consistent Usadel results for B_c1 vs. width (w) against the naive Pearl-London estimate. Figure 2 shows the temperature dependence of E_v, comparing Usadel results to empirical fits derived from the theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem and limitations of existing theories; it then details the microscopic framework using the Usadel equations solved self-consistently for vortex/Meissner states; finally, it presents quantitative comparisons showing the deviation from simple London models across varying temperatures and widths.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The lower critical field \(B_{c1}\) of a narrow superconducting thin-film strip sets the thermodynamic scale for vortex-free operation in a perpendicular magnetic field. The standard Pearl--London estimate requires a phenomenological vortex-core cutoff, because the London theory does not resolve the core. We formulate a microscopic theory for a dirty strip by solving the two-dimensional Usadel equations in the film plane, with the applied field included directly in the gauge-invariant momentum. Self-consistent vortex and Meissner solutions are computed at fixed field, and \(B_{c1}\) is obtained from their Gibbs-energy difference. The calculation resolves the vortex core and its finite-width deformation without introducing a cutoff. The resulting vortex self-energy is larger than the naive Pearl--London estimate and cannot, in general, be represented by a London logarithm with a single width-independent cutoff. The formulation applies at any \(T<T_c\) and provides a microscopic basis for predicting \(B_{c1}\) in superconducting nanostrips and related thin-film devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the interfacial properties of colloidal hard spheres under gravity, focusing on a discrepancy in the crystal-melt stiffness coefficient. Using computer simulations, the authors demonstrate that this missing physics arises from an explicit, previously overlooked dependence of the stiffness coefficient on the external gravitational field. This provides a general result for how external fields affect interface thermodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical colloidal systems, the rigorous treatment of external field effects (gravity) modifying fundamental thermodynamic coefficients like stiffness is relevant to understanding non-equilibrium or externally driven many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses an enigmatic discrepancy concerning the crystal-melt stiffness coefficient of colloidal hard spheres, particularly when external gravity is considered.

**Main result.** The authors show that this long-standing discrepancy can be traced to a previously unrecognized, explicit dependence of the interfacial stiffness coefficient on the applied gravitational field.

**Method.** Computer simulations (Monte Carlo) are used to calculate density profiles and analyze the capillary wave spectrum at fluid-crystal interfaces under tunable buoyant mass and gravity.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is colloidal hard spheres, which serve as an experimental analog for studying bulk and interfacial properties of simple fluids undergoing phase transitions into crystalline solids.

**Key observables.** Interfacial stiffness coefficient ($	ilde{\gamma}$), density profiles ($ho(z)$), pressure tensor components ($P_{zz}, P_{xx}, P_{yy}$), and the mean squared amplitude of surface Fourier modes ($\langle h^2_q angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Buoyant mass, external gravity ($	ilde{G}$), reduced temperature/pressure parameters (e.g., $eta p\sigma^3 = 11.65$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on relating interfacial properties to the capillary wave spectrum and uses simplified ansätze for density profiles in some theoretical derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density and pressure profiles showing stratification due to gravity, and compare simulated vs. fitted capillary wave spectra across varying gravity strengths ($	ilde{G}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model (colloidal hard spheres) and identifying the discrepancy, through detailed simulation analysis of equilibrium states under gravity, culminating in deriving the functional form showing $	ilde{\gamma}$'s dependence on $	ilde{G}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spherical colloids with harsh repulsive forces have long been used as experimental analogs of the hard sphere model, with demonstrated good agreement with computer simulations for bulk and structural properties of the fluid, glass and crystal phases. However, an enigmatic discrepancy remains for the crystal-melt stiffness coefficient. Here we perform computer simulations of colloidal hard spheres under tunable buoyant mass and show that the long-standing discrepancy can be traced to a hitherto unrecognized gravity dependent contribution of the stiffness coefficient. This effect is one practical realization of a more general result for the external field dependence of stiffness coefficients of arbitrary interfaces.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors investigated twisted $	ext{NbSe}_2/	ext{NbSe}_2$ junctions to determine if crystal alignment controls supercurrent diode effects. They found that while the primary Josephson coupling strength strongly depends on the twist angle due to momentum matching, the non-reciprocity remains negligible unless interface disorder is intentionally introduced. This decisively decouples the two phenomena, identifying interfacial quality as the source of apparent diode behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial experimental benchmark for understanding superconducting transport in engineered heterostructures, directly addressing how structural control (twist angle) relates to macroscopic quantum phenomena like non-reciprocity, which is relevant for Josephson circuits and quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to decouple Josephson coupling strength from supercurrent nonreciprocity (diode effect) in twisted van der Waals superconducting junctions, resolving debates on the role of twist angle engineering.

**Main result.** Josephson coupling shows a clear dependence on twist angle due to momentum matching, but the diode response is found to be negligible unless significant interface disorder is introduced. This establishes that interface disorder, not crystal alignment, dominates non-reciprocity.

**Method.** The research uses electrical transport measurements (I-V curves) combined with structural characterization (HR-TEM) on fabricated twisted NbSe2/NbSe2 junctions.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a vertical Josephson junction formed by stacking two layers of Niobium Diselenide ($	ext{NbSe}_2$) with controlled twist angles. Analysis involves comparing measurements to the Ambegaokar-Baratoff relation and using the RCSJ model for dynamics.

**Key observables.** Josephson coupling strength ($I_c$), supercurrent diode efficiency ($\eta$), critical current ($I_{c\pm}$), and characteristic voltage $V_c$ as functions of twist angle, magnetic field, and temperature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta_{	ext{twist}}$), in-plane magnetic field ($\mu_0H_{\parallel}$), junction capacitance ($C_{	ext{eff}}$), and interface disorder level.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the RCSJ model for dynamic characterization, and it assumes that deviations from ideal behavior can be attributed to specific physical mechanisms like interface degradation or momentum mismatch.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail $V-I$ and $dV/dI$ contour maps showing field and angle dependence; Figure 5 specifically plots the distinct $	heta_{	ext{twist}}$ dependence of coupling strength versus nonreciprocity ($\eta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates transport properties by first establishing the twist-angle dependence of Josephson coupling, then measuring supercurrent nonreciprocity under varying magnetic fields and angles, and finally contrasting these results with junctions deliberately degraded to isolate the source of any observed diode effect.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The microscopic origin of supercurrent nonreciprocity in van der Waals Josephson junctions remains under active debate, particularly regarding the role of twist-angle engineering in layered superconductors. Here, we investigate superconducting transport in twisted NbSe2/NbSe2 vertical Josephson junctions fabricated by dry transfer with controlled crystallographic alignment and chemically clean interfaces. High-resolution transmission electron microscopy is employed to directly determine the twist angle and assess interface quality. While the Josephson coupling strength exhibits a pronounced dependence on twist angle, with characteristic voltages maximized near crystallographically equivalent orientations and suppressed at intermediate angles, the supercurrent diode efficiency remains negligibly small and shows no systematic twist-angle dependence. In contrast, enhanced diode-like responses emerge only in weakly coupled junctions exhibiting interfacial disorder and reduced transparency. Deliberate interface degradation further amplifies the apparent nonreciprocity, yielding diode efficiencies approaching 30% together with an irregular magnetic-field-strength dependence. These results establish a clear decoupling between Josephson coupling and supercurrent nonreciprocity in twisted NbSe2/NbSe2 junctions. Our findings identify interface disorder, rather than twist-angle-controlled momentum matching, as the dominant origin of the observed diode response and provide a critical benchmark for interpreting nonreciprocal superconducting transport in van der Waals Josephson devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously compares two theoretical definitions of local spin magnetization in complex magnetic materials. It demonstrates that while both theories agree for simple collinear systems, non-collinear textures reveal a significant discrepancy due to an emergent 'local spin Berry curvature' term derived from field response. This suggests that precise measurement techniques must account for this novel contribution.

**Why it may be interesting.** The introduction of a Berry curvature term derived from thermodynamic response provides a powerful, unifying framework that extends concepts typically associated with orbital magnetism into the spin sector for itinerant magnets.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares two methods—conventional (equilibrium density) versus thermodynamic (field response)—for calculating local spin magnetization in itinerant non-collinear magnets.

**Main result.** For non-collinear systems, the thermodynamic approach predicts a novel contribution analogous to a local spin Berry curvature, leading to qualitatively different results (e.g., linear vs. plateau behavior) compared to conventional theory when probed locally.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving and comparing formulas for local spin magnetization using Green's functions and response theory, particularly focusing on the off-diagonal contributions arising from field perturbations.

**Model / system.** The study uses tight-binding models applied to non-collinear ferrimagnets on lattices like square or hexagonal lattices. The Hamiltonian incorporates hopping terms, onsite potentials, and exchange fields that induce spin textures like skyrmions.

**Key observables.** Local spin magnetization ($M_{	ext{spin}}(r)$), the difference between the two theoretical calculations ($\Delta M_{	ext{spin}}$), and the local density of states (LDOS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-collinearity angle ($\phi$), chemical potential ($\mu$), external magnetic field ($B$), and lattice geometry (square/hexagonal).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes zero temperature ($T 	o 0$) and examines specific limits, such as the antiferromagnetic limit or when onsite potentials are strongly alternating.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare off-diagonal local spin magnetization components for ferrimagnets on square/hexagonal lattices, explicitly contrasting the linear dependence predicted by the thermodynamic theory against the flat plateau of the conventional theory inside spectral gaps.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by defining both theoretical approaches; it then develops the mathematical framework using Green's functions and Hamiltonian models for various lattice types; finally, it presents detailed comparisons across different physical regimes (e.g., collinear vs. non-collinear) to highlight the quantitative discrepancies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventionally, the local spin magnetization in itinerant magnets is determined from the equilibrium local spin density. Here, we propose a thermodynamic approach in which the local spin magnetization is defined from the response of the system to an infinitesimal external magnetic field. The predictions of the two theories are identical for collinear magnets, but differ qualitatively and quantitatively for non-collinear magnets. In the present thermodynamic approach, the spin coherences determine an alternative distribution of local spin magnetization due to the field-induced deformation of the energy eigenstates. This effect is captured by a Berry-curvature-like contribution reminiscent of orbital magnetization and has several distinct observable consequences. We explore the differences between the conventional and thermodynamic approaches in several test cases.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces two-dimensional coherent spectroscopy (2DCS) as an advanced technique to overcome the ambiguity in separating homogeneous dephasing ($\gamma$) from inhomogeneous broadening ($\sigma$) in optical spectra. By analyzing orthogonal spectral slices, 2DCS provides independent constraints that significantly reduce parameter correlation compared to traditional one-dimensional fitting methods. This allows for a more accurate microscopic understanding of excitonic linewidths in semiconductor quantum dots.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful spectroscopic tool for characterizing open quantum systems by disentangling intrinsic decoherence mechanisms ($\gamma$) from static disorder effects ($\sigma$), which is crucial for understanding solid-state emitters and quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is separating the contributions of homogeneous dephasing ($\gamma$) from inhomogeneous broadening ($\sigma$) in measured spectral linewidths, as standard linear fits suffer from parameter degeneracy.

**Main result.** Two-dimensional coherent spectroscopy (2DCS) provides orthogonal constraints that significantly reduce the covariance between $\sigma$ and $\gamma$, leading to more reliable and distinct estimates for both parameters compared to one-dimensional photoluminescence (PL) analysis.

**Method.** The authors compare parameter estimation using standard 1D Voigt fitting against a joint fit of two orthogonal spectral slices—cross-diagonal ($S_{	ext{cross}}$) and diagonal ($S_{	ext{diag}}$)—obtained via 2DCS.

**Model / system.** The study investigates the exciton resonance in hBN-encapsulated $	ext{MoSe}_2$ at low temperature (8 K). The spectral lineshape is modeled as a convolution of Lorentzian (homogeneous) and Gaussian (inhomogeneous) components, forming a Voigt profile.

**Key observables.** Linewidth components ($\sigma$, $\gamma$), parameter covariance metrics (Pearson correlation coefficient $ho_{\sigma\gamma}$), and confidence region shapes derived from $\chi^2$ profiling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exciton resonance energy, temperature (8 K), homogeneous dephasing rate ($\gamma$), inhomogeneous broadening width ($\sigma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes symmetric Voigt lineshapes; the method relies on the assumption that physical parameters $(\sigma, \gamma)$ are shared across both 2DCS slices in the joint fit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the failure of 1D fits (showing highly elongated covariance contours) versus the success of 2DCS analysis, which yields more compact confidence regions and reduced parameter correlation. The comparison quantifies this improvement using covariance metrics.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical necessity of separating $\sigma$ and $\gamma$. It then details the methodology comparing 1D PL fitting to joint 2DCS fitting across orthogonal spectral slices, culminating in a quantitative comparison of parameter uncertainty reduction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Separating the contributions of homogeneous dephasing from inhomogeneous broadening in spectral linewidths is essential for connecting optical spectra to microscopic dissipation and disorder mechanisms. Voigt fits to one-dimensional spectra, such as photoluminescence, yield strongly correlated Gaussian and Lorentzian widths, so that neither width can be determined independently with confidence. We quantitatively show that two-dimensional coherent spectroscopy (2DCS) reduces this degeneracy by providing orthogonal spectral slices, diagonal and cross-diagonal, with complementary sensitivity to homogeneous and inhomogeneous broadening processes. As a demonstration, we measure the exciton resonance in hBN-encapsulated MoSe$_2$ at 8 K. A joint uncertainty-weighted fit maps the full $χ^2(σ,γ)$ landscape to quantify parameter covariance. Compared with linear Voigt analysis, 2DCS yields more compact confidence regions and markedly reduced parameter correlation, enabling reliable separation of the contributions to the excitonic linewidth.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on three novel chiral-structured superconductors ($	ext{RhGe}_4$, $	ext{IrGe}_4$, $	ext{IrSi}_4$), using advanced STEM to confirm their unique crystal structure. The research establishes these materials as a platform where structural chirality and strong SOC induce mixed-parity superconductivity, alongside predicted topological Weyl points.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter superconductivity, the explicit link between structural chirality, broken symmetries, and topological features (Weyl points) provides a rich platform for understanding emergent quantum states that could be relevant in engineered superconducting circuits or topological quantum computing architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to explore the complex interplay between structural chirality, strong spin-orbit coupling (SOC), mixed-parity superconductivity, and topological quantum phenomena in novel superconducting materials.

**Main result.** The authors successfully synthesized and characterized three chiral superconductors ($	ext{RhGe}_4$, $	ext{IrGe}_4$, and $	ext{IrSi}_4$), confirming their structure using STEM and demonstrating evidence for mixed-parity pairing and symmetry-protected Weyl points.

**Method.** The study combines high-pressure synthesis, advanced structural characterization (atomic-resolution STEM), detailed superconducting measurements ($ho(T)$, $M(T)$, specific heat), and first-principles electronic structure calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system consists of chiral-structured superconductors $	ext{TrX}_4$ ($	ext{RhGe}_4$, $	ext{IrGe}_4$, $	ext{IrSi}_4$), which possess broken mirror and inversion symmetries, leading to unconventional pairing mechanisms.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperatures ($T_c \approx 1.1-2.6 	ext{ K}$), magnetic penetration depth ($\lambda(T)$), specific heat jump ($\Delta C/T$), and the presence of Weyl points in electronic structure calculations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The enhancement of SOC is a key parameter driving mixed parity pairing; structural chirality ($P3_121$ space group) dictates the superconducting behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Traditional diffraction methods are insufficient for unambiguous chiral assignment, necessitating real-space STEM imaging. Superconductivity analysis relies on fitting specific heat and penetration depth data to various gap models (s-wave, p-wave, etc.).

**Figures summary.** Figures detail structural motifs, layered arrangements, temperature dependence of resistivity and magnetization curves, and analyses comparing superconducting gap fits from different symmetry models.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from material synthesis and definitive chiral structure confirmation (STEM) to detailed measurements of superconducting properties ($ho(T)$, $M(T)$), followed by theoretical analysis using specific heat and penetration depth data to constrain the pairing symmetry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chiral-structured superconductors, with simultaneous broken mirror and inversion symmetries, promote unconventional superconductivity through parity-mixing mechanisms. Yet a few bulk chiral-structured superconductors are known, partly due to the difficulty in directly determining their atomic-scale chirality. Here we report three chiral-structured superconductors, , RhGe4, IrGe4, and IrSi4, synthesized under high pressure, with Tc values of about 1.6 K, 1.1 K, and 2.5 K, respectively.Using atomic resolution Cs-corrected scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) combined with X-ray diffraction characterizations, we directly confirm their chiral structure (space group P3121). This real space imaging approach overcomes ambiguities in traditional diffraction based methods. These materials exhibit type-II superconductivity, and the enhancement of spin-orbit coupling (SOC) leads to the emergence of mixed parity pairing. Calculations also reveal symmetry protected Weyl points near the Fermi level, which is robust against the SOC. Our work not only expands the family of chiral-structured superconductors but also demonstrates the indispensable role of STEM in directly determining chiral crystal structures. These materials thus offer a clean platform to explore the interplay among structural chirality, SOC, mixed parity superconductivity, and topological quantum phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a robust machine-learning framework using potentials like GAP and MACE to simulate phonon-mediated heat transport in silicon. By applying this to nanocrystalline structures, the authors demonstrate that MLIPs accurately capture bulk vibrational properties while quantifying how grain boundary disorder limits thermal conductivity via NEMD simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter physics, the core methodology—developing and applying MLIPs for calculating complex many-body dynamics (phonons/heat flow)—is a prime example of advanced classical simulation techniques that bridge quantum mechanical accuracy with computational feasibility, relevant to numerical methods in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Understanding phonon-mediated heat transport in complex materials, particularly where grain boundaries limit thermal dissipation for nanoscale devices.

**Main result.** Machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) provide a more accurate and internally consistent description of bulk and interfacial phonon transport compared to classical potentials. These MLIPs successfully model the temperature dependence of thermal conductivity across various structural defects.

**Method.** The study develops and applies a comprehensive framework combining machine-learning potentials (GAP, MACE) with lattice dynamics calculations and Non-Equilibrium Molecular Dynamics (NEMD). This allows for the calculation of phonon properties and heat flow across interfaces.

**Model / system.** The system studied is silicon (Si), examined in both bulk form and nanocrystalline/bicrystal structures featuring controlled grain boundaries. The methodology relies on training MLIPs using DFT data to accurately capture interatomic forces, enabling simulations of vibrational dynamics.

**Key observables.** Phonon dispersions, phonon lifetimes, lattice thermal conductivity ($\kappa$), thermal boundary resistance (TBR), and temperature profiles across interfaces.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Grain boundary misorientation angles ($10^\circ$ to $40^\circ$), interface roughness amplitudes ($A=1 	ext{ Å}, 2 	ext{ Å}, 3 	ext{ Å}$), and simulation temperatures (up to $350 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The accuracy of the thermal transport modeling hinges on the MLIPs providing an internally consistent description of vibrational properties derived from both bulk and interfacial regions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare phonon DOS and dispersion relations between different potentials (MLIP vs. classical) against experiment, show $\kappa$ vs. T curves, and illustrate temperature profiles across interfaces with varying roughness amplitudes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from validating the MLIP framework in bulk silicon by comparing vibrational properties to established methods, then extends this validation to model structural disorder at grain boundaries using RDFs, culminating in NEMD simulations to quantify heat transfer limitations due to these interfaces.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding phonon-mediated heat transport in structurally complex materials remains a central challenge for next-generation electronic and nanomechanical devices, where grain boundaries and interfacial disorder strongly limit thermal dissipation. Although classical interatomic potentials enable large-scale simulations, their limited transferability can lead to inaccuracies in vibrational properties and interfacial phonon scattering. In this work, we develop a machine-learning based framework for modeling thermal transport in bulk and nanocrystalline silicon by combining Gaussian approximation potential (GAP) and multi-atomic cluster expansion (MACE) models with lattice-dynamical calculations and non-equilibrium molecular dynamics (NEMD). Harmonic and anharmonic force constants derived from machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) are used within a unified Phonopy/Phono3py workflow to compute phonon dispersions, lifetimes, and lattice thermal conductivity, providing an internally consistent description of vibrational properties. In nanocrystalline silicon, NEMD simulations directly quantify the thermal boundary resistance associated with grain boundaries and reveal its sensitivity to interfacial roughness and the underlying interatomic description. Compared with the Stillinger-Weber and Tersoff potentials, the MLIPs provide a more accurate and internally consistent description of bulk and interfacial phonon transport, enabling more predictive modeling of nanoscale thermal transport in low-dimensional materials.

</details>

### [Equivalence between the Axion Invariant and the $S_4$ Symmetry Indicator](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05719v1)

**Authors:** Mengyao Zhang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.05719v1>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a rigorous mathematical equivalence between the quantized magnetoelectric response (the axion invariant $2P_3$) and the symmetry indicator ($z_2$) for a specific class of topological insulators possessing $S_4$ symmetry. By connecting these two seemingly different theoretical frameworks, it provides a powerful new tool to classify and predict topological properties in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for theoretical physicists studying topological phases of matter, particularly those bridging continuum field theory descriptions (like axion electrodynamics) with discrete band structure calculations using crystalline symmetries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a mathematical equivalence between two distinct topological invariants characterizing axion insulators: the Chern-Simons derived magnetoelectric polarizability ($2P_3$) and a symmetry indicator ($z_2$). This bridges concepts from topological field theory (response) and band theory (symmetry classification).

**Main result.** The authors prove that for $S_4$-symmetric axion insulators with zero Chern numbers, the quantized magnetoelectric response $2P_3$ is exactly equal to the symmetry indicator $z_2$. This extends known equivalences to unitary, orientation-reversing symmetries.

**Method.** The analysis involves rewriting the Chern-Simons expression using an $S_4$ sewing matrix, reducing the problem to calculating the degree of a map from the Brillouin zone to SU(2), and comparing this result with the calculation of $z_2$ based on eigenvalues at high-symmetry momenta.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on three-dimensional axion insulators possessing $S_4$ symmetry. A specific tight-binding Hamiltonian is introduced for verification, allowing numerical confirmation of the theoretical equivalence.

**Key observables.** Axion invariant ($2P_3$), Symmetry Indicator ($z_2$), and the $S_4$ sewing matrix eigenvalues at high-symmetry points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry group $S_4$, vanishing three-dimensional Chern numbers, and the modulo two arithmetic used in degree calculations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on stable reduction to determinant-one two-band blocks and assumes that the system has vanishing three-dimensional Chern numbers.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates mapping degrees between circles; Figure 2 shows the band structure of the model; and Figure 3 displays the Density of States (DOS) for the model, with captions detailing specific matrix values at high-symmetry points.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the problem using the Chern-Simons form, then mathematically transforming this into a degree calculation involving the $S_4$ sewing matrix. It details the reduction to two-band blocks and finally proves the equivalence with the symmetry indicator via eigenvalue counting and numerical verification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The equivalence between the axion invariant and the $S_4$ symmetry indicator is established for three-dimensional $S_4$-symmetric axion insulators with vanishing three-dimensional Chern numbers. Starting from the Chern-Simons expression for the magnetoelectric polarizability, $2P_3=θ/π$ is rewritten in terms of the $S_4$ sewing matrix. After stable reduction to determinant-one two-band blocks, the invariant is expressed as the degree of a map from the Brillouin zone to $SU(2)$. The degree modulo two is then evaluated from the $S_4$ eigenvalues at the four $S_4$-invariant momenta and is shown to coincide with the symmetry indicator $z_2$. A minimal tight-binding model verifies the correspondence between $2P_3$ and $z_2$. The result closes a gap between the topological-field-theory description of the axion response and the topological-band-theory classification by symmetry indicators. It also extends the known response-indicator equivalence from antiunitary settings such as $C_nT$ symmetry to the unitary, orientation-reversing rotoinversion symmetry $S_4$.

</details>
